# SeaSick — Game Design Document

> Living document. Every AI task (Claude Code or Coplay) should reference this for design intent.
> Status: **v1 — core concept locked 2026-08-17**

## 1. High concept
A one-handed portrait mobile game. You lead an island tribe that must sail to distant islands for resources — but your people *hate* the sea. Sail smoothly to keep their stomachs settled, gather loot, and get back before seasickness turns the crew against you. Spend what you bring back on a better village and a better ship, then sail farther.

**Reference points:** Alto's Odyssey (one-thumb flow-state core), FTL (crew at stations), Cult of the Lamb (village + roster with traits), Sea of Thieves (manned-ship fantasy, scaled way down).

## 2. Pillars
1. **Sailing feels great.** One thumb, physical, wave-riding mastery. If sailing isn't fun, nothing else matters. Everything ships after this does.
2. **Sickness is the clock — and skill winds it.** Seasickness is the mission timer, but it accelerates with rough sailing (slamming waves, erratic turns) and slows with smooth sailing. Mastery visibly extends range before any upgrade does.
3. **The crew are people, not UI.** Sickness, panic, and mutiny are shown through whole-body acting on low-poly characters: green tint, staggering, abandoning a cannon mid-fight to puke over the railing. State is always readable from the gameplay camera.
4. **The camps are worth the voyage.** Whichever islands you settle (and the roster) are the progression spine — every trip makes the tribe visibly better off.

## 3. Core gameplay loop
**Prep** (pick crew, potions, cargo target) → **Sail out** (steer smooth lines through waves, manage sail trim and crew sickness) → **Gather** at the island (simple at MVP) → **Sail back heavier** (worse handling, sicker crew, push-your-luck) → **Spend** on village buildings, ship parts, potions → repeat, farther.

Failure is soft, and it is a loss of *capability* rather than an event: a crew that gets sick enough stops being able to work the ship. You lose time, cargo-carrying trips and the ability to defend yourself — never your save, and never control of the ship.

### Losing the crew (always visible, never a cliff)
The meter only ever goes up at sea. Past ~80% they start leaving their post to heave over the rail; the episodes get longer and closer together until the rail is all they do. Nothing is announced and nothing is seized — sail changes get slower, the oars weaken, guns fall silent one at a time as their gunner walks away. **The captain always keeps the tiller, and the sail stays wherever it was last set**, so a fully broken crew leaves a ship that still steers and still makes way. You lose your options, not your ship.

## 4. Platform, player & controls

**History.** 2026-09-24: tap-to-sail was tried on the phone and removed (*"the point and sail did not work"*). 2026-10-03: the floating heading / latched-throttle stick (and the wheel before it) was replaced with DREDGE's model, see `docs/PLAN-dredge-controls.md`.

- **Platform:** mobile, portrait, one-handed (two thumbs work). Sessions = one voyage (target 3–8 min).
- **Player role:** the captain — commands, never walks the deck. Crew executes.
- **Sailing controls = DREDGE (2026-10-03).** Nothing latches: the input *is* the boat.
  - **Phone, portrait, two zones.** The **lower 55 %** is the **boat stick** (`Ship/SeaStick.cs`, read by `Ship/HelmInput.cs`): a floating stick appears under the thumb and is **boat-relative**. Up / down = speed, in proportion to how far you push (down = astern, slower); left / right = turn the hull; a pure sideways push at rest pivots her. **Release = the engine stops** and she glides to a halt (no latched throttle, no heading hold, no tap-to-stop). A lift shorter than ~0.1 s does not drop the stick. The **upper 45 %** is the **camera stick** (`CameraRig/SeaCameraInput.cs`): a second floating stick, rate-capped, so the view turns while the thumb is pushed and **stays where it was left until a double-tap recenters**. Tilting the view down toward her **backs the camera off**, like DREDGE: up to 1.4x the distance at the steepest tilt, growing mostly in the last part (FEEL → `SeaCameraTuning.topDownZoom`, `topDownZoomCurve`; Kevin 2026-10-04). A double-tap on a ship locks it instead (it never also recenters). Each zone tracks its own finger: two thumbs work at once, one thumb is enough because the camera follows the hull by default.
  - **⚡ Boost** (Haste-style toggle button, bottom-right of the order strip): tap to arm, and a push ahead then reaches the burn tier (`ShipMotor.Burning`); it disarms on a second tap, on stopping, or after she has sat with no push ahead. **Free for now; fuel later** (the old wood-per-distance hook). The boost has punch: FOV kick + shake + haptic + surge on engage, all in FEEL → BoostTuning.
  - **Desktop:** W / S (or ↑ / ↓) speed, A / D (or ← / →) turn, each ramped so they read analog; **Shift** held = boost; **R** toggles rowing; **right-drag** anywhere off the HUD, or **left-drag in the top camera zone**, looks around (rate-capped like DREDGE's "max tilt"); **C** or **middle mouse** recenters; Space (anchor / lock), Q / E (broadsides) and G are unchanged.
  - **Camera:** third-person chase that yaws with the hull by default. The look offset persists until recentered. **No zoom at sea** (pinch / wheel removed, so a second finger never collides with two-thumb play; the island view keeps its pinch). Lock, kraken and the island overview keep priority over the sea pose. Brief automatic push-ins on notable events (imminent puke, mutiny stage change) still ride on top.
  - **Settings → CAMERA** (PlayerPrefs, not the save): *follow the boat* on / off (off = the world yaw stays fixed), look speed, invert X, invert Y.
  - Steering no longer depends on the camera: looking around never changes what the stick does. Every number (stick radius, dead zone, turn and throttle curves, reverse cap, lift grace, look rates, pitch limits, recenter time, boost punch) lives in FEEL: `SailControlTuning`, `SeaCameraTuning`, `BoostTuning`.
  - Speed emerges from sail trim and the state of the water (**not** from heading — see the 2026-08-19 wind removal).
  - Tap crew/station = small contextual order menu (trim, hold, potion, treat).
- **Input tech:** Unity Input System, touch; pointer simulation in editor.

## 5. World & setting
Archipelago explored outward in whichever direction the player chooses — there is no fixed island it radiates from; farther islands = richer resources = longer, harder voyages. **Distance is the difficulty curve**, and it is now *physical*: sea state is regional, so the water itself gets worse the further out you go — a calm shelf close in, lively water in the middle, mountainous seas at the edge.

**The premise:** the tribe has **never sailed**. Everything they know about the sea is folklore and rumour — waves the size of mountains, creatures nobody can picture, islands with stone people and mushroom trees that contradict their idea of what is real. The player goes and finds out which parts are true. Crew who come back are **heroes**, and the stories of what they survived are the reward the village actually cares about.

**Regions are a compass as well as a ring.** Distance sets *how dangerous*; direction sets *what kind*. North is cold, south is hot, east is dry, west is open ocean with scattered islands. The compass carries the cardinals for exactly this reason — "which way am I pointing" is the same question as "what am I sailing into".

**The sea is one system, not a set of zones (2026-08-28).** There are no borders in the water. Distance sets the **ceiling** — how bad it can get — and drifting storm cells decide where under that ceiling the sea actually is, so a squall can reach the home shelf (never past *rough*) and the deep west eases and gathers between about 45 and 65 m instead of sitting pinned at its maximum. Underneath that, roughness patches a few hundred metres across drift over the water and change its **texture** rather than its size: the chop swings by half across the visible sea while the long swell rolls straight through, which is what a real patch of rough water is. Every gradient is authored in **metres of wave height and interpolated geometrically**, because the eye reads height as a ratio — 3→6 m is the same step as 30→60 — so the crossing out west builds by about 20 % per hundred metres through lively, rough, heavy, wild and mountainous rather than skipping four of them in eight hundred metres. Nothing repeats: the wander, the sets, the cells and the patches all run on periods that do not divide into each other.

**Storm waves (rebuilt 2026-08-25).** Out west the sea itself becomes the mountains — **waves in the wave field, not obstacles placed in it**. The earlier design here described `SwellDirector`/`MountainSea`: ~100 m cliffs, 30 m thick, a separate moving obstacle you survived by meeting bow-on. That system died with the Gerstner stack and is **not coming back**; a wall taller than it is wide is something that kills you, not water you ride, and the premise asks for water you ride.

What replaces it is a genuine long swell: roughly **65 m of significant wave height on 470 m rollers**, so a face is a couple of hundred metres of climbable water — **about ten boat lengths** — rather than a cliff. The trade is deliberate: at that size a face steep enough to *read* as a mountain (15–20°) is close to the physical limit before water breaks, so the wavelength is chosen to buy the slope, not the height. Because they are real waves they behave like real waves — they refract, they carry the ship's own motion, and the sea lies down as the bottom comes up near land, which is what keeps them out west without a rule saying so. The one deliberate lie is the dispersion depth (30 m against a 180 m seabed), which slows the rollers to about 17 m/s so a 7.5 m/s boat can work a face instead of being overtaken at 32.

**How you survive one is still heading.** Bow-on you climb steepest and slam; quartering you traverse the face; along the crest the water is momentarily still relative to you and you can run the ridge. Beam-on in the trough is what rolls you.

**Land (rebuilt 2026-08-23).** The ocean is the default state of the world; islands are the exception. Terrain is a pure, seeded function of world position (`SeaSick.Terrain`): fBm → continentalness mask (sparse land, `landRatio`) → terrace curve (an inspector `AnimationCurve`: stepped = plateaus and cliffs, linear = hills) → detail → an altitude blend that keeps the 0–5 m band above the waterline smooth, so **every shore is a beach ramp and cliffs only start inland**. Seabed generates under the water, so there are shallows. Chunks (128 m, 65² verts) stream around the ship in Burst jobs with LOD rings; the world is unbounded by default (`worldRadius` clamps it). The starting island sits where `worldOffset` slides the nearest land under the spawn — it carries no special rules; the player can camp there or anywhere else. Waves **shoal and die at the shore** because the ocean reads a grid of terrain heights (`RegionField` shore factor). Gameplay islands (anchoring, crew, raiders) are discovered by flood-filling the height field around the ship's start and keep the old radial `Island` API, now measured off the terrain — "which side you approach from" is a slope question (`WorldSettings.beachMaxSlope`, 0.50: at 0.70 nearly every shore was landable). The minimap draws that same flood-fill mask rather than a circle per island, because a radius cannot describe a crescent.

**HUD rule: no full-width banners, ever.** A band of colour across the middle of the world is the most immersion-breaking thing a HUD can do. `UITheme.Banner` was deleted outright. Warnings live on the instruments the player is already reading, or on the world itself.

**Art direction:** graphic adventure (Option B): clean low-poly forms, cobalt water and turquoise shallows, warm timber and cream sails, grouped foliage and cool coloured shadows. Preserve the actual ship types and sail plans. Islands use broad rocky terraces, rounded broadleaf groves and fuller conifers. Trees and resource props are individually removable assets; island coves and wooded uplands retain connected usable ground. Warm and comedic. Body language over facial animation. Sickness reads at gameplay distance: skin tint shifts toward green, posture sags, walk becomes a stagger.

**Island art style — "carved" low poly (decided 2026-09-08).** Reference boards: a temperate crag (dark dense conifers, planar cliffs, beach ring, turquoise shelf, skerries) and a tropical atoll chain (sandbanks, dense palms, lagoons). Both say the same three things and the style is built from them:
- **Silhouette first.** Every triangle goes on the outline. A canopy is ONE jagged envelope (the spruce), one ray-sampled hull (the broadleaf), a fan of folded fronds (the palm) — never a stack of primitives.
- **Faceted form, soft colour.** Faces stay flat; colour is a per-corner vertex gradient — dark underneath and toward the trunk, lit on top, shade cooled toward blue. Vertex colour, which the terrain/scenery shader already reads, so it is free on mobile.
- **Rock is planes.** Convex shards with big flat faces, sunk into the ground. Boulder, ore host, skerry, sea stack and cliff are one shard at different sizes; an ore vein is a plane the shard is scored along so the stain has vertices to live on.
- **Mass over individuals.** Trees close at 4.5–5.5 m spacing; an island is judged as a wood, not as a tree.
- **Budget (portrait phone):** scenery tree ≈200 tris within 150 m, ≈115 beyond, a card past 600 m; boulders 30–40; harvest props ≤300. Two welded scenery meshes per island swapped by distance.
- **The water says the depth.** Foam line, turquoise shelf, teal, deep — the ocean's shoal tint is half of what makes the boards read as islands.
Generators: `tools/blender/seasick_style.py` (assets, contact sheet) and `tools/blender/seasick_island_v2.py` (the island structure as a visual spec: field → paint → scatter, one rule set at three sizes). Unity mapping: `RockBreak` = the proud-rock field, `VertexColour` = paint, `IslandScenery` = scatter.
- **In Unity (same day).** The kit ships as `Resources/Flora/seasick_flora.fbx` (readable, colours in the vertices, `FloraImport` sets it) and `SceneryKit` stamps it into `IslandScenery`'s welded meshes — now one mesh pair per 96 m CELL, swapped between ~200 and ~80 tris a tree by `SceneryLod` at 220 m and dropped past the streamed terrain. Cliff shards go on sheer faces (slope > 0.85, clustered by a slow noise, tilted against the face), not only where rock broke out; the terrain paint moved to the carved palette and writes "how much rock" into vertex ALPHA so the shader stops striating grass flanks it guessed were cliffs. **Kevin, on the first Unity shots: the Blender islands looked good partly because they are flat, sleek hills, not the steep domes the terrain was making** — so the relief came down (reliefHeight 60 → 42, massif 1–5.5 → 0.8–3.0, plainRelief 26 → 18, ridgeAmount 0.85 → 0.45, rockRelief 34 → 22), trees climb to 45° (vegSlope 0.6–1.0), stone paint starts at 44° (cliffRockStart 0.72), verdancyBias 0.7 so most islands are wooded. Measured with `CostProbe` against the morning baseline: triangles p50 462k vs 466k, SetPass 111 vs 119, GPU 4.39 vs 4.34 ms — the style costs what the cones cost.
- **Second pass on Kevin's Blender-sheet feedback ("the rocks and the small ridges… make the islands smaller").** Islands shrunk by raising the land threshold rather than the mask frequency, so home stays anchored (landRatio 0.15 → 0.10, skerries 0.3 → 0.4; the nearest-land home is now an 74 m islet). Proud rock became common instead of an event (rockBias 2.2 → 1.2, thresholds 0.80/0.44 → 0.72/0.40, crag scale 140 → 100 m, rockRelief 26) and the crest shards sit on it (proud > 0.8 m, 3–8 m slabs); sheer soil faces get only small slabs sunk deep, because big tilted ones floated where the ground fell away. Coasts stopped being walls: shoreSlopeMax 0.45 → 0.30, maskFalloff 0.10 → 0.16, skerryRelief 14 → 8. The kit's shards were re-exported lighter — Unity lights them again on top of the baked gradient, and at the contact-sheet values they stood two stops darker than the terrain's stone.
- **Colour direction D, "cream sandstone" (Kevin picked 2026-09-27).** One warm cream sandstone (sRGB ≈ HSV 39°, s 0.22, v 0.80; `SANDSTONE` in `TerrainVertexColor.shader`) for every stone: terrain rock paint, cliffs, Astra's `Cliff_*`/`Boulder_*`/shore stones (recoloured at load in `IslandNatureProfile.ColourD`, facet value kept). Rock paint only on facets steeper than ~32° — flat ground is grass. Grass is warm yellow-green in three tones (meadow / lush / dry, hue ≈ 66–97°), canopies brighter with a per-species hue. Shadows are warm, not blue: `_ShadowTint`, the shader's authored shadow bands, `AdventureLighting` ambient + `skyFillTint` (day only; night keeps its moon-blue). A mild grade (saturation +12, contrast +6, temperature +6) lives in the `Colour Grade D` global volume, which needs camera post-processing on.

- **Hand terrain edits — Kevin's home spit removed (2026-10-04).** Kevin's home island (seed 1337 world, centre (-732, 297)) had a ~250 m, mostly submerged sand spit (-0.5..+0.2 m) running east from its south-east corner and hooking north, which closed the water at his pier into a ~34 m canal his ship (16 m, 2.3-length turn radius) could not turn in. Kevin: "remove the spit on my island only." `TerrainEdits` (Terrain/TerrainEdits.cs) is a tiny fixed list of capsules (segment, radius, falloff, target) plus one optional rounded 'point' applied LAST in `TerrainHeight.Evaluate`, carried on `TerrainParams` so mesh, collider, shore grid, populator and horizon all see the same ground. An edit only ever LOWERS ground toward its target, never below it, and fades out above +0.3..0.5 m, so dry land is untouched by construction. The spit edit: the point plus three capsules along the crest to -3.5 m (3.2 m under the 0.3 m grounding draft; above the -4 m reef / -12 m anchorage thresholds so the seeded world build makes the same decisions). The dry beach of the arm stays (Kevin chose option C: keep the bay); it now ends at x ~ -592 as a rounded sandy point (an elliptical 'point' edit ahead of (-606, 223) whose slope matches the natural flanks), not the squared-off cut the first capsule left at the dry dock, whose footing (+2 m) is untouched. Keyed to the terrain seed + world offset: any other world gets no edits; every save in THIS world sees the spit gone, since terrain is per world, not per save. Gate: `TerrainEditSelfTest.Run(savePath)`. A player "dredge" tool is a later idea, not designed.

## 6. Key systems

### Sailing & ocean — MVP, build first
- Gerstner-wave ocean (custom, mobile-cheap). The same wave function drives water visuals, boat buoyancy/rocking, and the "roughness" input to sickness. One system, three jobs.
- Wind direction/strength; speed from sail trim vs wind angle.
- A **smoothness metric** computed from hull motion (impact accelerations, roll/pitch spikes) — this is the bridge between sailing skill and sickness rate, and the number we tune until sailing feels fair and fun.

### The bow harpoon — phase 1 "hook & reel" (approved 2026-10-04; plan `docs/PLAN-harpoon.md`; built, unplayed on the phone)
**The harpoon is the ship's hand**: a physical line from the bow, so while it is fast, steering becomes handling a load (pillar #1), and a yank or a snap shows on the smoothness meter (pillar #2).
- **Mount.** Every hull has a basic bow harpoon from the start: a fixed fitting on the stem, outside the slot grid (the modular hull's bow module at the art's own spot, the stock steamer's forward-most station with deck room). It follows the hull through every refit.
- **Targets (phase 1 = salvage).** Flotsam, wreckage clusters (the sail-over timber crates), lost-cargo crates, message bottles, the kraken's loot and castaways. Not your own swimmers (crew overboard) or fish shoals; not raiders (phase 3). **Delivery is the existing haul path** (`IOverboardTarget.OnHauled`), so every reward is exactly the sail-over one.
- **Arc and pick.** ±45° off the bow (90° total), 35 m. The nearest valid one is always the target. The world markers are **indicators only**: a ring over each target in the arc, and the picked one (the one the button fires at) is filled, larger and haloed. A marker takes no tap (Kevin, phone, 2026-10-04: tapping it "pressed on my ship").
- **Controls.** **Firing happens only from one fixed 🪝 button**, bottom right just above ⚡ (Kevin 2026-10-04: "fixed on the screen, not above whatever it's targeting"). It is a big thumb target (the icon at least 64 pt on the phone) with a fixed-width label beside it ("crate" over "22 m"; "no target", dimmed, with nothing in the arc), shown for as long as the gun is fitted, and it never moves or resizes: it becomes **✂ Cut** (with the tension bar), the reload ring and "hold full" in the same spot. A press on it reaches only the harpoon (never the stick, the camera, a lock, a rescue steer or the Ship sheet). Desktop **F**. No aiming: the barb leads itself, with a lead error that grows as the harpooner's accuracy falls. Skill is bringing the bow round, then handling the line.
- **Fire, bite or miss.** A short wind-up, then the barb flies on a slight arc. A bite inside 1.5 m hooks it; a miss splashes and the line reels back empty in ~2 s, with no reload.
- **The line.** The winch reels on its own, through a spring-damper on the line (never a step). A heavier load comes slower. The rope reads **slack** (sag), **taut** (straight, lighter) or **strained** (red-orange glow, flutter, creak). Tension comes from the load's drag and inertia against the ship's motion away from it: speeding or steering away raises it; easing off and pointing at the load lowers it. The winch stalls under strain and takes up again when you ease.
- **Snap, cut, reload.** Tension near full held ~1.2 s **snaps** the line; a second tap **cuts** it. Either way the load is let go where it floats and the gun **reloads for 5 s**. Ammo is unlimited (Kevin 2026-10-04).
- **The pull.** A load pulls back on her: modestly (a crate is felt, it never tows her), on both drives. The steamer takes it as a real force at the bow; the servo hull loses target speed and yaws a little toward the load.
- **The hold is finite.** Salvage comes aboard only if it fits under the same ceiling as other loot. Otherwise it is held alongside at the rail ("Hold full") until there is room, or until you cut it free. Castaways are crew, not cargo, and need no room.
- **Crew: the harpooner (2026-10-04).** While the gun has work (a target in the arc, or the line out) a named hand walks to the mount's stand and mans it: a **spare hand** first (no gun of his own, free right now, nearest the bow), else a **gunner from the disengaged side**, but only while the gunners left still cover every gun the fight needs; with nobody free, or a fight that needs every gunner, the **captain** fires it from the helm, slower (work rate 0.6). The gun waits up to ~2 s for him to walk to the bow. Work rate scales the wind-up and the reel (his own work rate); accuracy scales the lead error and falls with his seasickness (1 - 0.6 x sick, never below 0.3). Idle ~1 s, then he lingers ~3 s (a flicker of work does not march him back and forth) and walks back to the post he came from. Overboard, ashore, hauling, the jolly boat or the buckets take him off it at once (another hand or the captain takes over). Runtime only: after a load nobody stands there until the gun has work.
- **Gun lamp.** A hooded bullseye lantern bolted to the side of the gun, shining forward like a flashlight (a warm spot that follows the gun's aim, brighter at night), is the ship's bow light; the old stem and post lanterns are gone. The sea takes no spot lights (phone cost), so at night the beam is a stylised fake: a soft warm pool on the water ahead along the aim and a faint cone in the air from the lens, both fading in with dusk (`HarpoonBeam`, `HarpoonTuning.lampPool*`/`lampCone*`).
- **Knobs:** `HarpoonTuning` (FeelLab).

### Seasickness — MVP
- Per-crew-member meter, **0 → 100 and monotonic at sea**. Rate = base (time at sea) + roughness² (from smoothness metric) − modifiers (traits, cook, doctor, ship comfort upgrades).
- **Puking does not relieve it.** It is the tax: past the threshold they leave their post for the rail, and the frequency and length of episodes climb with the meter until they never come back from it. "A broken crew stops working" is therefore a duty cycle, not a rule at 100%.
- **Land is the only cure.** Shore leave drains the meter to zero at a rate, so a real harvest fully settles a crew and a touch-and-go costs you the time it takes. This makes sickness a per-*leg* clock and islands genuine sanctuaries.
- Visible stages on the body, not bars-first: green tint → queasy sway → walking off to the rail → slumped over it, grey and done.
- **Potions:** settle (reset a crew member's meter) and endure (slow accumulation for a stretch). Bought/brewed at home; limited cargo slots.

### Crew & stations — MVP-lite (one crew member), full later
- Stations: helm (player-directed), sails, lookout, cannons (later), below-deck (cook/doctor, later).
- Crew as data from day one: ScriptableObject per villager — name, role, traits, sickness stats — so the roster layer slots in without rework.
- **Roster (post-MVP):** named persistent villagers; recruit from islands; breeding with heritable traits (Cult of the Lamb-style); sea legs grow with voyages; death/retirement. Example traits: Iron Stomach, Weak-Kneed, Fast Trimmer.

### Islands — outposts you govern by hand (rewritten 2026-09-20)
_The "MVP simple" timer this section used to describe was overtaken on 2026-09-13. The working spec, the trap log and every measured number live in `docs/PLAN-island-outposts.md`; this is the shape of it._

**Islands make it, the sea risks it, the ship spends it, and the ship reaches further.**

1. **Anchor.** The camera lifts to a god's-eye view of the island and the player's **Hand** appears. On islands — and only on islands — the game plays like *Black & White 2*: grab the land and pull it, orbit about what is under the cursor, dive toward it, fling, fly to a spot.
2. **Govern by hand.** Pick a crewman off the deck and put him down: on a tree to cut timber, on a blueprint to build it, on a sawmill to be its sawyer, on the ship to come back aboard. Buildings are sited as ghosts. **The Hand gives orders and never produces** — a drop writes the camp's ledger row and that is all it does.
3. **Leave them working.** An outpost is a ledger that ticks on game time whether or not anybody is looking (**D2: watching must not beat leaving**); the people on screen are its puppets. A hand ashore is an empty berth at sea. A camp's stores had a ceiling that **only building raised**, bounding absentee income without a timer *(superseded 2026-10-03: island stores stack without limit — Kevin, "infinite stacking is allowed"; the ship's hold keeps its limits)*. *2026-09-27 (Kevin: "A villager walking with that resource has that resource on him and it gets where it gets when it gets there ... Only when a villager has delivered the object will the object be counted."):* the people are no longer puppets of a clock — **carrying is physical** (`docs/DELIVERY-ON-ARRIVAL.md`). Every trip is walked: the source gives the load up at the pickup, the destination counts it on arrival, and nothing is booked by a trip formula. While you watch, the bodies are the walkers; while you are away, the same villagers keep walking the same routes at the same 2.6 m/s as **invisible walkers**, and when you return each body stands where its walker got to, carrying what it carries. Watching still does not beat leaving: the rules and the walking speed are the same, only who moves the feet differs.
4. **Return and load.** A camp **never ships its goods back by itself**. The hold is the bottleneck, deck cargo is the greed, and the sea is where the value is at stake.
   **Cargo is carried, both ways (Kevin, phone playtest 2026-09-24):** *"when returning to the island I should have the option (in the store house, or camp fire) to unload things from my ship to the island and vice versa. These things should be physically carried from where they are to where they have been designated."* So the storehouse / campfire sheet orders a transfer (unload to the island, or load the ship), and idle villagers walk each armful between the pier head (gangway) and the store, visible in their arms, as timed trips on the same ledger arithmetic as every other haul — never an instant move. The hold line and the store ceiling still bound it; a transfer that cannot land waits and says why. The earlier one-unit-per-0.15 s loader on the ship sheet becomes a transfer order too.
5. **Spend it ashore.** *(2026-10-04: the yard and the home buildings are paid out of the **home camp's store** — home is a camp like any other; docking at the home pier leaves the hold aboard and the home camp's hands carry it ashore. The old separate home bank is retired; see the decisions log.)* **Ship-ladder rungs are priced in made goods** — timber first, then boards (a sawmill on a wooded island), tools (a smith on an ore island), spice (far out). A bigger ship reaches further rings, further rings hold new kinds, new kinds buy the next rungs. **Village growth** (storage, recruits, the settlement's appetite) is the second sink and comes after the ship.
6. **Upkeep.** Food is local (berries subsist, wheat feeds a settlement — an island tells you from the air how many it can hold), huts cap supported hands, the campfire's brightness is the provisions gauge, neglected hands get angry, and the camp's **influence** — how far from it the Hand can reach — grows with what is built. **[2026-10-02: assignments are permanent, Age-of-Empires style — mood and hunger never stop work and nobody pouts; see the 2026-10-02 decisions-log entry "Assignments are permanent".]**

   **Food (rework approved 2026-09-27, phases 1+2 built, unplayed).** Melvor-style Farming + Cooking on the existing station/order/walker systems. Every arrow is a villager carrying something; only stationary work has a timer.
   - **Day length (2026-09-29, Kevin: "the food production cant keep up with how fast the day cycles go"):** the sky day is **480 s** (`EconomyFeel.dayLengthSeconds`, live on FEEL; was 180 s). Eating (1 fill a hand a day), mood drift, the sun and "Day N" follow the sky day; every production timer (crops, benches, hammering, walking, gathering, hunting, recruiting, healing) stays in real seconds, because the ledger's own "day" is a fixed 180 s work day (`TimeOfDay.WorkDaySeconds`). Rates shown as "/day" are per sky day.
   - **Crops per plot** (`FarmPlot`, `Economy/FoodBook.cs`): 6 plots at Farm I, 9 at II, 12 at III; each plot has a crop, a real-time growth timer and a repeat flag. The farmhand plants (4 s) and harvests (4 s) at the farm and carries each harvest to storage; it counts on arrival. No seeds, no spoilage. Potato Farm I 5 min ×4 · Carrot Farm I 8 min ×5 · Onion Farm II 12 min ×5 · Wheat Farm II 20 min ×10 · Apple Farm III 60 min ×12. Farm II/III are upgrades at fire II until fire III exists. **Ripe plots (2026-10-04):** the plot tile and sheet show the units actually still standing (`FarmPlot.RipeUnits`), never the crop's full yield. Picking another crop on a ripe or part-ripe plot never converts the standing harvest: the pick is **queued** (`FarmPlot.nextCrop`, tile line "next: Potato"), the farmhand picks the ripe crop as it stands, and the queued crop is planted next (even with repeat off); picking the standing crop again cancels the queue, "Clear plot" on a ripe plot means harvest then leave it bare. A growing plot is still dug up at once (nothing standing to lose). The queue is saved (old saves read none).
   - **Other food in:** the fishing hut makes Fish; a hunt drops 3 Meat + 1 Hide; hands told to gather Food forage wild berries (`Res.Food`, raw). A landing party forages them too (2026-10-03): one Food a wild bush into the hold, the bush stripped and regrowing in 20 days like the camp's field (see the landing party, §6).
   - **Dishes** are Kitchen recipes (cook time in real seconds at the 180 s day): baked potato (1 potato, 15 s, fill 0.5), grilled fish (1 fish, 20 s, 0.6), roast carrots (2 carrot, 20 s, 1.0); Kitchen II + fire II: bread (2 flour → 3, 45 s, 0.75, mood +0.05/day), vegetable stew (2 potato + carrot + onion → 3, 60 s, 1.0, mood +0.1), ship's biscuit (`Res.Meals`, 2 flour → 4, 60 s, 0.5); Kitchen III: fish pie (2 fish, 2 potato, 1 flour → 4, 90 s, 1.0, work +10%), hunter's stew (meat, 2 potato, carrot, onion → 4, 90 s, 1.25, work +10%, mood +0.1). Flour: the **Mill** (fire II), 2 wheat → 1, 30 s. Harvest feast waits for fire III.
   - **Eating — supper at the fire (2026-10-02, Kevin: *"they all eat one time during the day, supper time together by the fire before they go to sleep"*).** No eating during the day and no meal trips. At the **supper bell, 21:00**, every hand on the island who is not down is served straight from the store and station racks (`OutpostLedger.Supper.cs`): round one gives everyone his best allowed dish (highest fill, then bonus; raw potato/carrot/fish/apple/berries at 0.25 fill, mood −0.1/day, only when no dish is left), further rounds go only to hands still under the ration's target (1.0, 0.5 on Half, none on None), at most 4 servings — so a shortage spreads evenly. A per-dish **save** toggle in the Larder keeps a dish off the menu. Fullness 0–1.5 drains 1.0 a sky day (half on Half) as a gauge; **mood follows the supper**, not the stomach: the share of a day he went short (ration cut included) costs −0.5/day × that share until the next supper, a full supper recovers +0.25/day — Half rations −0.25/day and None −0.5/day as before, and a fed camp is never docked for the empty hour before the bell. The best dish of his supper sets the mood/work bonus. Everyone at a whole day's fill = **fed together**: +0.05 mood (`EconomyTuning.fedTogetherMood`) and the fire sings. Bodies walk to the ring and eat a bowl or two there (status "Supper", then "Evening"); one who arrives after 23:00 skips the picture — the books already counted it. Catch-up rings one supper per sky day the same way.
   - **`Res.Food` is no longer the camp's food.** Gauges read `OutpostLedger.FoodFill()` (fill units in store; 1 = a hand-day) and `DaysOfFood`: campfire brightness, the fire sheet's food tile, recruiting (3 fill), auto-hunters.
   - **Larder** = the Stores sheet's Food tab: how long the food lasts, the rations switch, fill and eat/save per item.
   - **Standing orders (phase 2) — superseded 2026-09-30 by station spots (see Production chains):** a station holds a short queue worked top-down (1 + station level slots): make N, repeat, or **keep N in stock** — works while the camp holds fewer than N and every ingredient is to hand, else idles with "stocked 10/10" / "waiting for onion" and the next line runs.
   - **Old saves:** stored Food becomes ship's biscuit at 2 per Food (same days of food); station Food rows become potatoes/fish. New hands bring 3 days of biscuit ashore.
   - Tunables: `EconomyTuning` (Food header: crop/dish override rows, hungryBelow (sheets only now), fedTogetherMood, rawFill, plant/harvest seconds, plots per farm level). Offline catch-up (12 h cap) built 2026-09-27 (`Save/AwayProgress.cs`).
7. **Teeth, then the portfolio.** Raiders find rich unwatched camps; a lost camp must read as the player's mistake. The minimap becomes the dashboard of every ledger, and a save carries them.

8. **Walls and gates** (Phase 1 of `docs/PLAN-fortress-harbour.md`, 2026-09-23). A camp can be fortified. A **palisade** is drawn as a run of segments — plant a post, drag the next one, confirm, keep going (Kevin's own "connect the dots" design, D5) — snapped to a 2 m step, each segment its own site in the build queue at **1 log per 2 m** (D1) and capped at 12 m so a long wall is several things the hands can haul to at once. A **gate** is not sited: you tap a length of wall that already stands and its sheet offers "make this a gate", and the wall goes on standing until the gate is raised. Gates are automatic (D3) — open to your own people, shut to a raiding party, nothing to toggle — which is enforced in `CampPath`, where a segment's cells are blocked for everybody and a gate's only for raiders. **Raiders never climb** (D2): they break a segment or use an open gate, and a breach reopens the cells, draws the wall with its middle third gone and queues itself as a repair site the hands stock and build like anything else. **Rocks are obstacles now** (D4) and trees still are not — trees are felled where a thing is raised, as they always were. Stone walls, wall towers and the harbour are later tiers.

   **Wall towers, 2026-09-27.** Kevin: *"yes to the watchtower plan, go ahead."* A watchtower sited within 3 m of a wall post or wall line snaps onto that wall node (the ghost jumps there, goes the valid colour and says "on the wall") and becomes part of the wall: a standing run through the node is split there, the post at the node comes down and the runs either side stop 1 m short at the tower's legs, and the node blocks raiders and hands in `CampPath` like wall, while the tower stands, whatever the runs beside it are doing. The town reach (`Outpost.TownRadius`, 40 m) does not apply to a tower on the wall — walls can reach further than the camp. The lookout's door is on the camp side of the wall. Cost, the cannon and "raiders shoot towers first" are unchanged; a tower knocked down leaves a post, not a hole, because the runs still end at the node. Nothing new is saved: a wall tower is a watchtower some wall run ends at, derived on load. A queued (unbuilt) run is only snapped to at its ends, and a gate's posts never. A tower away from any wall works exactly as before.

   **Phone bug, same day: the exemption above was already right, and still could not be reached.** Kevin: "snapping watch tower to wall works, but only within the campfire's radius." `CanPlace`/`TooFarFromTown` had exempted a wall-snapped tower from the start; what had not moved was the CAMERA, left wherever the last thing sited had zoomed it to (35 m up), so a run built past the 40 m ring was simply off screen and out of a one-thumb drag's reach — not a siting rule, a viewport one. Fixed by widening `IslandCam`'s own pan reach to the camp's walls' extent for the life of a siting session (`Outpost.WallExtentFromCentre`, `IslandCam.ExtraReachCentre/Radius`), and by edge-panning while a drawing is being carried by one finger, the way holding a villager already did — so a wall tower is reachable along any side of the wall, not only valid there.

   **Ladders up the cliffs, 2026-09-27.** Kevin: *"is there any way to implement a ladder-platform-ladder-platform structure to ascend the mountains? it could be fun to use those in a build."* Since the slope cap, cliffs truly block every walker; a **ladder chain** is the player's way over one. Sited like a wall (fire sheet → fortify → ladder): tap the FOOT (walkable ground below), then the TOP (walkable ground above, within **8 m** flat and **3–24 m** higher); the chain is drawn live, green or red with the reason ("the top isn't walkable ground", "too far", "not a cliff — just walk", "the hands can't reach the foot"). The rise is split into flights of **≤ 3.5 m**, each a ladder leaning on the face, with a plank landing on posts between flights where the ground reaches that height, alternate flights stepping to either side — so it hugs the terrain. **Cost: 2 timber per metre of rise, at least 4 — PROVISIONAL, unplayed** (under the playtest cap like every price). It is an ordinary build site at the FOOT, stocked and hammered like a wall segment, so the builders must reach the bottom. A finished chain is a **link in the camp's map** priced at its climb time (**1.5 s per metre**), so a route takes it only when it is genuinely shorter, and ground on the plateau (stone, ore, trees) becomes reachable. **Hands and raiders climb; animals cannot** (they never path on the camp's map) — so a ladder round the end of a wall is a way in for a raid, and that is intended: the player defends it. Climbers face the ladder, go up rung by rung and pause on each landing. Chains are saved (`builtLadders`, additive; old saves load unchanged); tap one for its sheet (height, flights, climb time, Tear down — nothing refunded). Placeholder art, one mesh per chain; Astra redoes it. Check: `CliffLadderCheck.Run("", "/tmp/seasick-ladder.png")` in play mode.

   **The mine shaft, 2026-10-05 (branch `mine-shaft`, UNPLAYED, awaiting Kevin's visual OK).** Kevin: *"you can assign one villager to it he will walk in the mine shaft, and come back after x time and put stone or ore in the container outside. this structure can only be built on the side of a mountain / plateau on an island."* **Job:** one villager is assigned; they walk into the mouth (body hidden only while underground, status "In the mine"), come back after **~60 s** and put **4 stone** in the crate on the apron, booked only at that drop (delivery on arrival; the unwatched sim uses the same rule). Stone for now, ore later; it never runs dry. Trips are modelled on the fishing hut, and runners or the no-runner fallback collect from it as from the fishing-hut rack. **Siting:** only on a cliff face: the ground behind must rise steeper than the walker slope cap (33 degrees) over 2.5 m, with a 3 m standable apron in front that the camp's paths reach, else *"needs a cliff face to dig into"*. The ghost snaps to the nearest slope foot within 8 m and turns by itself to face downhill, like the dock (R-turn disabled); the back of the model is buried 0.35 m into the hillside so it reads as part of it. **Art:** Kevin's Meshy *Lantern Mine Entrance* (`Art/MineShaft/`, prefab `Resources/Buildings/MineShaft/MineShaftL1`, mesh untouched); the model's own crate has baked-in rocks, so the game's carry crate at 2.5x stands on the apron as the fill container (a plain stand-in). **Cost 8 timber, campfire level 1, copies 1/1/2/2 like the quarry (PROVISIONAL, unplayed).** Level 2 is available but only tinted like the other buildings, standard 1.5x rate (40 s trip); its cost (4 brick + 2 fine boards) is provisional, Kevin to set. Saved as an ordinary building row; it cannot be moved. Self-test `SeaSick.World.MineSelfTest.Run()`.

9. **Blueprints go down first, the ground clears itself after** (2026-09-23, `docs/PLAN-fortress-harbour.md` Phase 1 note). Kevin's rule, verbatim: *"any blueprint can be placed over any trees or small rocks etc and the area will be cleared by the villagers before buildings begin construction. obviously you cant place it on terrain thats unclearable."* Siting a building or a wall segment never refuses standing trees or resource rocks in its footprint; instead every queued site carries a CLEAR phase, worked off the same labour economy as construction, that fells the trees and breaks the rocks (booking their Timber/Stone like ordinary gathering) before a single hour of building labour accrues — hauling and stocking may run alongside it. What still refuses a spot is unclearable ground: beach, too steep, water, past the shore ring. Baked scenery boulders (as opposed to gatherable rocks) are not yet part of this and remain a known gap.

10. **The town centre goes anywhere; the crew walk to it** (2026-09-23). Kevin: *"i want you to be able to choose where you want on the island to build your town center. but the crew you drop off will have to walk from where you've docked."* The first thing sited on an island (the campfire) is the **town centre** and may go anywhere `Outpost.CanPlace` takes: no ring round the ship, no pre-made treeless clearing (the scenery no longer keeps its wood out of the survey's disc; the fire's plot is cleared by the hands like any other). ~~Everything sited after it must be within `Outpost.TownRadius` (40 m) of it, shown as a faint ring while siting and refused with "too far from the town centre (52 m, 40 m is the limit)".~~ **[superseded 2026-09-27, see below]** A hand left ashore starts at the foot of the gangway (or where he already stood ashore) and walks up on `CampPath`. An old save keeps its camp where it stands (its ledger has been keyed to the fire since 2026-09-20).

   **Slope cap for every walker, 2026-09-27.** Kevin: *"villagers and goats can just walk straight up the sides of the mountains."* Every walker now goes through one test (`World/Walkability.cs`): men, raiders and boar stand on nothing steeper than **33°** (no rise over 1.3 m between neighbouring 2 m cells, all eight neighbours), goats **45°**; each step is also checked against the ground 1 m ahead (backstop at 1.25x the cap), so the straight-line fallback, short hops and the last leg to a target on a bank can no longer climb a face. A hand refused by the slope works the target from within 4 m or, after 2 s, from where he stands — an errand never stalls. Stone/ore nodes and hunting quarry the fire cannot reach over the ground (`CampPath.Reachable`, a flood fill at grid build) are skipped for the next nearest. Check: `SlopeCheck.Run("Island_2", "/tmp/seasick-slope.png")` in play mode.

11. **Multiple buildings: copies by campfire level, each with its own level (2026-09-27).** Kevin: *"you can unlock the quantity of some buildings based on campfire. so like lvl 1 campfire is 2 houses, level 2 is 3 houses, etc. maybe second sawmill in lvl 3 etc. and they have their own levels, always. new houses cost a little bit more maybe."* (This replaces the Phase 1 "shelters, store huts and the storehouse may be raised any number of times" rule, and its `BuildPlan.allowMultiple` flag.)

   **Caps — PROVISIONAL, Kevin to tune** (`Economy.Techs.Caps`; built AND queued copies count; a wall tower is a watchtower):

   | Building | fire I | II | III | IV |
   |---|---|---|---|---|
   | shelter (Hut) | 2 | 3 | 4 | 5 |
   | store hut (Storage) | 1 | 2 | 3 | 3 |
   | watchtower | 2 | 4 | 6 | 8 |
   | farm | 1 | 2 | 2 | 3 |
   | sawmill, forge, kitchen, fletcher's, quarry | 1 | 1 | 2 | 2 |
   | campfire, pier, dry dock | 1 (one of each, unchanged) | | | |

   Fire III and IV do not exist yet; the table carries them so the second sawmill needs no data pass when they land (`RecipeGraph.Validate` checks the table: real plans, never shrinking, floor 1). At the cap the refusal names the unlock — *"a 3rd shelter needs campfire II"* — and the build list's row says "shelter ×2 of 2 — a 3rd needs campfire II"; below it the row prints "×n of m", the next copy's price and, on the last copy the fire allows, when the next one opens.

   **Price per copy — PROVISIONAL:** each copy past the first costs +25% of the base, per resource, rounded up (copy 2 = 125%, copy 3 = 150%), applied after the playtest cost cap (`BuildPlans.PriceForCopy`).

   **Own levels, always:** each building has its own level (`BuiltBuilding.level`, on its saved `raised` row). An upgrade raises only the building whose card is open; a station works at its own level's rate; beds and store room sum per building at each one's own level; only that building re-tints. A level-2 recipe needs THAT station at level 2. Demolishing one takes its level with it and no twin's shifts. **Old saves:** rows with no level read the plan's old shared level (and `Outpost.Adopt` writes it onto every row), so nothing loads downgraded; no save-version bump.

   **Several farms and towers:** Work hands on one plan are dealt round its copies (the stations' existing deal), so the nth farm or tower gets the nth hand. Raids: one tower or more halves the raid clock, a full lookout still stops it, each manned tower looses its own volley of arrows from the one quiver, and each tower's gun fires only while that tower is manned.

12. **The 40 m building radius is gone; the whole island is buildable** (2026-09-27). Kevin: *"I want to do away with the 40 m building radius for the campfire. There should be a reason for keeping it in the centre of camp, but the limitations are too much with the radius in place. The whole island should be built if you want it to."* `Outpost.TownRadius` no longer gates siting at all -- `Outpost.CanPlace` plus the one rule kept, **reachability**, decide a spot: nothing may be sited where the camp's own hands cannot walk to build and work it (`CampPath.Reachable`, the same walkability map the hands' own errands already use -- ladders count, a plateau joined by one is on the fire's ground). Walls, piers and a dry dock were already exempt from the old ring and are untouched. The camera's own pan reach while siting widens to the whole island for the life of the siting session (`IslandCam.ExtraReachCentre/Radius`, generalised from the 2026-09-27 watchtower-on-wall fix), so nothing sited far out is unreachable to the thumb either.

    **No walk label (2026-09-27).** The siting line that showed *"84 m from stores · ~70 s round trip"* was dropped the same day (Kevin: *"drop that information. I don't need to know to the second how long it takes."*). The only info line under a ghost now is a Hut's warmth ("warm — near the fire"). Distance still costs what it costs, because villagers walk it. `CampPath`'s ground map itself now sizes to the island's own measured radius rather than a disc round the fire (`CampPath.MaxCells` raised 160 → 320 to cover it; see the file for the cell-count/memory accounting), because `Reachable` has to have an opinion about any spot on the island now, not just the old ring's hundred-odd metres. No new gameplay rule beyond the label -- an aura or morale incentive for building close is Kevin's call, separately.

    **Warmth (2026-09-27, Kevin's "1" of "1 and 4," the incentive above named "separately").** A Hut standing within **30 m** of the fire (`OutpostLedger.WarmHutRadius`, straight-line -- the fire's own keyed position, `keyX`/`keyZ`) keeps its residents warm; a Hut past that line is a cold hut. **PROVISIONAL, unplayed:** a warm hand's mood climbs an extra **+0.1/day** (`WarmMoodBonusPerDay`), toward the same 1.0 ceiling ordinary mood recovery already climbs toward -- no second cap invented. Since `WorkFactor` already tops out at mood 0.5, this mostly speeds a hungry hand's recovery rather than helping a content one; the win is still real, just gated by however hungry the camp already is. **Assignment is deterministic, since no per-hand bed ever existed to assign** (`Housed` is only ever `hands.Count` against `HousingCapacity`): warm beds fill first, by a hand's own place in the roster (`OutpostLedger.IsHandWarm`) -- so with one warm Hut of two (houses = 2) and 8 hands, the first 2 are warm and the other 6 are in the cold hut, regardless of which Hut anyone would say they "live in." Upgraded Huts keep the bonus at any level; watchtowers and every other building are untouched. Visible on the Hut's siting ghost (*"warm — near the fire"* / *"cold — far from the fire"*, `CampSiting.WarmthLabel`) and on a hand's own sheet (*"content · warm (+mood)"* / *"content · cold hut"*, `HandSheet`'s mood row) -- the drawer's People row is unchanged. Check: `WarmthCheck.Run()` in edit mode, no scene needed.

    **Buildings never move (2026-09-27).** Kevin's hard rule: once a building, pier, wall, tower, ladder, road, farm plot or campfire is saved or built, it stays exactly where it was raised. Loading a save never spiral-searches a new spot for it and never refuses to stand it back up, even if the ground under it no longer validates (a terrain change, a new overlap) -- it stands there anyway and logs a warning. Only a NEW placement chosen by the player may be validated and refused. See `Outpost.Adopt`/`Outpost.Raise(plan, at, yaw, force: true)`.

Build order: **1** the god's-eye island (camera, Hand, living villagers) · **2** the sink (rung prices, camp → hold → home) · **3** upkeep and influence · **4** raiders and the watchtower · **5** portfolio and save.

### Death, rescue and repopulation (decided 2026-09-27; plan `docs/PLAN-DEATH-RESCUE.md`)
Deaths come only from things the player can see coming and answer: raids, going overboard (from collisions and enemy hits, not from ordinary sailing, since 2026-09-30), shipwreck and rare hunting accidents. **Starvation never kills**, and nobody is downed or dies while the player is away (downed timers tick in real time only while the camp is watched and the game is unpaused). **Always downed before dead:** a downed hand lies where they fell on a lenient timer (`LifeTuning.downedSeconds`, 180 s); a free hand drags them to a hut (phase 2), else they die. Every person carries a saved **life log** (global, keyed by name, so it follows them between ship and camps): recruited, came ashore, went aboard, went hungry, lived through a raid, hunting kills, and later overboard/rescues. A death writes a **grave** (name, camp, days, cause, spot) with a deterministic **3-sentence story** built from that log — how they lived, a second detail, how they died — then a forced tombstone placement (phase 3) and an optional graveyard list. A dead name is never reused by a recruit. Neglect makes a hand pout at the fire, never leave, and a camp never falls below a floor (phase 4). **[2026-10-02: assignments are permanent, Age-of-Empires style — mood and hunger never stop work and nobody pouts; see the 2026-10-02 decisions-log entry "Assignments are permanent".]** Repopulation: hut + food growth, recruits at sea (castaways, washed-ashore crew), ferrying hands between camps. Man overboard (grip meter, "Hold on!", swimmer on the real waves, tap to steer, Throw line) and village defence (spears, hiding in huts, raiders with health) follow in phases 5–12.

### The Kraken (deep-water monster) (approved 2026-10-03; steps 1-4 built, unplayed on the phone)
The first monster that fights back, and the answer to *"distance is the difficulty"*. Kevin picked severity and outcome himself: **three hits cripple, never sink** (failure stays soft, §3), and the player **drives it off, not kills it**.
- **Asset.** Kevin's own Meshy octopus (never decimated): **7 arms** (Meshy made seven; Kevin: keep seven), arms lengthened ×1.42 with his OK, rigged `Root`, `Body`, `Arm0..6` × 10 bones. Prefab scale **3.5×** the first preview (Kevin: *"it needs to be much bigger"*): ~65 m across, arms reaching ~40 m.
- **Where and when.** **Deep water only** — never the calm shelf near home; the chance rises with distance from home. At most **one per voyage**, with a cooldown between; storms and night raise the chance. **Transient:** never saved, cleared and rebuilt on load like raiders.
- **A warning, never an ambush.** ~10 s before it surfaces the water off one bow darkens and bubbles and a huge shadow passes under the hull; a red edge chevron points at it (`SeaEdgeMarkers`, new kind), a short toast (*"Something huge below…"*; no full-width banners), and a vibration. It surfaces off a **bow quarter, never dead ahead**, so there is always room to turn away. The breach throws a foam ring and a wave that rocks the ship (sickness rises through hull motion, as ever).
- **The swat.** Inside its ~40 m reach it attacks with **one arm at a time**. *Tell:* a foam ring forms on the water **where the ship is heading** and one arm rises and coils above it for ~2 s; then it slams onto the ring. Out of the ring = a miss and a big splash that rocks the ship; still in it = a hit. The tell is the whole skill: it is a steering test, not a stat check.
- **A hit.** A third of the hull (`HullIntegrity.Batter`; `KrakenTuning.hitsToCripple` = 3, so three hits take a fresh hull to 0 = crippled: ×0.60 top speed, full wallow -- the earlier ~15 % would have needed seven), a hard knockdown (`ShipMotor.Knockdown`; the sea rights her), a chance to throw a hand overboard (`HitOverboard`, into the existing rescue), a crew jolt. **Three hits cripple**: slow and wallowing, never sunk. Swats come every few seconds, and the longer the fight the more often **two arms** come at once. Every slam and splash feeds seasickness, so a long fight spends the voyage even if untouched.
- **Three answers.**
  - **Run.** Full ahead / burn takes you out of reach. It is slower than the ship, so escape is always possible; it sinks back once you are ~120 m clear.
  - **Fight.** Lock on with the existing lock button; the guns auto-fire. Head hits wear it down. **A swat cannot be cancelled** (Kevin, 2026-10-04): once an arm winds up the slam always comes, and the dodge (steer out of the foam ring) is the only answer. A ball that physically hits a raised arm costs it a little (`armHitDamage`, 0.35 of a body hit) and nothing else; the shots land where they land (normal ballistics, no lobbing, no padded hit capsule).
  - **Drive off.** After enough hits it sinks away wounded and leaves loot floating. It can return on a later voyage and stays a legend.
- **Loot.** A big haul of **kraken meat** for the island, plus one rare trophy, **kraken ink** (use decided later). The meat crates float at a big haul's size (~1.6 m across, `KrakenSpawnTuning.lootMeatSpan`), the ink smaller (~0.9 m, `lootInkSpan`), and the loot shows **no harpoon ring marker** (the rings covered the crates); the 🪝 button still picks it and names it, "loot" with its distance (Kevin 2026-10-04).
- **Phone.** No new buttons (stick, ⚡ boost, lock). The camera widens to frame ship and kraken (the camera stick still works, but the kraken framing takes over while it is up); a small shake on each slam. Every number (reach, windup, damage, health, spawn chance) lives in a **FeelLab** tuning class so Kevin tunes on the phone.
- **Build order.** **1** it surfaces (dev summon, breach, arm sway, foam; harmless) — *built 2026-10-03* · **2** the swat (ring tell, windup, slam, hit / knockdown / overboard, miss splash) — *built 2026-10-03, unplayed: ring 4.5 m, windup 2.6 s + 0.32 s slam, measured dodgeable from a stop (burn), half and full ahead (hard over) with the 9 m bootstrap ship and never by holding course (`KrakenDodgeProbe`); the auto-sink after `surfacedSeconds` is for dev summons only, a real encounter ends by escape or drive-off* · **3** fight back (guns, health, sink away, loot, escape) — *built 2026-10-03: the head is the lock target, 12 body hits drive it off (`KrakenTuning.hitPoints`; a raised-arm hit counts 0.35 and never cancels the swat, since 2026-10-04), loot floats where it sank; escape = 120 m clear for 4 s; a 300 s safety timeout also ends as an escape* · **4** in the wild (deep-water spawning, warnings, cooldown).

### Village / base — post-MVP (menu-first)
Buildings as upgrade cards before they're 3D: housing (crew capacity), blacksmith (ship parts), hospital (heal sickness aftereffects, unlock doctor), farmland (food → cook effectiveness), apothecary (potions). Village becomes a walkable/visible scene only after the loop is proven.

### Player roads (decided 2026-09-27, replaces the worn roads below)
Kevin: *"I don't like the road system. I'd rather place it myself, give the villagers a slight speed boost when using it, and have them use it as much as possible within reason -- if they'd get there faster using the road, they use the road. They can start/stop using the road wherever they want."*
- **Placing:** Build menu -> Shelter tab -> Road. Tap-to-tap like the palisade (tap the start, tap each bend, ✓ queues that stretch and carries on, ✕ stops); a point near an existing road end snaps onto it. Refused only for nonsense: sea, ground a hand cannot stand on (cliff, rock, standing wall; gates fine), through a building, more than ~154 m from the fire, or the same stretch twice. Crossing a road is fine.
- **Building:** one construction site per segment (max 16 m), priced 1 stone per 4 m (min 1; `EconomyTuning.roadMetresPerStone`, then FEEL's price multiplier), stone carried in and counted on arrival, then hammered like a palisade (3 s per unit). Tap a finished road -> Tear down (no refund).
- **Effect:** ×1.3 walking speed on a road cell (`EconomyTuning.roadSpeedMultiplier`). The camp's A* prices a step between road cells at 1/1.3, so hands take a road only when it is quicker and join/leave anywhere; the unwatched-camp walker meters its legs in the same road-discounted metres, so watched and unwatched agree.
- The worn roads were removed; old saves' wear rows are ignored and no roads appear on their own any more. The drawing below (one mesh, Astra's palette, torch posts) is kept and now draws the player's segments, joined into smooth chains.

### Worn roads (decided 2026-09-26, REMOVED 2026-09-27)
Kevin: roads form on their own where villagers walk most — *"just make sure that the roads don't overlap and glitch or look bad."* No gameplay effect yet (seam for a later speed bonus: `CampRoads.RoadAt`).
- **Wear:** every hand step adds its length to its 2 m cell (same world lattice as `CampPath` and walls); wear decays with game time (e-fold 6 days). A cell becomes road at 30 m of wear and stops being road below 14 m (hysteresis — no flicker). Only walkable ground (no sea, cliff, rock, wall; gates are fine), never under a building footprint. One-cell gaps bridged, holes in busy yards closed, one-cell spurs pruned, pieces under 4 cells dropped. Re-evaluated every 30 real seconds (6× per 180 s day); the mesh is rebuilt only when the picture changes. Saved in the outpost ledger (`roadCell`/`roadWear`).
- **Drawing (chosen):** ONE generated mesh per camp -- a 0.5 m lattice sheet draped on the height field, every vertex written once -- so nothing is ever stacked or double-blended. **v2 (2026-09-26, after Kevin's first look showed stair-stepped edges and a dirt plaza at the fire):** the road cells are thinned to centre-lines (Zhang-Suen, whiskers cut), cut into chains between junctions and ends, relaxed off the 2 m lattice, given a slow ~12 m wander and Chaikin-smoothed; each sheet vertex stores its distance to the nearest smoothed line and the shader turns that into coverage per pixel with a little edge noise, so edges are smooth curves at any zoom and every road is the kit's width (2.16 m incl. feather) however broad the trodden area was. Dead ends taper like Astra's `Road_End`; only a small irregular trodden yard (four overlapping blobs, ~2.8 m) is kept round the fire. Colours and edge feather follow Astra's roads-astra-lvl1-v1 kit (compacted crown -> darker shoulder). The rebuild is time-sliced (<= ~3-4 ms of main thread per frame, then one mesh upload). Shader `SeaSick/Worn Road` pulls vertices toward the camera along the view ray (depth only), so no z-fighting at 150 m and nothing visibly floats; lighting copies the island's terrain material.
- **v3 look (2026-09-27).** Kevin on the phone: *"what roads did you implement? they don't look like the asset Astra made. I also don't see the light posts."* v2 read as a narrow, uniform, dark-brown, half-transparent stripe. Same mesh and pipeline; the look is now Astra's kit: her exact vertex palette (worn crown #B09A76 -> earth #A38B65 at 0.55 m -> shoulder #968763 at 0.88 m -> 0.2 m feather to grass, linear between, as her 7-vertex ribbon interpolates; shoulder shaded down a little so the step survives the game's sun), +-9 % broad compacted patches, and her FACETED shoulders: a slow width wander (+-0.3 m, ~3 m bulges) and a per-vertex facet offset are baked into the vertex distance, so the 0.5 m lattice draws a polygonal outline instead of v2's smooth per-pixel noise (now off). Established roads are opaque (80-100 % by wear, was 50-93 %). Width stays her 2.16 m incl. feather.
- **Torch posts (2026-09-27).** Astra's `Torch_Post_A/B` (Resources/Roads) stand beside ESTABLISHED road only (cell wear >= 60, about a day past the "on" threshold) and go when it fades. Sparingly: one candidate per 2 m cell the road passes, highest world-cell hash first, at least 9 m apart (so ~9-16 m), posts within 4.5 m of a junction preferred, sides alternated along a chain, 0.5 m off the road edge; never on a road, in a building footprint (+0.6 m), within 1.4 m of a wall, off walkable ground (sea, cliff, rock) or within 3.5 m of the fire. Nothing saved: re-derived from the road chains on every rebuild, and hash-chosen so an unchanged stretch keeps its posts. At night every post shows a small low-poly flame at `Flame_Anchor` (one shared mesh + kit toon material); only the 4 posts nearest the camera (within 60 m) get a real point light (range 7 m), faded in/out over 0.6 s, fading out before a new one fades in -- iPhone budget (Mobile URP is Forward+).
- Rejected: Astra's modules snapped to the graph (a free-form wear graph needs crossroads, stubs and angles the kit lacks; junctions would stack translucent ribbons); a terrain splat mask (touches every terrain chunk and the terrain shader, one mask per island).

### Ship upgrades — post-MVP
Modular parts on the ship prefab (hull, sails, rudder, comfort fittings, cargo hold). Player-provided modular 3D assets (hull, mast/sail, cannons, rudder) are the basis of the ship.

### Production chains (the steps)
**Kevin's rule (2026-09-23).** *"There should be steps to things ... to hunt, you need a spear so the blacksmith needs a wood plank for the shaft and stone or metal for the tip ... the classic level up campfire to level up buildings ... this makes sure that you need to get a wide variety of resources to progress."*

**Three resource tiers.** Raw (timber, stone, food, game, hide, ore, spice) comes off the ground or off an animal with no recipe. Treated goods (boards, iron, brick, fine boards) are made from raw only. Items (meals, arrows, spear, tools, saw blade, iron spear) are made from raw and treated only — **no recipe takes an item**; items are used and worn out, never re-made.

**Fire levels.** The campfire rises in two levels (shipped 2026-09-23):
- **Level I (camp):** the base. Allows gathering, building, and cooking; nothing costs to raise it; starts the game.
- **Level II (hamlet):** costs **20 boards + 12 stone + 4 hide** (was 10 + 6 + 4 until the 2026-09-27 price pass), paid all at once at the fire. Opens the quarry and every building's second level; unlocks iron-smithing and fine boards.

Future levels (III, IV) stay unused and unpaid until they are designed.

**Tempo (2026-09-24, Kevin's phone numbers).** *"Plank making works"* but was far too slow. Picking a log up at the store takes ~1 s (was 3 s plus a wait for the next 18 s ledger step); 1 log → 3 planks; 15 s a plank (12 boards a hand-day, 45 s a log). The ledger now steps in 0.02-day quanta (3.6 s at a 180 s day) so a watched camp reads as real time; a hand under 90 % pace says why (*"working slowly — hungry (35% pace)"*). The other recipes' rates are unchanged and untested against this tempo: fine boards 90 s, meals 60 s, arrows 60 s each (3 min a job), spear 120 s, iron 120 s, saw blade 6 min, tools 3 min, iron spear 3 min, brick 90 s.

**Economy numbers — FIRST PASS, TUNE BY PLAY (2026-09-27).** Kevin: *"sure, go ahead and put in some real numbers"* — the playtest cap (every price held at 5) is gone. **One tuning file:** `Assets/_Project/Settings/Resources/EconomyTuning.asset` (`World/Economy/EconomyTuning.cs`, loaded by `Resources.Load` so the phone build carries it; the code holds the same numbers as defaults, so a missing asset changes nothing). It holds every plan's timber/stone/brick and **hammer seconds**, the fire's level-up price, upgrade prices and rate multipliers, the copy caps per fire level, the per-copy step (+25 %), recipe yields/rates/tool wear, cut seconds (wood 5 / stone 8 / ore 10 / spice 6.7), clearing seconds, rock yields (2/4/8), meat/hide per animal, spear life (4 / 12 animals), warmth radius/mood. **The main dials are on FEEL** (`EconomyFeel`, Settings → FEEL on the phone): cost ×, build-time ×, station-speed ×, gather-speed ×, meat per animal, hide per animal.

| Plan | Timber | Stone | Hammer (1 builder) | Why |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Campfire | 4 | — | 15 s | the door into the loop; a minute of one hand |
| Shelter | 12 | 2 | 30 s | cheap: more hands is the first want |
| Store hut | 16 | 2 | 40 s | the fire keeps 10 of a thing; fire II wants 20 boards, so a store is on the road |
| Farm plot | 12 | 2 | 30 s | food without walking |
| Fishing hut | 14 | 2 | 35 s | as before |
| Hunting lodge (plan id Fletcher) | 14 | 2 | 40 s | a bench under a roof |
| Kitchen | 16 | 4 | 45 s | |
| Watchtower | 14 | 6 | 45 s | a stone footing |
| Sawmill | 20 | 4 | 60 s | the first real goal: ~5 min after landing |
| Quarry | 20 | 8 | 60 s | a yard of rock (fire II) |
| Forge | 24 | 8 | 75 s | the priciest camp building; gates the spear |
| Pier / dry dock | 8+2 / 20+8 | | 60 s / 120 s | unchanged prices |
| Palisade / gate / ladder | 1 per 2 m / 4 / 2 per m (min 4) | — | 3 s per log | unchanged prices |
| **Fire II** | 20 boards + 12 stone + 4 hide | | instant | 7 logs through the saw, a quarter-hour of stone, 4 kills on one spear |
| Upgrades (level 2) | unchanged: 4–8 brick + 2–4 fine boards | | instant | reachable at fire II once an ore voyage feeds iron (blade, tools) |
| Store hut level 3 (2026-10-04) | 14 brick + 8 fine boards | | instant | fire II; the old Storehouse L3 bill — runner progression, provisional |

**The pacing arithmetic (today's rates, 180 s day, 2.6 m/s walk, sources ~25 m out):** a log is ~16 s of one hand's time (walk + 5 s cut, armful 2), a stone ~16 s (armful 3, 8 s each), a plank 15 s at the saw, a spear 120 s at the forge, a hunt trip ~1 min. **First sawmill** ≈ 24 logs + 4 stone + 75 s hammer ≈ 9 hand-minutes → **~3–5 min** for 2–4 hands with clearing. **Fire II** (store hut + forge + spear + 20 boards + 12 stone + 4 hide) ≈ 22 hand-minutes plus the serial sawyer → smith → hunter chain → **~20 min of focused work**; with food, clearing, walks and learning the island that is meant to land at **30–40 min** — *if it lands under 25, raise FEEL cost × to ~1.4 first*. **First level-2 building** needs the quarry, an ore island (iron → tools, saw blade), 6 brick at 90 s each and 4 fine boards → **~60 min**. **A raised ship section (~90 min)** is priced by the shipyard's own tables, untouched by this pass. **Every number is a guess until Kevin plays it.**

**Construction timer (Kevin, 2026-09-27: *"that timer is only active/relevant when resources are in place and someone is working on the building"*).** A site has three phases: clear the plot, stock it, hammer it. The **hammer timer runs only while every material is delivered AND the plot is clear AND at least one builder is at the site** (on the Build order, not walking a load); otherwise it is paused. One builder takes the plan's hammer seconds; **N builders go N^0.75 as fast** (2 → 1.7×, 3 → 2.3×, 4 → 2.8×: diminishing, they get in each other's way; `EconomyTuning.crewExponent`). The site sheet's line reads **"hammering · 42 s left"** only then; otherwise it says what it waits for — "clearing the plot", "waiting for 3 timber, 2 stone", "… · no builder", "stocked · waiting its turn" — and names **who is doing what** ("Gale is fetching timber · 2 on the way. Tam is building.", `OutpostLedger.WhoIsOn`). No walk-time estimate is shown anywhere. The fire's level-up and building upgrades stay instant.

**Hide comes off a kill and nowhere else (Kevin, 2026-09-27: *"fine that hide is the only way to reach campfire 2, as long as the hide can't be gathered"*).** `Res.IsGatherable` refuses any `Drop`-source resource, and `RecipeGraph.Validate` fails a build that lists a drop as gatherable or makes one from a recipe.

**Recipes at each fire level.** A station (sawmill, kitchen, fletcher, blacksmith, quarry, fishing hut) holds recipes that unlock by fire level and station level:

| Station | Takes | Makes | Yield | Rate/day | Tools | Fire | Level |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Sawmill | 1 timber | boards | 3 | 12 | — | I | 1 |
| Sawmill | 2 boards | fine boards | 1 | 2 | saw blade (0.05 wear/out) | II | 2 |
| Kitchen | 1 food | meals | 1 | 3 | — | I | 1 |
| Fletcher | 1 timber | arrows | 3 | 3 | — | I | 1 |
| Fletcher | 1 fine boards + 1 hide | bow | 1 | 1 | — | II | 1 |
| Blacksmith | 1 boards + 1 stone | spear | 1 | 1.5 | — | I | 1 |
| Blacksmith | 2 ore | iron | 1 | 1.5 | — | II | 1 |
| Blacksmith | 2 iron | saw blade | 1 | 0.5 | — | II | 1 |
| Blacksmith | 1 iron + 1 boards | tools | 1 | 1 | — | II | 1 |
| Blacksmith | 1 boards + 1 iron | iron spear | 1 | 1 | — | II | 2 |
| Quarry | 1 stone | brick | 1 | 2 | tools (0.1 wear/out) | II | 1 |
| Fishing hut | — (the sea) | food | 1 | 4 | — | I | 1 |

**Fishing hut (2026-09-27).** Kevin: *"fishing hut exists, import whatever assets Astra has made."* Astra's level-1 kit (canvas-roof shore station: net rack, prep bench, catch crate with 4 visual fish, `Worker_Stand`/`Catch_Anchor` markers) is in the game as `BuildPlans.FishingHut`, a station like the kitchen with one position, **fisher**. **All numbers PROVISIONAL, unplayed:** costs **14 timber + 2 stone** (under the playtest cap like every price), open at fire **I**, copies **1 / 2 / 2 / 3** by fire level (like the farm), level 2 for **4 brick + 3 fine boards** (rate ×1.5, like the fletcher). Its one recipe, **"fish"**, takes **nothing** and makes **4 food a hand-day** (two-thirds of a farmhand's 6: the farm costs a field, the hut only a shore); the output rack holds 8 and is hauled to storage like any station's. Food stays a Raw, gathered good: `RecipeGraph.Validate` now allows a raw good from exactly one recipe shape, a **catch** (no inputs, no tool) — gathering at a station, not making — and still refuses a made good from nothing. **Siting: it must stand at the shore.** Every corner on dry ground at least **0.4 m** above mean water (it may stand on the upper beach, where no other building may go), and water at least **0.5 m** deep within **7 m** of its footprint's edge, in any direction (the siting ghost's facing is fixed from the fire, so the rule does not ask the player to turn its back to the sea; its authored front, the dry-land approach, is +Z). Refusal: "a fishing hut must stand at the shore -- no water 0.5 m deep within 7 m of it". The fisher stoops over the prep bench (the Bend mime) and the fish on the board shows while he works. Not yet: boats, catches tied to fish stocks or weather, spoilage. Check: `FishingHutCheck.Run("Island_2")` in play mode.

**The fisher fishes at the water (2026-09-30).** Kevin: *"I want the fisherman to walk to the closest water when he fishes. After catching a fish he places it in the product part of his building. He only walks it to the storage himself if the product box is full."* The fisher no longer works the prep bench: each catch is a walked trip from the hut's **shore spot** (the nearest dry ground at the waterline that a hand can walk to from the hut, walls and gates included; found once per hut and re-checked only when the walls change) — he fishes there for the catch's own timer (the same 4 a hand-day), carries the fish back and it counts when it lands in the hut's **output box** (8, its capacity; the 4 visual fish show it scaled). When the box is full (or there is no order, or no way to the water — the sheet says "walled off from the water — needs a gate") he carries **the whole box** to storage in one trip (*"he will carry all the fish at once since they aren't that heavy"*: the fish armful is at least the box) and goes back to fishing; idle hands empty the box the same way. Fish in the box is camp food: it counts in the food total and a hungry hand walks to the box for a meal when the store has none (any station's output rack holding food, e.g. a kitchen's dishes, works the same). This replaces "all gathered resources go straight to storage" for the fishing hut only.

**Building upgrades (level 2).** Every camp building can rise to level 2 at fire level II, each costing a mix of brick and fine boards — sawmill and blacksmith want 6 brick + 4 fine boards (the most expensive), others 4–8 brick + 2–4 fine boards. Raising a building to level 2 multiplies its work rate by 1.5× (a store hut's level 2 gives it 4 runners instead of 2; island stores have no ceiling since 2026-10-03, see "Island stores: infinite stacking"). Until a real level-2 model exists, a levelled-up building shows it by turning gold — `BuildingLevelLook` (2026-09-26, Kevin) — and swaps to real art the day a kit has one.

**Hunting hard gate.** To hunt, a hand must carry a spear (stone or iron). A stone spear wears 0.25 per animal (lasts 4 kills); an iron spear wears 1/12 per animal (lasts 12 kills). Each animal hunted drops 1 hide as pay-in — so to reach the blacksmith, a hunter must first have a spear, and that spear's tip must come from stone (to start) or iron (later). Hide is the first treated-goods gate. *2026-09-26 (Kevin: "in storage the animal will be x amount of meat and x amount of furs"):* an animal is one carcass = 4 Food + 1 Hide, and the hunt stops only when the store has room for **neither** (whichever half does not fit is lost), so a full larder no longer silently blocks hides. The hunter stalks his claimed beast while the ledger works through it, closes in and spears it as the books finish it, the beast falls, and he carries the carcass on his shoulders to the store; a spearless or store-full hunter stays home and his row says why. *2026-09-27 (Kevin: "we need fur/hide as a resource … the fur number doesn't seem to be accurate"):* a hunt is now a **trip** like every other gather order — walk out + stalk (the whole trip keeps the old 0.5 animals/day, ×1.5 when it goes out with an arrow), **one whole kill** (herd −1, spear worn once, arrow loosed), carry the carcass home, and **deposit 4 Food + 1 Hide as whole units at the store** the moment it lands (what does not fit is lost). No fraction of hide or meat ever comes from hunting, the Hide count rises by exactly 1 per carcass delivered and at no other time, and the trip is saved on the hunter so a watched and an unwatched camp land on the same books. The hunter's body follows the trip: strikes in the last seconds before the books' kill, shoulders the beast, and sets it down in the step the store takes it. *2026-09-27, later (Kevin: "it should be them walking up to an animal, jabbing it with its spear, picking it up and walking back. There should be no arbitrary timer."):* **no stalk timer and no kill rate.** The hunter claims a beast, walks up to it, jabs (a ~1.5 s pose), and the animal dies at the jab (herd −1, spear worn, arrow loosed); he shoulders the carcass, walks home, and 4 Food + 1 Hide count on arrival. The only limits are a beast to claim, a spear, and room for meat or hide.

**Bows (2026-09-30, Kevin's decision; built, unplayed).** The fletcher makes **bows** from **1 fine board + 1 hide → 1 bow**, 180 s a bow (1 a hand-day, the pace of the iron spear and tools), at **fire II** (fine boards are fire II) and fletcher level I; arrows stay its default recipe. **Arrows are the ammunition:** every shot spends one whole arrow off the pile (store first, then the fletcher's rack), and a bow with no arrows cannot shoot — the hand's row and the raid line say so (*"bow has no arrows"*, *"defending, bow — no arrows"*, *"no arrows"*). A bow wears like a spear: it lasts **12** kills (`EconomyTuning.bowAnimals`). Three uses:
- **Hunting.** A bow + arrows satisfies the hunting gate as well as a spear (`HunterBlocker` → *"needs a spear or a bow"*), and a hunter takes the bow first when it can shoot. He shoots from **2×** a spear's arm's length (`bowHuntReachScale`); each kill spends **one arrow** and one kill of bow wear instead of spear wear.
