  - Not persisted, on purpose: crew sickness (cosmetic), steamer toggle, HUD prefs, felled-tree lists (a count reproduces them).
- 2026-09-21 — **The ladder costs something, and a camp's pile can be sailed home** (Phase 2 of the island loop, plus what Kevin's second play found). All uncommitted; detail in `PLAN-island-outposts.md`.
  - **Rungs are priced in camp-made goods** (`Ship/ShipPrices`): 1–6 timber, 7–11 boards (+stone), 12–16 boards + tools, 17–19 tools + spice; fits priced on their own table. The bands are the loop's own chain — rung 7 cannot be paid for until a sawmill is standing and manned — and **every number is a first guess, none played**, to be tuned from the `SinkProbe` pacing table so a rung is one or two voyages rather than ten.
  - **Where the money is charged is a design rule, not an implementation detail.** `Shipyard.Move` and `Shipyard.Upgrade` bill the `VoyageManager` purse, physical gate first and then the bill (a refusal is a sentence, and "needs 12 boards" is never said about a hull that would hog). `Apply`, `Undo` and the panel's dev buttons stay free because they are the path every probe puts her on a rung by, and there is deliberately no "free for probes" switch. The bay `+`/`−` board bills nothing: it is what you do with the hull you paid for. Both purchases refuse anywhere but home — *"she has to be at her own pier"* — because the stores are ashore, however full the hold is.
  - **The player loads; a camp never ships home by itself** (`World/CampLoading`). The loop's fourth step had no button anywhere in the game. It is a Load / Stop button on the camp bar and a row per kind in the open sheet, best-first (Tools, Spice, Boards, Ore, Stone, Meals, Food, Timber), one unit at a time, nothing on the tick — the plan's defence against risk #2, "the sailing becomes transport". The marked hold line is a decision the loader respects, not a wall.
  - **Builders spend the pile before the wood** (`OutpostLedger.Step`, haul then cut, 12 a day from the pile to 4 felled), and **standing timber is the island's, not the clearing's** (`Outpost.WorkedHectares`, 0.6 of the disc). Kevin: *"they gathered logs for it but it never built"* — ten logs sat by the fire while the sawmill starved on a small island. A blueprint that can no longer finish now says NO TIMBER LEFT rather than looking merely slow.
  - **Felling waits for the man; it does not let him choose.** Trees were falling at random while villagers chopped elsewhere. Workers now claim the NEXT trees in the ledger's nearest-first order and the front tree drops only when its claimant is swinging at it (10 s grace, whole debt flushed if the backlog runs past cutters + 2). The order stays the ledger's so the felled set is still reproducible from an integer after twenty unwatched days (D2 stands).
  - **A throw is a picture, never an order.** Kevin: *"i want villagers / items to retain some momentum if i drop them mid grab."* The Hand throws with its own velocity above 1.5 m/s and places below it; the order is still decided at the release point so preview == commit, landing writes nothing, and nobody can come down in the sea.
- 2026-09-20 — **The island loop is named, and islands play like Black & White 2.**
  - Kevin asked for a loop that fits the game and for islands — *only* islands — to take their *"game feel, camera, and movement"* from B&W2. Three calls, all his: **the Hand gives orders and never produces** (the full god hand that carries logs was on the table and was refused, because it makes being present pay better than sailing and D2 is the reason the ledger exists); **rungs are priced in camp-made goods, and home growth follows** ("both, ship first"); **villagers visibly do their work**, as pure animation of the ledger.
  - **What the survey found, which is why the sink is Phase 2 and not a nicety:** the 20-rung ladder is *free*. `Shipyard`/`ShipyardPanel` never read a `Stockpile`. Camps make eight kinds of goods and the game buys camp buildings, one storehouse and hull repair with them. The two halves of the game were not connected.
  - **§6 Islands rewritten** from the three-line "MVP simple" timer to the loop as it is actually being built. The seasickness prose in §6 is still stale against `PLAN-weight-water-days.md` (sickness is characterisation, not a clock) — noted, not fixed here.
  - **Phase 1 architecture:** no new rig. `ChaseCamera` stays the only writer of the camera transform and `IslandCam` stays the only thing that transforms the shot; `IslandShot` gained `direct` (a 1:1 grab cannot pass through a 2.2/s low-pass) and `clearance` (the near-ground clamp reads the height field, not the 46-sector profile). `CameraRig/IslandInput` is the **one** device reader for the island view and every gesture lands on a public method the probes also call.
  - **Input convention, stated because CLAUDE.md says otherwise:** no script in this project uses the `.inputactions` asset or a generated wrapper; all of them read `Keyboard/Mouse/Pointer.current`. The island input follows the code, reads `Touchscreen` raw for two-finger gestures, and the migration is nobody's job yet.
  - **The reach is the ship's, for a measured reason:** `TerrainStreamer.target` is the ship, so full-detail ground exists only ~256 m from her. The view is clamped to the island ∩ a disc round the ship — on a 2 km island you govern the side you anchored off. That disc is the placeholder for Phase 3's influence ring.

- 2026-09-11 — **Computer mode: landscape is the shape the game runs in, and the phone is still supported.**
  - Kevin: *"please change the game to computer mode, right now its in portrait mode."* Asked how far it went, he chose **landscape first-class with the portrait path kept alive** — not a pivot. Both aspects are now first-class, which means a framing or UI decision has to work in BOTH; CLAUDE.md says so and names the two commands that check it.
  - **Player settings:** default orientation LandscapeLeft, autorotate-to-portrait off, standalone default 1920x1080, resizable window (so the HUD's re-layout is exercisable by dragging).
  - **`ChaseCamera.narrowestAspect` 0.4615 → 16:9, and it stopped being scene state.** It was a `[SerializeField]`, and Unity had written 0.4615 into `Sea.unity` when the component was added — so switching the game to landscape by editing the default in code would have changed **nothing, silently**. That is the same serialization trap that made `_StormDeep` read the old colour for a session. The first fix pushed a new value through `SerializedObject`; the better one was noticing this is not per-scene tuning at all. There is one aspect the game is composed for, so it is a `const` and the stale scene line is ignored. **A value that cannot drift needs no script to un-drift it.** The behaviour change: the overview used to compose for the narrower of (actual, authored) and therefore always for portrait, backing off far more than a desk window needs; it now frames for the aspect it is actually running at, clamped at 16:9 so an ultrawide does not over-tighten, and a phone still backs off far enough to hold her.
  - **The HUD has no "mode".** `HudLayout` reads `Screen.safeArea` and asks the window what shape it is, so it re-lays out on resize. One number moves with the shape: **`RailTop01` is 0.30 upright and 0.14 wide**, because the scarce axis swaps. Upright, height is abundant and the tabs belong where a thumb reaches. On a desk, height is what runs out — at 1920x1080 a rail starting at 0.30 leaves the settings drawer 412 px of panel and the foam tuner alone wants about 560, so a tuner opened on a computer would have been cut off. At 0.14 it gets 585, and nothing reaches for a tab with a thumb on a screen driven by a mouse.
  - **`tools/compilecheck.sh` was checking half the project.** It excluded `*/Editor/*` — which is where every probe launcher, setup script and shot tool lives, 109 files, including all four written for this change. It now builds `Assembly-CSharp-Editor` too, against the runtime assembly it just built rather than a possibly-stale one. Verified it can actually FAIL by breaking a file on purpose and watching it report, then restoring: a check that only ever passes is worse than no check. Same family as the `git bundle verify` that certified a backup missing every piece of art.
  - **`GameViewSize` is now shared** by `DesktopGameView` (1920x1080) and `PortraitGameView` (1080x2340), and `HudOverlapProbe` prints which shape it ran at and names the one still to check. Setting `cam.aspect` is not a substitute for either: the IMGUI HUD lays out from `Screen.width`/`Screen.height`, which is the Game VIEW.
  - **Measured at BOTH aspects, closed and with a tuner open. Zero overlaps in all four.**
    - Desk, 1920x1080: rail 151..191 / 201..241 (the 0.14), Map 1666..1906 y 14..254, Wind 264..296, Ship 306..378, Crew 14..100, Compass 700..1220, helm 906..942 and 952..1066, nav 1030..1066, prompt 765..1165 y 832..886.
    - Desk with the foam tuner open: drawer 14..614 y **251..886 (635 px)**, tool body 553 px — it fits, where the old 0.30 rail would have cut it to about 330. That is the whole reason the number moved.
    - Phone, 1080x2340: unchanged from the layout pass, and with the drawer open the prompt moves to 652..1052, clear of the drawer's 14..614.
  - **One probe artifact worth knowing:** changing the Game view's aspect DURING play produces stale reports that can reach the 300-frame "permanent" line — switching desk to phone with the drawer open reported the prompt across the drawer by 269 px while the census on the same run showed them clear. Several hundred frames pass while the panels re-settle, and the probe counts frames rather than truth. **Restart play after changing shape**; from a clean start at either aspect the same configuration reports nothing.

- 2026-09-11 — **One HUD layout, one prompt slot, and every tuner behind a settings tab.**
  - Kevin: *"right now its a mess with overlapping ui, button placement doesent make sense and it often covers important parts of the screen. hide the foam editor and all other adjustable options inside a settings menu tab... i need the screen cleaned up and looking better before we proceed"* with in-game options.
  - **Fourteen `OnGUI` methods each read `Screen.width`/`Screen.height` and picked their own corner, and several picked the same one.** Measured at the shipping portrait aspect (1080x2340, `UITheme.Unit` 20): `SeaFoamTuner` took a fixed 440x560 box out of the top right and sat on the minimap, the wind row and the ship panel; `WaterClarityTuner` (430x470), `IslandTuner` and `DevHUD` all sat on the crew pips and on each other; `GunneryReadout` painted a slab across 62 % of the screen at 0.34 height, over the open Yard panel; `AnchorController`'s "come alongside" button overlapped `CombatLock`'s "space · lock on" **by 34 px**, and whichever drew second in the mouse pass took the tap — on a control that puts the ship somewhere. `SalvageSpawner` wrote a full-screen-width toast at 0.62 height, 0.02 from where `Bilge` put its jettison button. The deck-cargo toggle covered the top **0.9 units of "cast off"**.
  - **`HudLayout` owns the screen now.** One enum, read top to bottom, is the whole HUD: a panel calls `Place(slot, w, h)` and gets a rect whose y comes from the heights of the slots above it *as those panels actually reported them*. Adding a panel means adding a slot. `StatusHUD.RightColumnBottom` and `HomeTab`'s copied "0.30 of screen height, 2.0 units tall" comment are gone — a constant that is copied is a constant that drifts, and this project has paid for that with `Dock.ViewHalfWidth`, `DivergenceProbe` and `OceanShadingCheck`. Zones are inset into `Screen.safeArea`, so nothing draws under a notch.
  - **`Prompts` is the one contextual slot, bottom centre, and priority replaces position.** Anchor > combat > jettison > toast; the highest bidder draws and the rest stay silent. That is a design statement as much as a layout one — at any moment there is exactly one thing the game is asking you to do. The winner is decided from the previous frame's bids, because Unity does not order `OnGUI` between components and "highest so far this frame" would hand the slot to whoever ran first.
  - **The settings drawer swallowed every loose overlay.** One `⚙` tab at the top of the left rail, above Yard and Home. Two sections: HUD (the first real in-game options — minimap, compass, crew, perf readout, saved in `PlayerPrefs`) and TUNING, which is `#if UNITY_EDITOR || DEVELOPMENT_BUILD` and lists the six tools that register through `IDevTool`: Dev HUD, Gunnery, Foam & crests, Dock camera, Sail camera, Water clarity. One open at a time, drawn inside the drawer's own rect. A tuner no longer chooses where it draws, and `F1`/`G` open the drawer onto their tool rather than switching on an overlay with nowhere to go. It **installs itself** at runtime, so the three lab scenes get it too.
  - **Verified by `HudOverlapProbe` at 1080x2340, not by re-reading the arithmetic.** It reads the rects the frame drew — `UIBlocker.Claimed`, named for free by `[CallerFilePath]`, and `HudLayout.Issued` — and intersects them. Final measured state, drawer open: rail 702..742 / 752..792 with the panel below all of it at 802..2146; top-right Map 14..254, Wind 264..296, Ship **306..378**; Crew 14..100 and Compass 280..800 clear of both columns; helm 2166..2202 and 2212..2326; prompt stack 2046..2082 and 2092..2146. **Zero persistent overlaps.**
  - **The probe found three faults I would otherwise have shipped, and its own first version hid one of them.** It exempted every case of one rect containing another as "a button inside its panel" — and the real bug was the ship panel drawn INSIDE the minimap. *A check whose exemption covers the failure mode is a check that stops before the broken thing.* Then: reserving a slot only on Repaint made the layout depend on frame rate (panels now reserve on every event and draw only on Repaint — reserving is two float compares, it is the text meshes that cost); expiring a slot on wall-clock broke it again the moment the editor stalled (count frames, not seconds); and the open drawer crossed the centred prompt by 114 px **permanently**, which is why the probe reports at 1, 30 and 300 frames — "it overlapped" and "it overlaps" are different bugs. Centred things now centre in the space beside an open left panel, and the prompt moved to x 572..972.
  - **Not yet judged by eye.** The geometry is measured and clean; whether the screen READS better is Kevin's call, and the numbers above are all reproducible from `PortraitGameView` + the console.

- 2026-09-10 — **A Home tab: she can always get back to her berth.** [superseded 2026-09-22: no home island — the "Home" tab is slated to become "your camps"]
  - Kevin: *"i want a button for respawning at home."* Asked what it should cost and he chose a **free tow** — instant, cargo comes home with her, the voyage closes exactly as if she had sailed back. So this is a convenience, not a mechanic, and nothing about the voyage loop's balance moves. **If it ever needs a price, the price belongs in `VoyageManager`, not in the button.**
  - **`AnchorController.BerthAtHome(out why)` is the ONE place that knows how to be berthed.** The spawn path already had the routine inline; the tab would have been a second copy, which is exactly the divergence `GetUnderway` had to be written to end — two copies of a let-go/tie-up routine already cost this project a week of broken landings. The spawn path calls it now too.
  - Three things the extraction had to add that the spawn path never needed. It **lets go first** (`GetUnderway`), because arriving somewhere new with `CurrentDock` still pointing at the last place is that same fault reversed. It takes its Y **off the ocean**, not off the hull — the spawn runs on frame one when she is at sea level, but a respawn from mid-voyage would set her down at whatever height the trough she was sitting in happened to be. And it **rings down stop through the telegraph**, since `HelmInput` re-asserts `ThrottleOrder` every Update and writing the value would last exactly one frame (`HelmInput.AllStop`).
  - **It refuses while the crew are ashore**, and says so on itself. The plank is the only way they get back aboard, so moving the hull would leave them on a beach a voyage from home.
  - **The tab arms on the first press and acts on the second**, lapsing after 3.5 s. It sits under the Yard tab on the left edge, in the same idiom — one tap that teleports a loaded ship off a wave face is the kind of mis-tap that ends a session, and this lives a thumb's width from a control in constant use. Hidden entirely while she is at her own pier: a control with nothing to do should not be on screen.
  - **Gated end to end by `HomeTabProbe`, not at the transform.** Putting the hull on the berth is the half a check is tempted to stop at — the `LandProbe` lesson. Pressed from 1009 m out at 8.1 m/s with six timber aboard: **0.17 m from the berth, 0.09 m/s, 0.0° off the pier heading, `AtHomeDock` true, the voyage closed on its own, and all six timber landed on the beach.** The first version of that probe gated on the hold still being FULL afterwards and duly failed a button that was working perfectly — arriving at the berth completes the voyage, and completing a voyage is what unloads her. Measure where the cargo ended up, not where it was.

- 2026-09-09 — **The sea was rendering the phone's ocean, and the foam was a coat of paint. Both fixed; crests now break.**
  - Kevin: *"right now it all feels very soft when i'm sailing… i'd like the foam that appears when waves snap and fold. some sharp tips to the ocean."* Three separate faults sat under that one sentence, and the first two are bugs rather than taste.
  - **The editor was running `OceanQuality_Mobile` while every quality dropdown said PC.** The tier was picked as `GetQualityLevel() == 0 ? Mobile : PC`, and quality level 0 in this project is **named "PC"** — so the whole ocean rendered at `displacementFadeDistance` **350 m** with 5 clipmap rings and a 128 FFT instead of 2600 m / 8 rings / 256. Measured with `CrestProbe`: mean displacement fade across a deck-level view **0.00**, not one pixel above 0.1. From about 350 m out the sea was a **flat plane with foam painted on it**, which is the whole of "soft". A quality level's INDEX is not a stable key — the list can be reordered and Unity keeps a separate per-platform default — so `OceanQuality` now picks by NAME, and `ReadQuality` asks it instead of carrying its own copy of the rule. (It had one, and it printed the wrong conclusion directly beneath the level name that contradicted it.)
  - **The instant fold-foam term produced nothing at all: 0.09 % of the open sea.** Two causes, both structural. Its Jacobian was missing the cross term — `J = (1+Dxx)(1+Dzz) − Dxz²`, and dropping `Dxz²` can only make J *larger*, so every fold was under-reported and the SHEARED folds, which is exactly where the two crossing swell trains make pyramidal peaks, were missed outright. And it weighted the Jacobian by the **clipmap** weights, so the band that actually folds (cascade 2, the 32 m chop) was weighted to nothing past a few tens of metres: the water folded and the shader was not looking. `FoamAccumulate.compute` never had either problem, which is why the persistent buffer was carrying **100 %** of the white on screen.
  - **That buffer was a wash, not a pattern.** Injection ran `saturate((threshold − J) * 2)`, a ramp half a Jacobian unit wide, against thresholds of 0.30–0.45 — so barely-folding water injected nearly as hard as water turned inside out. Measured in a 14 m sea: buffer **p50 0.32, p95 0.36**, i.e. a constant field, and 100 % of water pixels above 0.1 foam. A breaking crest had nothing to stand out against. Walking the threshold showed where it stops being flat: the useful band is **0.02–0.10**, not 0.45. New `foamInjectSharpness` replaces the hard-coded 2.
  - **Measured after, deck-level, one wave phase, the ocean layer only.** Hs 14: foam mean 0.50 → **0.035**, 6.6 % of the sea carrying whitecaps instead of 100 %. Hs 55: **41 %** above 0.1 and 27 % bright — a storm sea a quarter white, with the fold now the larger source rather than the buffer.
  - **Sharp tips came free, and only because of where they live.** Choppiness was one scalar for all three cascades, which made crest sharpness and sampler cost the same knob — lambda is the horizontal displacement the CPU twin inverts by Newton, and that budget is spent (7 iterations against 0.4 ms). Split per cascade, **cascade 2 is not in the physics readback at all** (`PhysicsCascades = 2`), so it can be sharpened for nothing. `crestSharpen.z` 1.35 takes the folding fraction of the near-field chop from **24 % to 32 %** with no change to what the hull feels. x and y remain locked; moving them costs an iteration there is no room for.
  - **New: spume at breaking crests**, in `StormSpray`, gated on the ocean's own foam field so it can never disagree with where the water is drawn breaking. A breaker is a LINE, so a hit throws a row of particles along the crest rather than a puff at the sample — which is what makes 24 samples a frame enough. Unlike spindrift it is **not** behind the storminess gate: a crest folding over is water, not weather, and gating it on the storm is why the seas the game is actually sailed in carried no white water. 7 crests/s at both Hs 14 and Hs 55.
  - **The boat's own spray stopped coming through her deck.** `SpeedJuice` placed every emitter at a coordinate typed for the deleted paddle steamer — bow at z 9.2, beam at x ±3.2 — and nothing resized them when the ladder began swapping hulls, the same fault `HullWaterClip` had. On the skiff the bow emitter was **4.7 m ahead of her stem**; on the ship of the line z 9.2 is amidships, x 3.2 is well inboard of a 6.5 m half-beam and y 0.8 is six metres below her rail, so the burst was born inside the hull and came up through the deck. Sized from the rung now, in `Shipyard.Refit`, alongside the clip. The second half was aim: the beam bursts were `Euler(-52, ±90, 0)` in SHIP space, which is "up and outboard" only while she is upright — at 30° of heel that cone points across her own deck, and in a storm she is rarely anywhere else. They are aimed about the world vertical now. `SprayRigCheck` walks four rungs and gates on both.
  - **Left as Kevin's:** how much foam, how sharp the crests. `SeaFoamTuner` (right-hand panel, keys 7/8/9/O/Home) drives fold, trail, spume and sharpness live with the numbers on screen, and prints a paste-able block. Straight application of the hand-him-the-controls rule.
  - **Traps banked.** A `ScriptableObject` under Resources IS the asset, a runtime write to it survives leaving play mode, and `ImportAsset(ForceUpdate)` does **not** throw that away — so a tuner or probe that writes sea states at runtime leaves four flattened anchors loaded, and the next `SaveAssets` anywhere writes the flattening to disk. It did; the four sea states came back out of git. `SetupSeaFoam` now WRITES every value rather than read-modify-writing, and both the tuner and the probe were rewritten to leave the anchors alone. Related: an always-on dev overlay must be **inert until touched**, or it pins whatever it seeded from and the weather silently stops moving.

- 2026-09-09 — **The islands become woods. Density was the whole gap to the Blender boards.**
  - Kevin, handing the Blender sheet back: the rock ridges are what give an island character, get Unity as close to this as possible. Measured against the render that produced that sheet (`isl_crag_air.png`, the Crag: R 85 m, relief 30, rock 0.90, verdancy 0.90, trees on a 4.8 m grid ≈ **230 a hectare**), the Unity crag came back at **22 trees a hectare** — a savanna where the reference is a wood. Nothing else in the style could read while that was true: the outcrops Kevin likes are rock breaking out of a closed canopy, and there was no canopy.
  - **Three separate faults, all of them arithmetic, none of them art.**
    - `verdancyRockSuppress` 0.65 made the *rockiest* islands the *barest* — exactly inverting the reference, which is rock 0.90 AND verdancy 0.90 in the same island. Cut to 0.15; rock still clears trees where it is genuinely proud, through `vegRockSuppress` 0.88, which is the right place for it. With `verdancyBias` 0.7 → 0.45 and `verdancyFloor` 0.10 → 0.25 the world's spread moved from **median 0.37, max 0.75** (no island as green as the board) to **0.51–0.87**.
    - The scenery budget was a **count**, so density fell with area: 22 trees a hectare on the r386 crag against 129 on an r48 islet. Density is what the eye reads; the budget is now a guard, and verdancy is counted once instead of three times.
    - The guard also **ended the scan**, so hitting it left a bare band with a straight edge down the far side. Two attempts to fix that by picking a better constant for the expected acceptance both failed, because acceptance is a property of the ISLAND (measured 0.09 to 0.29 across one world), not of the world. `IslandScenery` now **samples its own accept test** — 600 points, about 1 % of the walk — and sizes the thinning from that. One `ChanceAt` function serves both passes, so the estimate cannot describe a different island than the one being built.
  - **The beach was the second half of the picture.** The foreshore compresses heights near the waterline by `shoreSlopeMin`, which was 0.07, and sand was painted to 3.2 m — so on ground that falls at 1:10 the sand ran **~450 m inland** and half of every island rendered as beach. The reference paints sand below 0.8 m and is grass by 2.0. Now `sandHeight` 1.2 and `shoreSlopeMin` 0.18: the beach is a ring.
  - **Measured after:** most islands 130–310 trees a hectare, in the reference's band; the two giants (r380, r340) are cap-limited at 115 and 153. `CostProbe` against the same-day baseline: triangles p50 **465 k → 644 k** (+38 %), p95 596 k → 676 k, GPU frame **4.34 → 4.71 ms** (+8.5 %), SetPass 119 → 111. Four times the wood for a third more triangles, because LOD1 and the cell cull carry it.
  - **Still open: island SIZE.** The reference island is 85 m across; Unity's run 36–380 m, and the two over 300 m are the only ones that cannot reach reference density inside the triangle guard. Shrinking them is the one change that would buy the density for free, and it is a gameplay call (settlement flats, harvest yield, harbours) rather than an art one.

- 2026-09-08 — **A small hull cannot charge a big sea — and stops being swallowed by one.**
  - Kevin: *"from the second-largest ship downwards the ship clips into the waves… going under water and then emerging doesn't fit the gameplay."* Measured on the Long boat at severity 0.60 head seas: inside a wave **13.9 % of the run**, rails **4.65 freeboards** under at the worst.
  - Root cause was the absolute-metres trap on the wave thresholds — the burial clamp only engaged at 1.2 m of green water over the rail, which is a wetting for the three-decker and a metre past fully swallowed for the skiff — **plus one that is not in metres: the head-sea penalty keys on SEVERITY, a weather coordinate that is the same number for a 9 m skiff and a 46 m first-rate.** Whether a head sea is trouble is a ratio of sea to hull.
  - Three changes: `BuoyantBody.ConfigureWaveResponse` scales the clamp to **half of HER freeboard** (cap 1.0 m) and the plow floor/ceiling to her own speed and sail authority; `ShipMotor` adds a second resistance rule — **full head-sea penalty from a sea a third of her length**, taking the worse of the two rules — so the Long boat that held 7.9 m/s into that sea now makes 5.6 and takes the faces slowly enough to ride them. Worst burial nearly halved, 4.65 → 2.68 freeboards; the brig and up are unchanged within the instrument's noise. **This is the §6 skill trade stated at fleet scale: the penalty for a steep sea is SPEED, never a submarine.**
  - Two roads measured and rejected, both in code comments: scaling reserve buoyancy (its matching damping glued her to the falling back of every wave — swallowed time tripled) and strengthening bow lift (reproduced the stern-under failure number for number). And a clamp at a third of freeboard parked her INSIDE the crests at 1.8 m/s, where the waves just washed over her — swallowed time went UP.
  - Instrument: `SwallowProbe` (segmented, phase-re-pinned, heading-set warps; reports in freeboard units with the servo/plow/lift terms printed). BuryProbe's severity-1.0 storm gate measured 4.7 % vs 10.6 % on two identical control legs — chaotic, and no longer an A/B for this hull class.

- 2026-08-30 — **The wood is cuttable. All of it.**
  - Kevin: *"most of the trees are cosmetic and dont even get cut down."* Measured, he was being generous — **1346 scenery trees against 26 harvestable props on one island, 1.9 %**.
  - **The mesh stays welded; it gets an INDEX instead.** The scenery is hundreds of trees in one mesh precisely so it costs one draw call, and that is worth keeping — six hundred GameObjects each carrying a trunk and two canopies is eighteen hundred renderers an island. `IslandScenery` now records every tree's base and the exact range of vertices it owns *as it writes them*. Felling collapses that range onto the tree's own base, so every triangle it owns degenerates and the index buffer is never touched. Reconstructing the index afterwards by trunk colour is possible — the probes do it — but that is a guess about a run of bytes, where this is the builder saying what it built.
  - **Nodes are materialised on demand around the shore party**, not up front: 1300 trees an island across fifteen islands is twenty thousand GameObjects for a crew of five who can reach a few dozen. `SceneryWood.Populate` stands up the nearest unfelled trees within 130 m, capped, and tops up every 3 s as they work inland.
  - **The economy is deliberately untouched.** How much timber an island yields is `Island.Remaining`, set from the prop count and decremented by the crew. This changes *which* trees they walk to and whether the one they cut falls over — nothing else. Raising the yield to match the visible wood would be changing the economy to fix a visual problem, which is the mistake `IslandScenery` was written to avoid in the first place.
  - **Measured on a real landing, off the baked mesh:** 580 scenery trees, **57 standing as harvestable at once** against 26 prop trees for the whole island before, **5 felled** — counted as trunk runs whose vertices have all collapsed to one point, which is the shipped artefact rather than the bookkeeping that felled them. 3 logs aboard in 30 s, island stock 26 → 21.
  - Known cost: felling re-uploads the whole scenery mesh (`SetVertices`), up to ~90 k vertices. Felling is a few per second at most so it has not been a problem, but it is the obvious thing to batch if it ever is.

- 2026-08-30 — **The hull collides with the coastline now, not with a radius.**
  - Kevin: *"there doesent seem to be a clear hitbox."* Correct, and it was one line deep: `HullIntegrity` grounded against `Island.RadiusToward` — a 46-sector radial profile that was the **only** hull collision boundary in the game. It cannot describe a bay or a lobe. Measured against the real waterline on 360 bearings: out by a mean of 1–23 m and a worst of **934 m out to sea** on the biggest island, with stretches of real land **690 m inside it** that you could sail straight through. Invisible walls and phantom water, from the same number.
  - **Land is the height field.** It is what the coastline is drawn from and it agrees with the rendered mesh to 7 mm, so it is asked directly. The push-off direction is the field's own gradient — downhill is deeper water — which needs no centre and therefore does not care what shape the island is. Reefs and enemy hulls keep the radial test, because they really are circles.
  - **Skipped while anchored.** Deliberately putting her ashore is not running aground, and the shore party moors her 11 m off a beach in inches of water.
  - **The draft had to become a waterline, and the measurement is why.** At a realistic 1.0 m the contour she is stopped at sits a mean of **55 m** off the home island's visible shore and 798 m at worst — these islands have broad shallow aprons, so that is honest water and a dishonest game: the landing prompt only reaches 30 m past the shoreline, so she would have been walled out of her own beaches on most bearings. **The same symptom as the bug being fixed, arrived at from the opposite side.** At 0.3 m the wall hugs the visible coast: median island **4.9 m** mean gap, home 5.2 m mean / 20 m worst. Both original defects are gone by construction — nothing stops her in deep water, and nothing lets her through land.
  - **`groundingDraft` is a `const`, not a `[SerializeField]`, and that is the story.** As a serialized field it never took the value written in the code: the scene file has no entry for it, yet the component reported the old 1.0 through recompiles *and* through fresh play sessions. It produced two byte-identical measurement runs that I nearly believed. Same trap `SetupStormSky.ResetToCodeDefaults` exists for. Nothing needs to tune it per scene, so it is not tunable per scene.
  - Regression-tested end to end with grounding live: the shore party still lands (plank out, five ashore, trees falling) and the full voyage still closes (cast off → load → alongside → 30 banked, 8 spoiled → storehouse raised, capacity 30 → 70).

- 2026-08-30 — **Erosion measured and affordable; rock is a fact now, not a guess.**
  - **`TerrainPerfProbe` was dead code and nobody knew.** A MonoBehaviour sitting in `Dev/Editor/` cannot be `AddComponent`-ed, so every attempt to run it logged "Can't add script behaviour … because it is an editor script" and returned nothing. It has no `UnityEditor` dependency and never did. Moved to `Dev/`. `IslandLook` carries a comment warning about exactly this trap.
  - **The first benchmark measured the wrong thing, and it is worth recording why.** Timing `TerrainHeight.Height` at erosion 0 against erosion 2 reported **1.00×** — because `Noise01` now always routes through `ErodedShaped`, so both arms ran `SimplexD` and they differed only in a multiplier. That measures the erosion PARAMETER, which was never in doubt. **The baseline has to be the function that was replaced**, not the new function with its feature turned down.
  - **The real cost: `ErodedRaw` is 2.07× `Fbm01`** (1618 ns against 784, managed, 400k samples). The landform noise is one of about six fields per height sample, and the whole sample is 5645 ns with erosion against ~4810 without — **about +17 % on the height function**. Terrain gates still pass with room: worst stream crossing **1.63–1.95 ms against a 4 ms budget**, 39 k visible verts against 150 k. Affordable; kept. Burst vectorises differently from managed code so the ratio is indicative, and only a phone can answer GPU and thermals.
  - **Rock is coloured by whether rock WON, not by the gradient.** `VertexColour` painted stone wherever `up < 0.8` — 37 degrees, ordinary hillside — which is what put broad brown smears across every green flank: the shader was guessing at rock because there was no rock in the geometry to ask about. `RockBreak` knows exactly, so the metres of proud rock now ride out of the height job alongside the height and the material is a fact. **It costs nothing**: `Height` is `Evaluate(...).height`, so taking the whole sample and carrying `rock` out with it is the same work. The slope term stays, moved out to genuine cliffs (0.62 → 0.40 of normal.y, 52–66 degrees), because a sheer face is stone whether or not a crag broke out on it.
  - Knobs: `rockShowsAt`, `cliffRockStart`, `cliffRockFull`.

- 2026-08-30 — **Erosion: fBm that carves valleys instead of stacking blobs.**
  - The thing no knob could fix. Each octave is damped by how steep the COARSER ones already are — `n / (1 + erosion·|grad|²)` — so fine detail survives on flats and shoulders and is suppressed on steep faces, which is what erosion does to roughness: material will not stay on a slope. What comes out is spurs with smooth flanks running down to hollows between them. Plain fBm adds smaller blobs to bigger ones and ridged noise makes crests without ever making a drainage; neither can produce this.
  - **It is an approximation, not a simulation.** Real hydraulic erosion is a stateful sweep over a finite grid; this height function is a pure function of world position running in Burst on streamed chunks. There is no water and nothing moves. What it reproduces is the statistical signature.
  - **`SimplexD` — simplex with its analytic derivative**, exact rather than a finite difference: within one simplex cell the corner offsets are the sample point minus a constant, so the derivative is the sum of the three corners' own and no chain rule through the skew is needed. **Checked against a central difference before anything was built on it: mean relative error 4.1e-3, worst 9.9e-3.** A wrong derivative does not error — it damps by the wrong thing and produces a plausible field nothing downstream would ever disagree with.
  - **`erosion = 0` reproduces `Fbm` term for term** (worst difference 1.8e-7 over 4000 samples), and that is what makes the coast safe *by construction rather than by tuning*: the erosion is faded by `interior`, the shoreline sits at mask ≈ 0.23 which is below `massifMaskStart` 0.35, so `interior` is exactly 0 there and the field is bit-for-bit plain fBm. Every beach slope and coastline number this project has measured is untouched. It also means ONE noise evaluation, not two and a lerp.
  - **The distribution had to be remapped, and the project has been burned by this before.** Measured over 60k samples: erosion leaves the mean alone (0.4995 against Fbm01's 0.4998 — unlike the ridged field, which shifted it a quarter of the range and flattened every island) but takes **13% off the spread**, 0.1604 → 0.1396, saturating by erosion 0.5. Unremapped that is 13 % less relief everywhere and it would read as the profile curve being wrong.
  - **Measured across the world, nothing regressed:** walkable median 68.5 % → **72.8 %** (erosion smooths flanks and concentrates the steep, which is the right direction for a game about walking on these), min walkable 40.4 → 41.3, `corr(r_eff, peak)` 0.78 → **0.79**, islands and size distribution unchanged.
  - **The cost, stated plainly:** it shaves the summits. Max peak/r_eff **0.82 → 0.60** and the home island's peak went 111 m → 80 m. That is physically what erosion does, and it is a real loss of drama — compensate on the relief knob if wanted. Median peak/r_eff held at 0.30.
  - **Two things NOT measured.** Perf: `SimplexD` is roughly 1.5× `Simplex` and this is the hottest function in the terrain, on a mobile target — `TerrainPerfProbe` is a MonoBehaviour inside an `Editor` folder so it cannot be attached and could not be run (the same trap `IslandLook` carries a comment about). And the visual: **`BigLook` targets the tallest island but `IslandLook` reframes on the one nearest the ship**, so every shot this session photographed a 60 m islet instead of the 249 m island that was aimed at.

- 2026-08-30 — **Archetypes, part one: there is no tree line any more.**
  - **The horizontal tree line was the most artificial thing on these islands**, and it appears nowhere in the reference boards: `treeLine = max(18, peak * 0.62)`, a contour, with the wood stopping on it like a mown lawn. In the references green climbs nearly to the summit up gentle spurs while bare stone reaches the water on steep faces. **Cover is a function of slope and of what the ground is made of; altitude barely enters into it.** It is now `verdancy × slopeTerm × rockTerm × exposure`, where proud rock refuses trees outright (rock that has broken through is stone, not soil) and exposure only thins the top of an actual mountain.
  - **`Verdancy01` is the third character axis, and it is deliberately NOT independent.** Rolled on its own it produced lush crags and bare downs — which is noise, and is "one weird shape" wearing a third costume. It is suppressed by `Rock01`, so lush-and-soft and bare-and-rocky fall out as recognisable island KINDS without anything enumerating a list of them. Measured spread 0.17–0.83.
  - **Two bugs the instrumentation caught, both invisible from a screenshot.** (1) **The budget double-counted.** `keep` thins uniformly to the 3000-tree cap and was calibrated against the OLD acceptance rate; multiplying three more reducers on top collapsed the wood about fivefold, worst where `keep` was already small — a 314 m island came back with **152 trees**. The expected acceptance now includes verdancy and the budget scales with it, so a bare island is bare because it is bare and not because it is large. (2) **Exposure broke on flat land.** Keyed to `peak` alone, a 5 m sandbank put its exposure band at 4.0–5.1 m, which is the entire island above the beach line — every tree refused, and a 10,000-cell island came back with **zero**. Exposure is a mountain phenomenon and is gated on the island being one.
  - **Measured, big islands before → after the fixes:** 212 → **579**, 605 → **900**, 1061 → **1563** trees. Tree counts across the world now run **0 to 1563, median 38**, read off the baked mesh rather than the scatter rule. The remaining zeros are 2 m-peak sandbanks lying entirely below the beach line — bare sand, which is on brief.
  - Rocks are thinned by `keep` too and the cap is 900: un-thinned, a big island spent its whole 300-boulder budget on the first band the scan reached and left bare ground beyond it, which is the same failure the tree budget already carries a comment about.
  - Nine new tuner knobs (verdancy bias/floor/rock-suppression, the two slope limits, plus the islet set).

- 2026-08-30 — **Islets: "i really like this size of islands. throw in a few more of those."**
  - **The size he pointed at, measured:** 2.7 ha, effective radius 93 m, peak 16 m, **99.9 % walkable** — a low wooded sandbank 562 m from home (his HUD read "home 437 m", carrying Timber). Not a mountain. Worth noticing on its own: **the islands he picked out by eye are the small flat ones**, which is a design signal about camera distance and crew scale, not just a size preference.
  - **`Skerry` adds them to the continentalness NOISE, not to the mask.** `Seabed` is keyed on that same noise and `OverSeabed` lerps from seabed to land *by the mask*, so lifting the mask alone would have left deep-ocean floor under a new island and drawn a spire from −180 m straight up to the beach. Lifting the noise moves the shelf, the shoreline and the beach profile together, exactly as they move for every other island. Gated to open water by a clearance band so an islet can never fuse onto an existing coast and quietly reshape land the world has already been sited against.
  - **An islet is a sandbank, not a sea stack — and nothing else in the pipeline knows how big a landmass is.** Relief comes from fields sampled per point, so a 50 m islet was handed the same mountain amplitude as a 500 m island: measured, the first pass produced 50 m-radius islands with **98–119 m peaks at 5–15 % walkable**. Needles. The islet field is the one place that *does* know a landmass is small, because its own lift says so, so it collapses `upland` and lerps the amplitude to `skerryRelief` (14 m).
  - **Measured, before → after the flattening:** islands 16 → **30**, under 8 ha **7 of 16 → 21 of 30**; worst islet ratio 2.03 → **0.59**; minimum walkable 5.3 % → **40.4 %**; `corr(r_eff, peak/r_eff)` −0.32 → **−0.02**; `corr(r_eff, peak)` **+0.78**. Median peak/r_eff 0.23 → **0.30**, the bottom of the reference band, without touching a single relief knob. And Kevin's own islet is unchanged.
  - Four new tuner knobs: how many, rarity, spacing, islet relief.

- 2026-08-30 — **Correction: big islands are NOT pancakes. And the tuner is Kevin's.**
  - **The "peak/width anti-correlated with width" finding from the entry below was wrong, and it was wrong for a reason this project has already written down once.** It used `MaxRadius × 2` as width — the radial `Island` API's largest sector, which on a lobed island is the length of one thin arm. The GDD already records dropping an island-width investigation for exactly this ("the 355 m was the radial `Island` API, not the land"), and I walked into it anyway. Measured off the actual footprint instead: `corr(r_eff, peak) = +0.69` and `corr(r_eff, peak/r_eff) = +0.22`. **Bigger islands are taller, which is the right direction.** The "3684 m" island is 185 ha with an effective radius of 767 m.
  - What IS true: peak/r_eff runs **0.03 to 0.82, median 0.23** against references at 0.30–0.45. The spread is healthy and the shape of the distribution is fine — the median is simply low, and rock is far too shy at 0.54% of a median island. Neither needs a structural change; both are numbers to turn. So the relief/extent recoupling I proposed is **cancelled** — it was a fix for a bug that measurement says is not there.
  - **`RockBreak` confirmed on screen, not just in the field.** Cranked to `rockRelief` 60 / threshold 0.24, the rockiest island goes from a smooth green loaf with grey painted on its steeper flanks to real rock forms with hard edges and soil filling the hollows between them. At that setting it is far too much — a bed of dragon's teeth, self-similar because the ridged noise is 3 octaves of the same cone at one scale, and unrelated to where ridges and valleys actually run. That last part is the drainage problem again and no amount of tuning fixes it.
  - **`IslandTuner` (`RunProbe.Tune`)** — ten knobs live over the shipped `TerrainSettings`, `↑↓` to pick, `←→` to change (shift ×4), **N** flies the ship to the next island so variety is judged by comparison rather than from memory, **M** measures the island in view by the same numbers `RunProbe.Variety` reports for the world, **P** dumps the settled values. Frequencies are edited as the SCALE in metres they represent, because 1/140 is not a thing anyone can picture. It rebuilds the terrain MESH only — the populator sampled its height function once at Start, so islands, props, dock and village keep the old terrain until play restarts, and the panel says so.
  - **The tuner shipped with a per-frame NullReferenceException and the fix is an old lesson.** Its knob array is plain C# holding delegates, so a domain reload restores the MonoBehaviour with the array NULL and never calls `Start` again — and tuning is precisely when you are editing scripts. `SalvageSpawner` carries the same note for the same reason. Built on demand now, not in `Start`. In this editor that failure presents as a hung MCP bridge, not as an error anyone notices.

- 2026-08-30 — **Rock that protrudes, and the variety problem turns out to be the opposite of what it looked like.**
  - Kevin, on the plan: *"not every island will have a massive cliff... the rock isnt just revealed in the terrain, sometimes it potrudes."* Both land.
  - **Every tuning value in `TerrainSettings` is global.** The only things that differ between islands are `Massif01`, a height multiplier, and `Upland01`, where the relief sits within one island. The archipelago is one island at sixteen sizes — which is a structural fact, not a shortage of noise.
  - **`max`, not `+`, is what makes rock protrude.** The pipeline was lerps, adds and multiplies of one smooth field end to end — not a single `max` in it — so rock could only ever be a *colour on a slope* (`VertexColour` paints it wherever `up < 0.8`). `RockBreak` is the rock's own surface offset from the soil by a sharp ridged field: `max(soil, soil + relief × (ridge − threshold))`. The crease where the two surfaces cross is what the eye reads as a different material pushing through; an add gives a soft bump and nothing else. Soil buries rock in the hollows and rock breaks out on the shoulders for free, and a sea stack is the same field surfacing where the soil is already under water.
  - **`Rock01` is per-island and gates it.** Island-scale field, biased so most islands are soft and a rocky one is an event. It is deliberately NOT gated by `interior`, unlike the massif and the ridges: a rocky island should be rocky down to the waterline. A soft island returns exactly zero, so every shore profile BeachProbe was tuned against is untouched on the islands meant to have beaches. Measured spread across the world: rockiness **0.02 to 0.76**, rocky ground 0% to 13%.
  - **The finding I did not expect: peak-to-width is ANTI-CORRELATED with width.** The widest island in the game (3684 m) has a ratio of **0.048**; the narrow ones run up to **0.577**. The references are big islands with big mountains; ours are pancakes when large and cones when small — backwards, and a large part of why they read wrong. The mechanism is `Upland01`: a 1/700 field, so a 384 m island fits inside one upland blob and is mountain everywhere, while a 3.7 km island averages to the mean and is plain everywhere. Relief has to be tied to the landmass's own extent, not to an independent field sampled per point.
  - New probe: `RunProbe.Variety` — the distribution of peak/width, rocky % and walkable % across every island, because "they all look the same" is a question about the SPREAD and cannot be settled by looking at two of them.

- 2026-08-30 — **The crew were walking 30 metres above the island.**
  - Kevin: *"my crew dont walk on it as much as they glide above it and then into it."* Exactly right, and it measured worse than it looked. `CrewAgent.WalkToWorld` set `y = target.y` — **the elevation of the destination, held for the entire walk**. A crew member setting off for a tree sixty metres up the hill popped to sixty metres on the first step and glided there through the air; coming back with a log they took the ship's height and sank into the slope. Measured against a raycast onto the drawn mesh, across a five-hand shore party: **mean 30.3 m off the ground, worst 94.3 m, four of five airborne**. After sampling the height field under their feet: **mean 0.02 m, worst 0.21 m**.
  - **The terrain itself was never the problem, and that is worth knowing.** The drawn chunk mesh agrees with `TerrainHeight` to **7 mm mean, 48 mm worst** over 90 m inland. So the analytic surface *is* the visible surface: anything placed with the height function is on the ground the player sees, and the whole class of "is it where it looks" doubt is closed for good.
  - Also fixed in passing: the in-the-water test used the island's MEAN radius, which on a lobed island calls dry land sea and floats the crew home on the swell. It is the shoreline on their own bearing now.

- 2026-08-30 — **You could not land anywhere, and it was two bugs wearing one symptom.**
  - **Casting off from the pier left the ship still thinking she was at it.** `weighTime` is 0, so `WeighAnchor` takes its instant path every single time — and the *timed* branch in `Update` was the only one that cleared `CurrentDock` and the forced mooring heading. It was dead code. `MoorAlongside` takes its pier branch whenever `CurrentDock` is set, so at every island the crew ever landed on **the plank stayed inboard, `landingPending` never resolved, nobody went ashore**, and the anchor spring was quietly easing her back toward the home berth from a thousand metres away. Harmless while she started adrift; on the only path there is now, it was every voyage. The two teardowns are one `GetUnderway()`.
  - **`Island.Nearest` ranked by CENTRE distance in a world of lobed islands 900–2000 m across.** A ship lying 20 m off a small island's beach is routinely nearer the *centre* of a big one several hundred metres away — and then the landing prompt, the grounding check, the camera's hill clamp and the crew's pathing all answer about an island she is nowhere near. Measured: **12 of Island_1's 34 approach bearings had no landing prompt at all**, on the island whose shore lies closest to home and which the player therefore meets first. Ranked by shore gap instead (negative inside, which is what grounding wanted anyway): 22/34 → **34/34**, and the world went to 15 of 15 islands landable. The 3–7 residual "wrong island" bearings on the big ones are real shore interleaving and correctly answered.
  - **The lesson is about the probe, not the bugs.** A survey of the geometry said all 15 islands were landable and every beach test passed — and you still could not gather a log. The failure was entirely *after* the prompt. `LandProbe.Sail` presses the button and then watches for thirty seconds; the first version of it reported `gangway False, crew ashore 0, hold 0` for the full thirty, which is the whole diagnosis in one line. **A check that stops at the last thing it can compute stops before the thing that is broken.**

- 2026-08-30 — **The loop closes: cast off, cut wood, come alongside, build.**
  - **A voyage now begins and ends at the BERTH, not at a radius.** The old test was `distance to homePoint < homeRadius`, which stopped working the day she started the game tied up: the home island's centre is a couple of hundred metres inland of a 46 m pier, so she is already outside any sane radius while still made fast. Arrival is `AnchorController.AtHomeDock`; departure is 150 m off the berth. **And she has to have BEEN at her berth before she can leave it** — the dock does not exist until the populator has found the island and the ship is not moved onto it until the frame after, so for the first frames she sits at her scene position 483 m from a berth that does not exist, which reads exactly like a ship that has sailed. The loop completed a 0-second voyage on frame one. Three-second grace for a dev spawn that never touches the pier (`PlaytestStart` puts her in the western storm).
  - **What home can KEEP is not what she can carry, and that gap IS the first building.** The hold takes 24 to the marked line and 38 stuffed with deck cargo; the open beach keeps **30**. So the first full voyage lands short — measured, 38 landed, 30 kept, **8 left on the sand** — and the panel says so in amber. A storehouse costs 25 timber and keeps 40 more. That is one full hold and a log over: **two voyages, never one**, and the second one has to come home. [superseded 2026-09-22: no home island]
  - **The first buildings in the game.** `BuildPlan` (data, no prefabs) → `Village.Raise` sites it → `BuildingFactory` raises it from primitives at `WorldScale.Storehouse` (4.4 m ridge, above a hut, well under the longhouse). Golden-angle spiral from the middle of the clearing so the village fills inside-out; doors face the middle, because a building with its back to the village is the tell that nobody chose where it went.
  - **Nothing is flattened, because nothing CAN be.** `TerrainHeight.Height` is a pure function of position running in Burst on streamed chunks — there is no per-building data it could consult. So a site is *chosen*, its four corners are measured, and the building sits at its **highest** corner with a footing deep enough to bridge down to its lowest. Measured across five: 0.87–1.54 m of drop under an 8×5 m footprint.
  - **The clearing is not the inscribed circle, and that was worth 5 buildings.** The largest circle that fits entirely in buildable ground guarantees every point in it is buildable — which sounds right and costs the village everything: home's best in-frame one is **12.6 m**, which holds exactly one storehouse. The guarantee was never needed: `Village.Corners` tests each building against the actual rectangle it stands on. The clearing is sized by what the SHOT holds instead (30 m), and bad ground inside it is simply refused. Five storehouses fit where one did. [superseded 2026-09-22: no home island]
  - **The village is sited into the camera, not the other way round.** The island's largest buildable patch is 92 m from the head of the pier. `SettlementSite.Find` gained a `preferNear` reach so the village goes where the docked shot looks — and the two constants that define that shot moved onto `Dock` (`ViewCentre`, `ViewFrom`), because the camera and the siting now both need them and two copies of 41 m is a gate that stops gating the day one is retuned.
  - **The editor's Game view is landscape and this game is not.** Projecting ground through the live camera at 1.41 put the village at viewport x 0.18 — comfortably in frame — and at the shipping 900×1500 it is at **x −0.26, off the left edge**. The docked shot in portrait is a long narrow wedge: **±43 m across the pier axis against 252 m inland**. `Dock.ViewHalfWidth = 42` records it. A shot judged in the editor window certifies a picture no player will ever see.
  - **A keep-out that suppresses geometry, never draws.** `IslandScenery` walks ONE `System.Random` through the grid in order, so a `continue` removes rolls and reshuffles every tree after it — nudge a clearing five metres and the whole wood moves. Every candidate takes the same numbers out of the stream whether it is built or not. Measured off the two baked meshes: **267 of 267 trees outside the clearing matched at 0.000 m displacement**; the clearing cost 31 trees and moved none.
  - Probes: `RunProbe.Village` (clearing, emptiness, the shuffle check), `RunProbe.VillageBuild`, `RunProbe.VillageShot`, `RunProbe.Loop` (a whole voyage in ten seconds — it moves the *ship* and lets the real systems run, rather than calling `CompleteVoyage` itself).

- 2026-08-28 — **You can see the weather you are sailing toward.**
  - **A storm you cannot see the edge of is a wall, not weather.** Kevin sailed the western deep and could not find a way out in any direction, because the sky was one colour all round: `SkyDirector` sampled storminess only at the ship. Measured from where he was, at (-2445, 724), the eight bearings 3 km out read **0.84–0.88 everywhere except due south at 0.05** — there *was* a way out, and nothing on screen said so. Note east was still 0.84: "sail toward home" would not have worked either.
  - **A rose of eight bearings**, sampled a few km ahead through `SeaStateController.SkyStorminessAt` — a new method rather than a copied `LogInverseLerp(8, 55, ...)`, because a duplicated constant is a gate that silently stops gating. Pushed as two float4s; the sky shader unpacks them by view azimuth with a branchless dot gather (dynamic indexing is not dependable at target 3.0).
  - **The first version was invisible, and the measurement said so.** Colouring only the horizon gradient moved the clear bearing **6.7 %** brighter — and a *fully stormy* bearing came back as the brightest of the four tiles. In a storm the cloud deck owns everything above ~8° (`lift = smoothstep(0, 0.13, dir.y)`) and `_HorizonSharp` 3.4 drags the zenith down to the skyline, so the cue was being painted over. **The rose had to drive the cloud lid**: it thins to 22 % and lights toward the clear horizon on the bearing that is better. After: blue-channel difference between the stormy bearing and the clearing, **62.7** of 255.
  - **Phrased as a CONTRAST, not an absolute.** `saturate(_SS_Storminess - roseS)`, not `1 - roseS`. The absolute form thinned the cloud deck across the whole sky in fair weather, where every bearing is clear and there is nothing to point at — a cue that fires everywhere points nowhere, and it would have quietly rewritten the fair-weather sky this feature was never meant to touch.
  - **Brightness is the wrong thing to measure here.** The sun dominates it: the tile toward the sun read brightest whatever the weather was. The clearing's signature is the **blue-red spread** (14.4 against ~7.0 on every other bearing), because the cue is the clear horizon's colour, not its luminance. Measuring the centre third of the frame rather than the full 60° also matters — a clearing one bearing wide is diluted to nothing by a full-width average, but it is what fills the view when you steer at it.
  - Knobs on `SkyDirector`: `horizonRoseStrength` (0 disables), `horizonRoseDistance` (3 km), `horizonRoseResponse`. Weather only for now; GDD §5's north-cold / south-hot / east-dry character can ride the same eight bearings later.

- 2026-08-28 — **The sun rises in the east, the moon has a real phase, and night is dark but not black.**
  - **Time of day is layered UNDER the storm, not beside it.** The clear palette became a function of the hour and `Storminess01` then blends that toward the storm palette exactly as it always did, so `Apply(t)` kept its shape and a night storm is darker than either on its own without a third palette. The blend runs on **sun ELEVATION, not the clock** — dawn and dusk then share one authored palette and differ only because the sun's own arc puts it on the other side of the sky.
  - **One directional light, two roles.** URP shadows exactly one directional light, so the sun hands off to the moon at `sunY < -0.02`, where the blended intensity is already down at moon level — a change of direction in a dim light rather than a flash. Storm dims the key light by a **ratio** (0.34/1.15 ≈ ×0.30), not to an absolute: an absolute 0.34 made a midnight storm *brighter* than a clear night, because the moon only gives 0.20.
  - **Measured, day vs night:** sky 57.7 % → **4.0 %** grey, sea 24.6 % → **8.4 %**. Fourteen times darker overhead, three times on the water — dark enough that the lantern will matter, light enough to pick out a wave face, which is the design intent (night is attention and risk, not a wall).
  - **`MoonPhase01` had the wrong sign, and the geometry is what caught it.** `MoonDirection` puts the moon at `t + 0.5 + lag`, so at lag 0 it is *opposite* the sun — a **full** moon — while `0.5 - 0.5cos` called that new. The shader draws the terminator from the two direction vectors, so a fully lit disc was lighting the world like a new moon (×0.25 of the moonlight) with nothing reporting a disagreement. `+cos` makes the light agree with the disc.
  - **Two ocean night terms, both phrased so an unset global is today's behaviour** (the `_SS_LayerOff` rule). The body colour carries no diffuse and no normal term — it is authored, not lit — so unlike the specular and the subsurface, which ride `sun.color` and dim themselves once the moon takes over, nothing about it knew the sun had set; it is dimmed ×0.30. And the reflection probe is deliberately never refreshed (`SkyDirector` skips `DynamicGI.UpdateEnvironment` for cost), so at night it is still a daylight sky: the sky mix leans 0.55 → **0.93** on the authored horizon, which *is* correct after dark. **The foam had the same fault** — its `0.55` ambient term is flat and left every whitecap burning at 55 % white on a 4 %-grey sky, reading as snow rather than water.
  - **The stars had to be sized against a PIXEL.** A cell is 1/90 rad, under four pixels at this camera; the first falloff faded over 1/9 of a cell — a star about **four tenths of a pixel** wide — so almost every one fell between sample points. Measured result: no pixel in the whole night sky above 25/255 and not a star in it. Fading over ~0.3 of a cell puts the core near a pixel across.
  - **`ShaderStrip.DaySheet` pins the DAY, not just the hour.** `pinTime` goes through `SetTime01`, which preserves the day number, and the moon's bearing *and* phase both hang off `day / synodicDays` — so at a 180 s day a play session rolls the counter over in three minutes, and the dawn tile lost its moon between one run and the next with nothing reporting a change. Same rule as pinning the sea state and the wave phase.
  - **Two traps re-paid.** A `MonoBehaviour` cannot live in an `Editor/` folder — `AddComponent` returns null and the sheet dies on the dereference; `ShaderStrip` moved to `Scripts/Dev/`. And the fragment shader already owned `n` for its cloud noise, so the moon normal is `mn` — a redefinition there compiles clean in C# and renders **magenta**.
  - **Open:** the sea keeps its daylight teal at night — the body dim scales brightness but not saturation, and moonlit water should read desaturated blue-grey. `nightSeaDim` / `nightSeaSkyMix` on `SkyDirector` are the knobs; it is a feel call, not a bug.

- 2026-08-28 — **She floats where she was drawn, and the paddle wheels stop strobing.**
  - **Measured, not eyeballed: she rode 0.57 m above her designed waterline, and it was not speed.** The obvious suspect was dynamic bow lift (the screenshot was 20 m/s in flat water — maximum lift, no wave to bury her). `WaterlineProbe` says 0.57 m at rest and 0.55 m at 19.7 m/s, so it was a **static displacement error** the whole time. The visible cost was the paddle wheels: the model draws them biting 1.32 m and they were biting **0.75 m**, which is exactly what "floating on top of the water" looks like.
  - **The sign of the fix is the opposite of the intuition.** Lowering the probe rig does not sink her — the probes sit deeper for a given hull position, make more lift, and she rides *higher* (measured −0.57 m of rig → +0.59 m of hull). `ProbeLift` 0.55 m raises the **hull** probes; the rail probes stay on the actual rails, because their job is to measure green water over them. After: designed waterline 0.00 m above water, keel 1.02 m under (drawn 1.02), bite 1.29 m (drawn 1.32). `BuryProbe` still passes on 0.55 m less freeboard — deckOverMax −0.57 m against −0.52 before.
  - **The wheels were honest and that was the problem.** Rolling without slip at 20 m/s on a 2.53 m wheel is 75 rpm — with eight blades, a strobe against the frame rate. Real paddle steamers turn at 25–35 rpm; her 20 m/s is a game number, not a ship's speed. A **visual gearbox on the way to the mesh only** (`rate → m·tanh(rate/m)`, m = 3.6 rad/s = 34 rpm) is linear near zero, so at manoeuvring speeds the wheels still read as what she is doing, and it only compresses as the rate climbs. `PortRate`/`StarboardRate` stay honest for spray, audio and probes.
  - **Open:** the wheel axle sits 1.23 m above water, which is the drawn geometry. Putting the arms *on* the surface as asked means burying the wheel to its axle — roughly four times a real paddle steamer's immersion — and costs another 1.2 m of freeboard on top. Left as a design question rather than tuned blind.

- 2026-08-28 — **The ocean becomes one living system: weather in metres, a sea between a pond and the apocalypse, and water that drifts.**
  - **The abruptness had three causes and only one was tuning.** (1) *There was no middle sea*: three anchors at Hs 1.3 / 3.5 / 65, `nominalHs` lerped linearly, so 95 % of the visible range lived above severity 0.5 and every sea worth sailing sat in a 0.07-wide sliver. (2) *The storm ramp was a doorway*: 450 → 1250 m, measured at **×1.82 in wave height every 100 m sailed**, and then pinned at 65 m for the rest of the world. (3) *Every knob was denominated in severity*, which is a blend coordinate and not a unit — the same mistake that took 57 % off the waves on 08-25.
  - **`SeaState_Rough`, Hs 14 on 260 m rollers, at severity 0.70** (normal moves to 0.40). Hs 14 is the geometric mean of 3.5 and 65, which is what makes the ladder even. Authored rather than interpolated: wind 17, depth 50 so a 260 m roller runs at 17.8 m/s against her 20 and she can still catch it, 13 m of swell plus a 5 m train crossing at 45°, median face about 10° — a real step between normal's 3.5° and the storm's 13–15°. Severity 1.00 is still byte-exact Stormy.
  - **The weather is authored in METRES of Hs.** `HsAt`/`SeverityForHs` are exact inverses of what `LerpFrom` actually produces, and every gradient runs in **log Hs**, because the eye reads wave height as a ratio — 3→6 m is the same step as 30→60. `setDepth 0.16` now really is a 16 % swing in height, and sets are no longer gated to storm water: as a fraction of Hs they self-scale, so a 3.5 m shelf breathes 2.9–4.1 and the storm breathes 55–75.
  - **Storm ramp 500 → 2800 m.** Steepest step now **×1.20 per 100 m**, and the crossing passes through lively, rough, heavy, wild and mountainous with hundreds of metres in each instead of skipping four of them.
  - **Distance sets the CEILING, not the sea.** `WeatherField` adds drifting **storm cells** (kilometres, C# only) that decide where under that ceiling the water actually is. §5's "distance is the difficulty curve" survives — it governs what is *possible*. A squall can now reach the home shelf and can never take it past *rough*; the deep west runs 45–65 m instead of being pinned at 65.
  - **And drifting roughness patches** (hundreds of metres, GPU + Burst) in the envelope, coupled 0.35/0.85/1.0 across the cascades: the chop swings ×0.55–1.0 across the view while the swell only moves ×0.84–1.0. That is what a roughness patch *is* — short-wave energy the long swell rolls through — and it keeps the layer clear of everything already measured, since cascade 0 carries nearly all of Hs.
  - **One baked tile, read by both twins.** Periodic value noise baked once; the GPU and the Burst job sample the same texture, so there is no second noise implementation in HLSL to drift. It *translates* rather than evolving (weather advects) with a closed-form meander, so the path through it never closes — and closed form rather than integrated, because the field must stay a pure function of (seed, `OceanTime`, position) or `Scrub` stops making probes repeatable.
  - **Patches only ever reduce.** An envelope above 1 asks for waves the depth limit and the seabed cannot hold (why `farScale` went 1.5 → 1.0), and steeper water buys another Newton step there is no budget for. The cost is a mean below 1: the first version's 0.419 took a quarter off the chop *everywhere* and would have quietly undone the 08-25 texture pass. `patchBias` is applied **at bake time** (this runs eight times per query inside the Newton loop) to put the mean at 0.66 — the default state of the water is the sea as authored, and a patch is a slick passing through it.
  - **`SeaSeverityAt` was wrong in the direction that mattered.** It scaled *severity* by the envelope; half the severity of a 65 m storm is a 7 m sea, half its *envelope* is a 32 m one. Sickness roughness, the sky lift and the nav tape were all being told the water beside an island in a storm was nearly flat. `SeaHsAt` is the honest version and `SeaSeverityAt` derives from it. Sea-state names come from metres now (six bands, 6 % hysteresis so a set cannot make the label flicker), and `Storminess01` is a geometric ramp on Hs 8 → 55.
  - **Gates.** `DivergenceProbe` **PASSES** and now actually *exercises* the new term — it builds a weather field on a deliberately tiny 220 m tile so patches vary hard across its 600 m disc, and reports the field's span so a term that went flat would say so instead of being certified for free. env max 0.000267 (unmoved), total max 2.61 cm, 0 of 5000 over the 6.5 cm gate, `SampleBatch` 0.286 ms against a 0.4 ms budget. New probe `LivingSeaTrace` measures the two things "alive" means and neither is visible in a screenshot: ×1.20/100 m steepest gradient, 30 min at one spot spanning Hs 4.2–10.0 with the closest self-repeat at 14 % of mean, slicks every ~3 min lasting ~30 s over 16 % of the time.
  - **The serialisation trap bit again, on the component this work added.** Two rounds of retuning changed `tileMetres` and `driftSpeed` in code and neither reached the scene; only the octave periods, which are a local inside `Bake()`, actually moved. `TuneLivingSea` pushes every value through `SerializedObject` and reads it back.

- 2026-08-25 — **The sea out west becomes the mountains, and they are water you ride.**
  - **What the folklore is, physically.** Severity 1.0 in the deep west now measures **Hs 62 m with the worst wave 79 m crest-to-trough on 515 m rollers** — a face of **263 m, about 11 boat lengths** to climb. The old sea was Hs 9 on 125 m waves: a 63 m face, 2.6 boat lengths, nothing to climb. Height was never the problem and adding height alone would have repeated the 2026-08-18 mistake; the job was **wavelength**, and the acceptance criterion is face angle and face length, not metres.
  - **`SwellDirector`/`MountainSea` are not coming back.** §5 rewrote them out. A 100 m cliff 30 m thick is something that kills you; the premise asks for water you ride, and these are waves in the wave field with no bespoke obstacle anywhere.
  - **Cascade 0's patch 512 m → 2048 m.** The wavelengths this needs did not exist in the simulation: at 512 m the ONLY representable wavelength in the 400–900 m band was that single mode, a perfect sine repeating every 512 m. 2048 m carries 32 radial bins where there were 8, and costs nothing — same N, same textures, same FFT, cascade 0's texel merely goes 2 m → 8 m on waves it only ever carries at 64 m and up.
  - **The swell is integrated over its k-bin, not sampled in it.** Its radial width is `k_s/(2·sharpness)`, about a fifth of one bin for a 470 m roller — a supersample hits or misses it by luck and realized height stops matching authored height. Closed-form (erf) integration fixes it exactly, which is what made a *narrow* band usable, and the narrow band is what makes the rollers uniform: the typical wave went from 0.59×Hs to 0.71×Hs, and per-wave height from 10.8 m to 39.8 m at the same Hs.
  - **A wave may not be taller than the water under it.** The envelope gains a depth limit (0.55 × depth, from the shore grid the terrain already streams) in both twins. Chosen over a hand-tuned inshore falloff because it makes seabed clipping *structurally* impossible at any wave size instead of a thing to re-tune whenever the sea grows — and it reads as gameplay for free: the sea lies down as you come in off the deep. **Seabed clearance went 4.07 m → 43 m** at severity 1.0.
  - **The open ocean floor is −180 m, the shelf is still −12 m.** A single global seabed cannot do both: deeper drags every shoreline down with it (the mask lerps land *towards* the floor) and turns the archipelago into spires. The ramp is keyed on the mask **noise**, not the mask — keyed on the mask it steepens every beach, because the shoreline sits at mask ≈ 0.23, and the mask is exactly zero across the whole ocean so it cannot express distance from land at all. Measured: beach-walkable failed at 57 samples over 1 m/m, worst 4.22; re-keyed, 5 samples, worst 1.38.
  - **The dispersion depth is a deliberate lie.** 30 m against a 180 m seabed, which slows the rollers from 32 m/s to about 17 so a 7.5 m/s boat can work a face instead of being overtaken four times over. It only touches the long waves (`kd` saturates by λ≈9 m), it costs nothing, and it halves orbital velocity into the bargain — peak vertical acceleration at these sizes is **0.09 g**, so the sea gets five times bigger and *gentler*, which the smoothness pillar wanted anyway. Nothing in buoyancy or the sampler reads it. Do not "fix" it.
  - **Horizon on PC: 8 rings (4096 m) and displacement out to 2600 m**, because a 500 m wave cannot be seen from inside it — the baseline shots show the old 500 m fade as a disc of detail around the boat with flat water beyond. An extra ring rather than a coarser inner cell, so near-field chop is untouched. **Mobile is deliberately unchanged and the feature does not read there yet**; that is its own pass.
  - **`farScale` 1.5 → 1.0.** A post-hoc amplitude gain is the wrong instrument once the west has a storm spectrum of its own: multiplying an authored 45 m sea by 1.5 asks for 67 m, which needs 150 m of water before the depth limit will allow it. The regional story is carried by the weather target instead.
  - **Foam was saturating and it was measured, not guessed.** A one-variable A/B at a pinned instant: near water went **77.3% grey → 47.4%** with injection off, so the white was foam and not fog. `foamThreshold` 0.68 → 0.30, injection 0.9 → 0.45.
  - **`DivergenceProbe` was never an ocean bug** — see its own entry below. It is green at 0.23 cm and now gates the *shipped* storm amplitudes, with a synthetic shore grid so the depth limit is actually exercised (50% of sample points).
  - **She already sails it, which was not the expectation.** `RideProbe` (new) puts her bow-on to the storm in genuine deep water for a minute: pitch median 4.9°, max **21°**; roll max 11.4° against a 20° limit it never reaches; the chase camera sits **19 m** over the water and never once touches its 2.6 m floor; speed median 6.2 m/s with peaks of **12.8** as she surfs down a face. `BuryProbe` passes and is *better* than on the old sea — deck-over max −0.52 m against −0.14, draft RMS 0.07 m against 0.60. The reason is the same one that made this feature possible: a 515 m wave is nearly flat under 24 m of hull, so the giant sea is gentler on her than the 125 m chop it replaced.
  - **`pitchLimit` 16° → 26°, and the honest note is that it barely mattered.** The limit was measured firing on 11.5 % of frames, which looked like the spring holding her bow down into a face. Freeing it moved peak pitch from 18.9° to 19.1–21.0° across runs — inside run-to-run variation. She tops out around 20° on her own because a 20.9 m hull follows the mean slope under its length, not the steepest point of a face. The change is still right (nothing pushes back now) but it is a tidy-up, not the fix that was predicted.
  - **Open:** `StallProbe` is void until it stops warping to a hardcoded (-1500, 0, 0) — that spot is in 12 m of water at 11 % envelope, and it duly reported `maxPitch=0` at every severity with plow ON identical to OFF. `BuoyProbe` is still stale from the ship swap. Plow drag, spray thresholds and the seasickness roughness metric are still authored against a 9 m sea and have not been re-examined. Mobile tier untouched. Surface shading is still the interim look.
- 2026-08-25 — **The parity gate had been measuring nothing at all, for a fortnight.**
  - `DivergenceProbe` went red at the world-generation rebuild and was read as an ocean parity drift ever since. It was two instrument faults, found by making the probe report a **three-way split** — envelope / readback / inversion — instead of one number that could be any of them.
  - **An unbound texture silently skips a whole compute dispatch.** `RegionField.Publish` bound `_Ocean_ShoreTex` only when a shore grid existed, and OceanLab has no terrain. A fragment shader tolerates that because a guard stops it sampling; a compute dispatch validates every declared resource up front, refuses, and writes **nothing**. The verify kernel returned zeros for every query, so the probe was comparing the CPU sampler against an empty buffer. The tell was only ever in `Editor.log`.
  - **The readback ring ranked slots by timestamp**, so any backward scrub wedged it permanently: the fresh slot's time is lower than the stale ones, so it never becomes Latest, so it counts as free, so the next Tick recycles it. Probes scrub backwards constantly. That is the entire explanation of the famous scatter — 26 cm, then zero samples, then 147 cm, from identical code. Slots are ranked by completion order now.
  - Green at **0.23 cm max / 0.019 cm mean**, three consecutive runs byte-identical including one whose every target was a backward scrub. The 0.23 cm is the half-precision quantum of the displacement texture — the measurement floor, not an error.

- 2026-08-25 — **She doubles in size, and the sea always moves.**
  - **Twice the boat, same hands.** Import scale 1.7 to 3.4: 24.2 m overall, hull 20.86 by 8.44 m, 19.2 t. Crew, guns and the helm wheel stay their old size, so she reads as a ship with people on her rather than a launch. `SetupPaddleBoat.Scale` is now the ONE number that sizes her — hull, probes, mass (cube law), hull speed (root law), camera, freeboard, gun and crew stations all derive from it, because the first cutover proved what happens when half of them stay behind in absolute metres.
