The helm wheel is counter-scaled on itself; counter-scaling its *stand* instead moved the wheel 3 m up the deck and took the helmsman with it.
  - **The sea is never glass.** `RegionField` multiplied wave height toward literal zero near land — a calm shelf at 0.35, times island falloff, times the shore factor — so sheltered water was a mirror. The envelope now has a **chop floor** it cannot fall below in water, while land stays perfectly flat (the floor is gated on a "is there water here" term from the same shore lookup). `SeaStateController` gets a matching `calmFloor` so the open sea does not go glassy either. Measured at the gentlest weather the game produces: inshore RMS **0.068 m** where it used to be zero, 24 % of offshore — texture without consequence, which is what was asked for. Both twins edited together per the parity contract.
  - **The lanterns hang through their brackets.** Two faults. `Quaternion.FromToRotation` pins an axis but leaves the roll about it arbitrary, so the chain ring came out flat and sticking sideways; it now constrains two axes. And more seriously, the pivots had been rotating **empty GameObjects** all along — Unity refuses to reparent a child out of a prefab instance and `SetParent` fails silently, so the lanterns never left `Details`. The setup unpacks the instance first.
  - **Open, and not caused by the boat: `DivergenceProbe` is red.** 26.32 cm max with the ocean changes stashed against 26.43 with them — the same number, so the chop floor is not the cause, but the documented baseline was 0.23 cm. Something between then and now broke the C#/shader parity gate. Symptoms look like readback lag rather than a formula mismatch (repeat runs of identical code scatter across 26 cm, zero samples and 147 cm). Needs its own session.
- 2026-08-25 — **Paddle boat, second pass: the sea stays out of her, and everything stands on the planking.** Field notes from the first build, all four fixed.
  - **The sea no longer renders through the deck.** In mountainous seas the surface climbs above the planking and drew straight through it — you looked at open water inside the hull. Fixed in the ocean's own fragment shader: `HullWaterClip` feeds it the hull's inboard volume in ship space and the ocean clips itself away inside it. Deliberately **not** a stencil pass — a stencil mask needs an extra draw and gets the near field wrong, because ocean *between the camera and the boat* lands in the same screen pixels as her interior and gets punched out too. The clip is exact from every angle, costs four instructions a pixel and adds no draw call. The plan section is an **ellipse**, not a box, because a hull tapers and a box would reach past the stem and cut a notch in the sea ahead of her. Gated by A/B at a pinned wave phase with the sea held 0.50 m over the deck: clip off, she is swamped to the rails; clip on, the deck is dry and the sea stops at the hull.
  - **The deck is not flat, and one deck constant floated everything.** It has camber and sheer and spans 0.67 m in height; the crew, guns and cargo were all placed at its *maximum* (1.23 m) and hung in the air by up to half a metre. The setup script now samples the deck mesh — 1992 verts, y 0.67 amidships against 0.99 forward — and stands each thing on the planking under its own feet. Guns are also placed inboard of where the planking actually ends at their own station, since the deck narrows toward the bow and a fixed offset put the fore guns out through the railing.
  - **The lanterns hang.** They were rigid and pointing sideways: the pivot had been given the lamp's own world rotation *and* the lamp kept its 90° grandchild rotation, which is 180° out. Worse, `LanternSwing` wrote `localRotation` outright and wiped the rest pose. Now the pivot is parented to the **ship** (so the pendulum runs in hull axes — the visual root's 90° yaw would otherwise swap fore-and-aft for athwartships), the swing is applied *from* a stored rest pose, and the lamp is oriented by **measuring** it: point the mesh's longest axis down, decide which end is the chain by which half spreads less, and slide it so the chain top meets the pivot. No hand-derived Euler angles through an axis conversion that had already been wrong twice.
  - **Water aboard reads on the ship panel**, under the hull bar, as a percentage — `water 22 % — 2 bailing`. It used to be a panel that appeared over the boat mid-storm, which is precisely what you are trying to look past. The jettison **button** stays low and central because it has to be under a thumb.
- 2026-08-25 — **The player ship is a paddle steamer.** The sloop is replaced by `lowpoly_boat.blend`, imported at 1.7 (12.1 m overall, hull 10.4 by 4.2 m) rather than scaled to the sloop's 17.5 m: at the sloop's size the boat's own detailing — railings, planks, lanterns — scaled with it and read as oversized. The raiders keep the old hull for now.
  - **She is not driven by wind.** She carries no sail, so `ShipMotor.windDriven` is off for her: no Stokes drift, no sail trim. Wind still exists — it drives the sea state and the raiders who *do* carry sail — and it now reads under the minimap instead of as `WindArrow` floating over the ship. What was deliberately *kept* is the head-sea penalty: that term is about **waves**, not sails, and a paddle steamer still loses way charging a sea, which is the skill trade §6 wants.
  - **The wheels turn her.** `PaddleDrive` runs both wheels off a common rate — the greater of rolling with the water and what the throttle asks, so they slip while she builds way — plus an opposite term from the helm. Outer wheel faster, inner slower and reversing at full helm; with the throttle shut and the helm hard over they counter-rotate and she comes round in her own length. The tuned yaw model still rules at speed; the differential only adds a low-speed assist, so honesty in the visual never cost handling. She has **no rudder**, so the wheels and the helm wheel are the only steering cue.
  - **The hull was re-measured, not inherited.** `BuoyancyProbeSet.HullLayout` exposes the probe radii that `SloopLayout` hardcoded — radius is what sets how fast a probe saturates, and the sloop's 1.1 m spheres on a shallow-draft boat make a cork. Probes 10.43 by 4.22 m, keel −0.51, rails 1.10, radii 0.50/0.55/0.45, mass 2400 kg, `totalVolume` 3.90 m³ — the volume chosen to hold the sloop's float ratio of 0.60 so she sits at the same *relative* submersion the buoyancy gates were tuned against. Measured: draft 0.46 m light with 1.28 m of freeboard, 0.75 m laden with 0.99 m.
  - **Everything authored in metres had to be rescaled**, and this is the lesson worth keeping: `maxSpeed` 18 is 35 knots and the first probe run measured her doing 20.4 m/s with her deck buried in her own bow wave; the freeboard sink amounts were authored against the sloop's ~2 m of freeboard; the camera framed a 21 m ship. Now 7.5 m/s, sinks 0.30/0.44/0.26, camera 12/8. Cargo stacks amidships instead of over the transom, guns and crew stations moved to the 10.4 m deck, and a helmsman stands at the wheel permanently (the player character later).
  - **Lanterns swing.** `LanternSwing` runs each lamp as a damped pendulum off the hull's own acceleration and attitude rather than a sine wave, so it lurches when she lurches and hangs still in a calm.
  - Gate: `PaddleProbe` — float light and laden, makes way with the rim speed matching, wheels differential at helm, counter-rotating and spinning on the spot with the throttle shut. ALL PASS.
  - **Open:** `player_ship_cannon.fbx` is empty — it imports as a GameObject and Transform with no mesh — so the guns are still the procedural cubes-and-cylinders `Cannon.Build` makes, repositioned for the new deck. Wiring a real cannon model is one edit once a non-empty FBX exists.
- 2026-08-25 — **The islands are finished: honest map, honest shores, measured cost.** Closing the four items the world rebuild left open, ahead of the ship work.
  - **The minimap draws land, not circles.** It was drawing one `MaxRadius` disc per island, which on a 500 m concave island covered more water than land and swallowed bays the ship can sail into. `TerrainWorldPopulator` now keeps the flood-fill grid it was already computing (`LandMask`, 24 m cells over the 3 km discovery disc, tagged by component) instead of throwing it away, and the minimap paints it into a point-filtered texture coloured by what each landmass carries — a scan cell is about a pixel wide at map range, so this costs one blit and one texture rebuild whenever an island is stripped. Land too small to become an island is drawn too, because it is still something to hit. `MiniMap.Ring` was also drawing a *filled* disc at half alpha; over real coastline the raider patrol circles covered the coast they were about, so it is an outline now.
  - **`beachMaxSlope` 0.70 → 0.50, chosen off a distribution.** `BeachProbe` re-measures the shore rise per metre over 12 m inland on all 46 bearings of every island and reports the landable fraction at five candidate thresholds. At the old 0.70, **93 % of all shore in the world was landable** — every island was one long beach. 0.50 gives 77 %, 0.40 gives 62 %, and the median shore slope is 0.35. No island is unlandable at any of them, so the safety floor never binds; on the big islands (Home 346 m, Island_7 396 m, Island_1 514 m) 0.50 is the first value where an approach is a decision again. 0.40 is on the table if the cliffs should bite harder. [superseded 2026-09-22: no home island — "Home" is now just one island's name]
  - **`IslandMeshBuilder` deleted.** It had outlived the radial mesher and lingered only for its `Profile` struct; the struct is now `Island.Profile`, trimmed to the three fields the terrain actually fills, alongside `Island.Sectors`. The dead authored-profile setter and the radial-mesh branch of `SurfacePoint` went with it — a profile now only ever comes from the terrain, which always sets `TerrainHeight`.
  - **Terrain cost measured, and the geometry is not the problem.** `TerrainPerfProbe` reports what the GPU is asked to do (device-independent) and leaves GPU ms to a device, as `PerfProbe` does for the ocean. Against a **portrait** frustum at the phone tier: 306 chunks and 261 k verts are *loaded*, but only **17 chunks / 37 k verts / 71 k tris are submitted**, with 2 chunks in the shadow pass and 9 mesh colliders — roughly 17 draw calls, comfortable inside 60 fps. Mesh memory 18.4 MB. The streamer's main thread runs **1.9 ms mean per chunk crossing**, with 3 of 23 crossings between 4 and 5.4 ms. That tail is the only 60 fps risk left and it is the one number the editor cannot settle (see the main-thread-steal trap, which faked a 9.3 ms mesh apply on the first run of this very probe). Knobs held in reserve if a phone says it matters: `lod0Radius`, `colliderRadius`, `MaxAppliesPerFrame`.
- 2026-08-23 — **World generation rebuilt from scratch** (`Assets/_Project/Scripts/Terrain/`). Seeded simplex fBm with hashed gradients and per-octave domain rotation (no FastNoiseLite: not Burst-able; no Mathf.PerlinNoise: unseedable, mirrored, ridged); mask applied *before* the altitude beach blend so shorelines are smooth whatever the terrace curve does; curve floor at +3 m (a floor below sea level made lakes). Chunks on a global integer lattice with a one-ring height border → seams bit-exact (0 mismatches across 554 streamed pairs); LOD boundaries use skirts, not stitching, so chunks never depend on a neighbour. Main-thread cost 0.26 ms mean / 5.7 ms worst while streaming 289 chunks. Ocean coupling replaced the 24-circle island array with a streamed 256² shore-height grid (GPU + Burst twins): 0.34 m RMS in deep water → 0.000 at the shoreline. `ArchipelagoGenerator` deleted; `TerrainWorldPopulator` discovers islands and repopulates resources/reefs/monsters/raiders. Probes: Noise/Height/Chunk/Stream/Crossing/Shore/World. Open: minimap draws islands as `MaxRadius` circles (huge for concave islands); device perf unmeasured; `IslandMeshBuilder` lingers only for the `Profile` struct.
- 2026-08-17 — Project created: Unity 6 (6000.4.3f1), URP 3D template, Input System package.
- 2026-08-17 — Platform locked: mobile, portrait, one-handed. Player is the commanding captain (no deck-walking avatar).
- 2026-08-17 — Sickness rate tied to sailing skill (roughness/smoothness metric), not just time — so the timer rewards engaging with sailing rather than punishing it.
- 2026-08-17 — Art: low poly stylized; whole-body acting instead of facial animation.
- 2026-08-17 — Combat deferred until sailing is proven fun.
- 2026-08-17 — Roster with persistent villagers/traits/breeding planned post-MVP; crew modeled as data (ScriptableObjects) from day one to allow it.
- 2026-08-17 — Development order: sailing first, work backward to base.
- 2026-08-17 — Device target: iPhone (iOS first). Default orientation set to Portrait.
- 2026-08-21 — **Dynamic bow lift: burial resisted by speed instead of by braking.** Asked for a boat that behaves "as close to a real boat as possible — without sinking", after the drag that keeps the deck dry kept killing the sailing. The old model resisted burial with **plow drag, a brake**, so going faster cost speed to stay dry; past ~28 m/s it actively backfired, because plow acts at the stem ahead of and below the CoM and its bow-down couple drove her nose under (at maxSpeed 40: **23.0% of a head-seas run with the deck under and 6.47 m over it with plow ON, against 5.8% with plow OFF**).
  - **The fix is `1/2 rho v^2 A Cl` on hull driven past its static waterline**, applied upward and weighted forward — full at the stem, zero at midships, none aft. It is exactly zero at rest, so the calm float equilibrium is untouched, and it grows with speed, which inverts the relationship: faster now means *drier*, not slower.
  - **Weighting it forward is load-bearing, not cosmetic.** Applied at every immersed probe it made things WORSE than no lift (deckOverMax −1.00 m → +0.75 m): with the stern deep and the bow up, lift at the sternpost pitches her nose down. Planing lift belongs where the hull meets oncoming flow.
  - **Result**: at maxSpeed 40, deck-under **23.0% → 0.0%** and rails **38.5% → 0.0%** — 40 m/s went from submarining to viable. At the tuned maxSpeed 21 the gate still passes (deckOverMax −0.14 m, rails 0%) and she is slightly quicker (meanSpeed 8.0 → 9.0). Plow's ceiling came down 6 → 4 m/s^2 on the strength of it.
  - **What this did NOT fix, stated plainly: the cost of hitting a wave is still ~60% of her speed.** Lift raised the ceiling on how fast she can go while staying dry; it did not buy enough headroom to also cap the brake. Four separate levers were measured and every one that helped the sailing flooded the deck: plow factor, plow ceiling, a total forward-deceleration ceiling (wave cost 60% → 36% and she held 25 m/s for 40 s straight — but 33.9% deck-under, worse than having no lift), and softening the surf climb brake (0.0% → 15.0%). Shedding way IS the anti-burial mechanism; bounding it is bounding that. `BuoyantBody.maxForwardDecel` is left wide at 100 as the documented dial this trade lives on.
  - **Process failure worth recording**: three tuning runs were wasted because saving `Sea.unity` for any reason snapshots *every* `BuoyantBody` field, so newly added ones froze at their first-ever value — the scene held `maxPlowDecel 6` and `dynamicLiftCoeff 0.04` while the source said 2.5 and 0.12, and the "differences" measured between those runs were pure noise. `TuneStormFeel` now calls `SceneDefaults.ResetToCodeDefaults` on the BuoyantBody so code defaults actually reach the game.
  - Also confirmed again: **severity-1.0 runs are chaotic.** The same config returned deckOverMax +0.46 m and +3.19 m on two runs. Only `poopDeckUnder` was stable enough to tune against.
- 2026-08-21 — **Second field pass: the spike, the camera and the stall.** Three reports from playing it, all fixed, plus one confirmation ("the boat dipped once or twice under the water but it's hugely improved").
  - **The water spikes behind the boat were a second GPU race, not the one fixed yesterday.** `Inject` ran one thread per IMPULSE, each doing read-modify-write over its own footprint — so two overlapping impulses raced and a texel could silently lose a whole impulse its neighbour received. That is a single-texel cliff, and the ±2 m clamp added yesterday bounded its height while doing nothing about its steepness. Inject now runs **one thread per destination texel**, looping the impulse list, so every texel has exactly one writer and overlapping impulses sum instead of racing. Two supporting fixes: impulses are injected as a **displacement** (same delta into both leapfrog levels) rather than a velocity kick, and the Step is **sub-stepped** to hold the Courant number at 0.5 — it had been clamped at 0.45 against a hard 2D limit of 0.5, i.e. riding the stability boundary every frame on both tiers. Measured neighbour gradient **0.392 → 0.073 m/texel**.
  - **The gate could not have caught it.** `RippleStressProbe` asserted `max|offset| < 3 m` while the Step kernel clamped every texel to ±2 m — the assertion was unfalsifiable and reported PASS throughout. It now scores **gradient** and clamp saturation, which is the shape a needle actually has. A clamp upstream of an assertion is a recorded trap now.
  - **The storm camera was 4 m above the ship.** `stormDrop` 9 and `stormPullIn` 6 are subtracted from the SCENE's framing (distance 20, height 13), not from the code defaults (25/19) — so a full storm put the lens at 14 m astern and **4 m up** while she was thrown around by 20 m seas. Halved to 4 and 3: now **17 m astern, 9 m up**. Measured camera-above-hull over a mountainous run: **4.2–13.0 m → 7.9–16.6 m**.
  - **The stall ("drops from 20 to 2") was plow drag still pulling after she had already stopped.** Plow exists to stop a hull CHARGING into a wall of water — a fast-ship problem — but keyed on bury depth alone it kept braking at 1 m/s, and in mountainous seas that pinned her at 1.4 m/s making 11 m in 40 s against 381 m with plow off. Added `plowSpeedFloor` (4.5 m/s, fading in over the same span again): a slam takes her from the top down to single figures and no further. Measured at severity 1.00: **10.4 m/s, 90% of target, 365 m against a 409 m no-plow ceiling** — plow now costs 10% where it cost 97%. Lively 94%, heavy 92%, slams 1.5/min taking ~5 m/s and recovering. Free-sailing mean speed in a mountainous sea **11.8 → 15.4 m/s**, and the deck gate came out *better* than before (deckOverMax **−1.00 m** against HEAD's −0.70).
  - **Raising `ShipMotor.acceleration` 2.6 → 3.6 was tried and reverted.** It looked like the obvious lever for "recovers too slowly" and it made things worse: she charges harder, buries deeper, saturates reserve, and the bow started going under (deckOverMax −0.24 → **+0.80 m**), while lively speed *fell* from 89% to 62%. More propulsion feeds the same bury-more-plow loop. The floor was the fix; the budget was not the problem.
  - **A measurement caution worth keeping**: at severity 1.0 the runs go chaotic — one StallProbe pass returned identical numbers with plow ON and OFF (2.9 m/s, 66 m both) where the next returned 2.9 vs 10.4. Check the OFF leg still differs from the ON leg before reading a high-severity number as signal.
- 2026-08-21 — **Plow drag was a handbrake, not a slam: onset and ceiling.** Field report: *"there is an issue now that is a hard stop for the ship when sailing, can we see why i suddenly lose speed so often"*. Yesterday's plow drag (the same day's earlier entry) cost **95% of her distance made good in ordinary sailing water** — measured by A/B over identical water: at severity 0.40 she made **32 m in 40 s with plow on against 636 m with it off**; at 0.75, 10 m against 289 m. Two faults, both geometric rather than tuning:
  - **The onset was 4 cm away from floating.** Reserve keys on "past full probe submersion", which the design comment claimed has "zero effect at the normal float equilibrium". Solving the static float says otherwise: a keel-line probe is ~96% submerged just floating, so the stem sits at depth 0.508 m against its 0.550 m threshold. Four centimetres. **Load-invariant** — `SeatOffset` moves the waterline too, so a laden ship is no closer — but any wave over the bow started the brake. Measured at anchor in a calm: reserve active on **38% of steps**.
  - **The brake had no ceiling while the sail is a rate-limited servo.** Propulsion can only ever apply `acceleration x mass` = **10.4 kN**; plow reached **101.7 kN (25.4 m/s^2, 9.8x the sail)**. Worse, it is applied at the stem — forward of and below the CoM — so it pitched her bow-down into more bury, into more plow: max pitch **60 degrees in a merely lively sea**, mean submersion driven 0.67 → 0.95. She was being held under by her own brake.
  - **Fix**: plow gets its own, later onset (`plowOnset` 0.6 m) decoupled from the lift's — the lift must still ramp from the first millimetre or the rails go under, that finding stands — and the whole plow set is clamped against one total (`maxPlowDecel` 6 m/s^2, 2.3x sail authority), scaled by a common factor in a second pass so the bow-down couple keeps its shape while being bounded.
  - **Result**: severity 0.40, **594 m against a 666 m no-plow ceiling (89% of target speed)**; severity 0.75, **436 m against 517 m (85%)**. Distance made good up **18x** and **44x**. Slams survive as events — 3-4.5 stalls/min, ~4 m/s off the top, most recovered — which is the head-seas skill trade §6 wants. Calm-water plow is 0.23 kN mean against 10.4 kN of sail. BuryProbe still passes (poopDeckUnder 0%, deckOverMax **-0.24 m**, rails 0%, meanSpeed 7.8 vs 7.7 at HEAD), and the storm half of BuoyProbe improved (roll 10.0-12.2 vs 11.6-12.0).
  - **A tried-and-reverted idea worth recording**: gating plow on vertical closing speed (fire on the slam, release as she rises). It read beautifully in lively water and was wrong — it switched the term off almost entirely (0.0 kN through a whole storm run), she charged the faces at 10 m/s and pitchpoled. Plow is what stops that. The physical term is about driving *horizontally* into a wall of water, which the forward-velocity factor already carries.
  - **And a measurement that lied.** BuoyProbe FAILed the fix on calm-drop settle time (5.93 s and never-settled against 2.49 s), which looked like a pogo regression — except a vertical drop has no forward speed and plow is forward-only. It was the probe: "|vy| < 0.12 m/s for 0.5 s" on a live calm sea measures whether the water happens to go flat, and the *unchanged* HEAD build returned 1.04 s, 2.49 s, 5.93 s and never across runs. Scored properly — three drops from pinned phases, on draft rather than absolute height — the two builds are **identical** (overshoot 2.16/1.96/1.95 vs 2.14/1.98/1.95). BuoyProbe's drop gate was rebuilt on overshoot + late ringing RMS, which repeat to ±0.02 m where settle time ranged over 8 s.
- 2026-08-21 — **The deck stays dry: reserve buoyancy + plow drag.** Field bug: in mountainous seas the hull buried to the sails (measured 9 m of water over the bow deck, rails under 53% of a head-seas run). Root cause was force saturation — past full probe submersion the old model added ZERO extra lift, and no plausible lift beats 4 t of way charging a crest at 10 m/s. Two coupled changes in `BuoyantBody`: (a) **reserve buoyancy** — past full probe submersion, lift keeps growing with depth (the sealed hull above the waterline), linear from the onset (that shallow band raises her storm ride height ~0.5 m — the freeboard that actually keeps rails dry; every onset softening tried re-wet them), with lift+damping bled on upward relative motion so the spring can't pogo (symmetric lift tripled the calm 2 m drop settle to 6–9 s); (b) **plow drag** — forward drag factor scales with reserve, so slamming a wall of water sheds way (~2 m/s in storm head seas), and once the way is gone the lift wins. Gates: BuryProbe (phase-pinned severity 1.0 head seas) poop deck under 0%, deckOverMax −0.78 m, rails 0%; BuoyProbe storm roll 11.6° (was 19.8°), settle 2.49 s. The slam-slowdown also feeds the smoothness/sickness loop: charging head seas now costs speed, exactly the skill trade §6 wants.
- 2026-08-21 — **Ripple-sim race fixed.** The wake/splash field intermittently exploded into stretched vertical spikes behind the ship: the leapfrog `Step` kernel updated `Curr` in place, racing neighbour reads against writes across GPU threads. Now proper ping-pong across three rotating buffers, plus a ±2 m clamp and non-finite guard as a backstop (the field's R channel is added raw to ocean vertex height, so one bad texel was a permanent needle). WakeProbe and a new RippleStressProbe (splash spam + wake under storm, GPU readback bounded) both pass.
- 2026-08-20 — **The ocean is a Sea-of-Thieves-class FFT simulation and the ship is a rigidbody.** Full replacement of the Gerstner stack (see the ocean rewrite plan). What changed and why:
  - **Simulation**: three band-limited FFT cascades (512/128/32 m patches, N=256/128 by quality tier) of JONSWAP × Hasselmann directional spreading, evolved on GPU compute. Spectrum validated against oceanography: Hs within 1.1% and Tp within 5% of the analytic values for the canonical calm/normal/storm (wind, fetch) triples. Two non-obvious correctness requirements: spectral bins are energy-integrated (4×4 supersample — a calm sea's peak is narrower than one k-bin), and seed noise is unit phasors, not complex gaussians (per-band energy lives in ~10–30 bins, so gaussian amplitude draws swung realized Hs ±25% per seed).
  - **One surface for eyes and physics** (the governing rule): physics reads the GPU result via a triple-buffered AsyncGPUReadback ring and a Burst sampler with Newton displacement inversion. Measured max divergence between the rendered surface and the sampler: **0.23 cm** in a λ=1.2 storm (gate was 5 cm). 1000-query batch: 0.15 ms. There is no second implementation of the wave math anywhere.
  - **The ship is a Rigidbody** on 12 buoyancy probes: heave/pitch/roll/surf emerge from the water. ShipMotor keeps its entire public API and its tuned control laws (target-speed sail servo, keel grip, yaw-rate steering) applied as forces; hard attitude clamps became soft spring-torque limits; knockdown/recoil are torques. Camera-clamp incidents in the western deep: **93% of frames → 0%**. Two hydrodynamic traps cost a day each in miniature: drag anisotropy must use world-vertical + flattened hull axes (ship-frame vertical turns pitch into a diving plane that sucks the hull under at speed), and orbital velocity from readback finite-differences must be clamped (a spectrum rebuild between slots reads as ~200 m/s of water motion and catapults the hull).
  - **Weather is three spectrum-parameter assets** (Calm/Normal/Stormy) blended by lerping parameters with h0 rebuilt at 4 Hz — never cross-fading two sims. Sailing west ramps the target to storm (region field); the calm shelf near home is a post-FFT amplitude envelope evaluated identically in HLSL and C# (the divergence probe gates both together). Measured calm→storm: Hs 0.66→6.15 m over 45 s, max 1-s step 5.7% of range.
  - **Cut with the old stack**: swell fronts, mountain seas, currents, and gust patches (and their UI touchpoints). Regional sea state survives as envelope + weather-target. Wind-as-physics stays removed. StormSpray ported; WakeTexture replaced by a ripple-sim API (stub, real sim next).
  - **Android is Vulkan-only** now: the simulation is compute-shader based and GLES3.0 has no compute.
  - Still to come from the plan: real surface shading (scattering/backlit crests), Jacobian+intersection foam, the ripple sim, and the perf/tier pass. The interim surface uses the debug shader — the sea is uglier than before on purpose until the shading milestone lands.
  - Feedback: *"sometimes my boat is all the way over the water and sometimes its all the way under ... it feels like the boat is almost following a different set of waves ... this issue happens in the choppy waters."* That description was an exact statement of the cause and it took three wrong diagnoses to hear it.
  - **`ShipMotor` low-passed the hull's height:** `pos.y = Lerp(pos.y, hCenter - SinkDepth, 1-exp(-verticalResponse·dt))`, with `verticalResponse` 5.5 — a 0.18s time constant. A first-order lag's error is proportional to how fast the surface is MOVING, and that went up an order of magnitude in one step: the deep got three times taller and she crosses it at 25 m/s, so the water under her now runs at up to **19 m/s vertically** where it used to manage about one. A lag also delays and attenuates every frequency by a different amount, so she traced a *genuinely different curve* from the water rather than a delayed copy of it — which is why it read as a different set of waves, and why it was worst in the choppiest water.
  - **Measured before:** seating error **−2.23 m to +3.39 m, RMS 1.60 m**, against 1.3m of freeboard to the waist. So the deck went under, and on the way back up she hung in the air.
  - **Fix: ease then CLAMP.** She may lag the surface by at most `maxSeatError` (0.30m), so she can never be swallowed however fast the sea moves, while the easing still softens calm water. `hCenter` is already the mean of three float points along the hull — that was always the real smoothing, and the lag was doing the same job a second time in the one place that guarantees hull and water disagree. The cap is in metres rather than a fraction of wave height because what it has to stay inside is the BOAT's freeboard, not the sea. **After: −0.64 m to +0.71 m, RMS 0.32 m** — five times tighter.
  - **Three wrong diagnoses first,** each killed by a measurement: the inverse-displacement solver (max error 0.02m against a converged solution), the camera's surface clamp (fires 5% of frames, 1.6m worst shove), and the storm fog hiding long waves (reshot wide open — unchanged). Worth recording that **the camera-clamp check was circular** — it measured the camera against the same `SampleHeight` the clamp itself uses, so it could never have revealed a disagreement with what is drawn.
  - Also done while in here: the ocean is **`Cull Off`** with the normal flipped on back faces, so a lens that does dip under the surface sees water rather than a hole through to the sky dome; and the **swell front is now included in `sumSteepness`**, which the anti-fold clamp had been ignoring — a front standing over the ship could push the surface past a fold limit the clamp believed it was holding.

- 2026-08-20 — **UNRESOLVED: the near/far brightness step in the storm.** Distinct from the seating bug above, and still open.
  - A hard, near-horizontal edge across the frame: measured **43.6% grey above it against 8.8% below**, with the near water a flat 8.7% over a large area. It reads as a waterline with the ship beneath it, which is most of why the sinking looked worse than it was.
  - **Proved it IS the ocean**, not a hole: tinting the water magenta filled the region. So there is no missing geometry and no culling fault.
  - Physically the step is real — distant water is replaced wholesale by an unlit fog colour while near water is lit and at normal incidence, so genuinely dark. Attempts that did NOT close it: lifting `_StormDeep` nearly 4× (0.058 → 0.200), raising `_SkySoft` 0.30 → 0.65, flattening the fresnel exponent from 3.0 to `lerp(3.0, 1.4, storm)`, raising the storm ambient floor, and widening the fog ramp (reverted — it was speculative and changed nothing).
  - **The method ran out before the bug did.** Controlled term-by-term tints with the ship pinned still are not repeatable, because the waves keep moving between shots: "far water" alone read 50.8%, 31.5%, 24.5% and 28.1% across four frames of the same test. Tint comparisons on a moving sea cannot resolve this.
  - **Next step is a repeatable harness, not another guess:** pin the sea state *and* the wave phase (freeze `Time.time` for the field, or step it manually) so two tinted frames are pixel-comparable, then tint one term at a time. Signals so far point at the foam term — with `_StormCrest` tinted blue the near water came back B=163.8 against R=66.5 — but that is one unrepeatable frame and is not yet evidence.

- 2026-08-20 — **The western deep became an epic ocean, and four things had to be fixed to let it.**
  - Feedback: *"sometimes my boat is all the way over the water and sometimes its all the way under. i like the randomness but its still very flat. do you understand what im asking for ... waves sizes of mountains."*
  - **Three wrong diagnoses, each killed by a measurement rather than by argument.** (1) The hull's seating solver — `SampleHeight` inverts the Gerstner displacement by undamped fixed-point iteration, which only converges under total steepness 1, and `Displace` multiplies by a RegionScale of 1.5 out west. Measured against a converged damped solution: **max error 0.02m**, hull within ±1m of the true surface. Not it. (2) The chase camera's "never go under" clamp, which samples the sea at the CAMERA's position ~20m astern — a different wave from the one the hull is on. Measured: **fires on 5% of frames, worst shove 1.6m**. Not it. (3) My own storm fog at 128–547m hiding waves 200–250m long. Shot at 400–2000m: **still flat**. Not it.
  - **It was simply that the waves were not tall for their length.** Storm waves ran `amp/L` around **0.011** — a 0.6° face. No amount of amplitude spread over 250m reads as anything. `stormAmplitude` 12.7m → **40m**, wavelengths 70–260 → 120–340m so they stay broad. Storm trains are gated by `StormAmount01`, which is zero on the home shelf, so the village's water is untouched — this is literally "a section of the sea".
  - **Measured: hull heave 40m at 1500m west** (y from −32.5 to 7.7), against 15.2m before.
  - **`choppiness` became self-limiting, and that is what makes the size safe.** A Gerstner surface folds through itself when the HORIZONTAL steepness sums past 1 — and only the horizontal part; vertical height is not constrained by it at all. So chop is now clamped automatically to hold `chop × totalSteepness × farScale × seaState` under `foldLimit` (0.85), computed against the deepest water and uploaded as one uniform so CPU and GPU cannot disagree. It settled at **0.523**. Amplitude can now be cranked freely without the sea ever turning inside out.
  - **The near water rendered BLACK.** The storm sky pass mixed sky colour in on fresnel alone, which vanishes at normal incidence — fine when every wave was a grazing plane, useless when a steep sea shows the camera its faces. Added `_SkySoft`, an angle-independent scatter term that grows with the storm, lifted `_StormDeep`, and raised the storm ambient floor 0.19 → 0.28.
  - **A material asset snapshots a shader property's default when the property is created, and changing that default later does NOT reach it.** Verified by reading it back at runtime: `_StormDeep` still returned (0.020, 0.045, 0.055) long after the shader said (0.058, 0.086, 0.098). The `.mat` text did not even list the property. This is the scene serialization trap wearing a different hat; `SetupStormSky` now pushes the ocean palette explicitly.
  - **The decoration was all built for a calm sea, and a big one exposed it.** `SeaStreaks` and `GustVisuals` place untextured `PrimitiveType.Quad`s pinned to `Quaternion.Euler(90,0,0)` — dead horizontal. On a 40m face they cut straight through the surface and showed as grey plates. Both now take the wave normal by central difference and lie ON the surface, and use the shared soft blob. `SpeedJuice`'s foam was deliberately opaque and untextured so it would "catch the sun and read as thrown water with mass" — true under a bright sky, and wet cardboard under a storm lid at a third sun. It is alpha-clipped to a disc now.
  - **`SpeedJuice`'s impact thresholds were absolutes** (`beamImpactThreshold` 1.5 m/s²), so in the new sea every frame was a hard knock and the bursts became the weather. They now scale with `SeaSeverity01` — the same lesson the spindrift crest threshold taught, which is that a threshold into the sea has to be relative to the sea.
  - `OceanRenderer`'s mesh bounds were 80m tall. Displacement happens in the vertex shader, so Unity culls against those bounds and never sees where the surface actually went — the whole ocean would have popped out of frame. Raised to 600m; bounds are free.

- 2026-08-20 — **The sea became a noise field instead of a row of waves.**
  - Feedback: *"the top of the waves are still very sharp and very unison ... above all, the larger a wave, the wider it needs to be ... more like a noise map than a row of waves going by."* Three separate complaints, and all three measured true before anything changed.
  - **Row-ness**, a new metric: mean surface slope walking ACROSS the dominant direction divided by the same walking ALONG it. A pure wave train is flat across itself, so 1.0 is an isotropic field and 0 is corrugated iron. **Measured before: 0.55 on the home shelf, 0.35 at 600m west, 0.76 inside the storm.** The six storm cross-trains had fixed the storm and left everywhere else as rows.
  - **Cause, exactly as the old code comment admitted:** directional spread was `peakL / wavelength`, which pinned the LONG waves — the ones that carry the sea's shape — to about ±9° of the wind. The four longest base waves measured −1°, 4°, 11° and −1°. **There was no longer any reason for it:** wind stopped being physics in `3fd9309` and nothing in the gameplay reads the spectrum's directions. The arc opened right out (±80° for the longest, ±125° for the shortest).
  - **"The larger a wave, the wider it needs to be" is one identity in Gerstner:** amplitude = steepness × wavelength / 2π. So amplitude now rises in proportion to wavelength (`ampPower` 1), which makes every wave in the set equally steep and means a tall crest can only ever be a long one. The old spectrum did the opposite — it weighted steepness by a log-normal energy curve around a 45m peak, which decoupled amplitude from wavelength: **the tallest base wave was 71m and the 170m wave was the smallest in the set at 0.17m.** `amp/L` spanned 17× across the spectrum; it now spans 1.5×.
  - **`totalSteepness` was replaced by `baseAmplitude` in metres**, because it conflated two things that have to be separated: how TALL the sea is and how SHARP it is. Same for `stormSteepness` → `stormAmplitude`. That also made size independent of the seed — changing the number of random draws in the base loop had silently shrunk the storm from 12.7m of amplitude to 9.9m.
  - **Sharpness got its own dial.** Gerstner's cusped crest comes from the HORIZONTAL part of the displacement pinching the tops together, so a `choppiness` factor (0.72) scales only that. Crests round off at unchanged wave height, and it buys headroom under the self-intersection limit. Applied in four places that must agree: `Displace`, `JacobianAt`, and the shader's `WaveDisplacement` and `BreakingAmount`. **Crest skew 0.34 → 0.06.**
  - **Directions are stratified in mirrored pairs, biggest wave first.** Covering the strata is not enough on its own: amplitude now rises with wavelength, so a handful of waves decide everything, and a plain shuffle that put three of the four longest on one side just rebuilt rows on a new bearing — measured 1.88 that way round, as directional as the 0.55 it replaced, only rotated. Pairing the biggest waves into mirrored strata makes the dominant components cancel each other's bearing by construction.
  - **The storm trains stopped being *cross* trains.** Their whole justification was crossing a base spectrum that ran in rows; once it did not, six big waves all aimed 58–124° off the wind simply became the new dominant axis (row-ness 1.87 in the storm against 1.09 on the shelf, from the same base spectrum plus those). They now spread across the fan like everything else and cross each other by covering it.
  - **Result: row-ness 1.07 on the home shelf, 1.07 at 600m west, 1.07 in the full storm.** The agreement across three places with wildly different wave heights is the cross-check — it is now a property of the spectrum rather than of where you stand.
  - **Two instrument bugs found and fixed before any tuning was done on their output.** Row-ness walked a single 400m line, which is one realisation of a 20-wave field and not an estimate of it: it reported 2.13 on the home shelf against 0.99 at 600m west from very nearly the same spectrum. It now averages 32 parallel lines. And the skew grid sampled 400m at 48×48 — **8.3m spacing, which cannot resolve a 10m wave** — while using `SampleHeightFast`, whose one-iteration error is signed differently at crests than troughs. It was reporting *negative* skew, a sea with sharper troughs than crests, which Gerstner cannot produce. Now 160×160 over 300m using the 3-iteration `SampleHeight`.
  - **The cost, measured honestly.** A spread sea does not stack its peaks the way a marching one does. With the sea state **pinned** (the probe now does this — SeaState01 drifts 0.14–1.15 over minutes and scales every amplitude, and an unpinned run produced 23.5m and 8.4m from the same build), heave at 1600m west is **15.2m against the old 23.5m, pitch 16° → 9°, and roll 10° → 18°.** She pitches less and rolls considerably more, which is what a confused sea does to a hull. Amplitude was deliberately left alone so that only the shape changed; `baseAmplitude` and `stormAmplitude` are single metre values if the size wants raising.
  - `StormSpray`'s crest threshold needed no retuning at all — being a fraction of the sea's own measured spread, it re-calibrated itself to the new spectrum (mean −0.9m, crest 6.6m, threshold 2.3m).

- 2026-08-20 — **Storm sky and light: the picture catches up with the simulation.**
  - The sea had been mountainous for two commits and still looked like a nice day with unusually large water. A storm is mostly a **lighting state**, so one number now drives all of it: `SkyDirector.Storminess01`, taken from `WaveField.StormAmount01` at the ship and lifted by a swell front standing over you, eased in over a few seconds. Sky dome, sun, ambient, fog, the ocean's own palette, the spray and the camera framing all read that one value, so they cannot disagree with each other.
  - **Procedural sky (`SeaSick/Sky`), not an imported cubemap.** A gradient dome with a sun disc, a cloud deck projected onto a plane overhead (so it compresses into a bank at the skyline the way real cloud does), and ragged low scud tearing past three times faster once it is genuinely blowing. Built now rather than at milestone 3 because the water has to match the sky — a static cubemap would leave the sea the same blue under a black lid. `TimeOfDay01` slots into the same structure later.
  - **Measured, home vs 1500m west:** storminess 0.04 → 1.00, overcast 0.14 → 0.90, scud 0.93, sun 1.12 → 0.43, fog 577–1454m → **128–547m**. Visibility collapsing is most of the effect, and it is the same lever that will make night bite.
  - **Ambient is Trilight, driven by hand.** Ambient from a custom procedural skybox needs `DynamicGI.UpdateEnvironment` every frame — milliseconds on a phone. Three colours set directly cost nothing and are exact.
  - **The ocean grades by the per-vertex storm term, not by swapping material colours**, so it stays *positional*: from the home shelf you can see the dark water lying out west. Grazing angles also mix in `_SS_SkyHorizon`, so the sea can never stay blue under a black sky. Calm foam is cyan-white, which read as a lit ring around the hull under a storm — storm foam goes neutral.
  - **The camera was the real culprit, and it was not the tilt.** The plan blamed "19m up / 41° down"; the rig was actually at 22°, and the fault was that `shipFlat` forced y to 0 — **the camera was pinned to mean sea level and never followed the ship vertically at all**. In a storm that throws her 23.5m, the horizon stayed nailed and the *boat* bobbed through the frame, which is exactly backwards. It now low-passes the ship's height (rides the swell, ignores the chop) and drops 9m closer to the water as the sea builds.
  - **Measured, same spot, back to back:** ship-in-frame spread **0.0218 → 0.0126 of screen height per metre of heave (42% steadier)**, and the lens sits **8.2m above her instead of 18.1m**. *Normalising by heave was necessary* — the sea grew 3× between the first pair of windows and made the raw spreads uncomparable. The same normalised figures came back from a 90-sample run at 10fps and a 1664-sample run at ~185fps, which is a useful check that the metric is frame-rate independent.
  - **Heave is added AFTER the framing lerp, not folded into its target.** Through the lerp it becomes a second low pass stacked on the one that produced it and the rig lags the very swell it is meant to ride: that alone was the difference between 26% and 42%.
  - **Spindrift is emitted at measured crests**, with the threshold set as a *fraction of the sea's own spread* tracked across frames — no wave-spectrum API to keep in step with, and it self-calibrates to any sea state and region scale.
  - **Three bugs, all found by measuring rather than looking:**
    - The first crest threshold was an absolute 6.5m above the local mean and produced **exactly one spindrift particle alive in a full storm** — 6.5m was very nearly the ceiling of a sea that turned out to be heaving 12.4m peak to peak, not the 23.5m the storm reaches at its worst. *Tuning values are balanced against each other, never absolute.*
    - The emission budget **banked unspent allowance without a ceiling**. Sustained output was correct at 110/s, but the accumulator reached 178 particles of credit during a quiet stretch and dumped it the moment the crests returned — measured at **681/s against a 110/s budget**, and frame-rate dependent (the dump rate is `samplesPerFrame × fps`) which would have behaved differently on a phone. Unspent budget must evaporate.
    - Untextured particle quads have a hard rim: the first storm shot was full of grey **rectangles** sliding over the sea. Same mistake the bow foam made before it was turned solid. Both systems now use a soft radial blob generated in code, and spindrift is narrow and stretched along its own velocity — the first pass was 3m wide and read as cotton balls resting on the water.
  - **`SetupStormSky` resets components to their C# defaults on every run** (copying the serialised values off a freshly constructed instance). This is the fourth time the serialization trap has cost time; re-running the setup is now the fix rather than hand-editing the `.unity` file.

- 2026-08-20 — **Mountain seas built.**
  - A wall of water ~96m tall and 30m thick, running across the western deep. **Not a wave in the wave field**: Gerstner self-intersects long before that steepness, and at five times the ship's length tall and narrower than it is high this is a moving cliff, not water you ride. It is an obstacle with a procedural low-poly mesh — which is precisely what lets it have rules of its own.
  - **It mauls, it never kills.** Everything scales off one number: `beam = sin(angle between the bow and the oncoming wall)`. Bow-on *or* stern-on is survivable — what ruins you is presenting the whole length of the hull. Measured across four angles, all survived: bilge 0.16 → 0.58, way scrubbed to 6.1 → 3.4 m/s, and **deck cargo torn off only when caught across it** (20 held → 10 at 60°, → 8 at 90°).
  - **Roams rather than ambushes.** `MountainSeaDirector` launches them only in the western sector beyond 700m from home, on a heading that *crosses* the player rather than chasing, from 900m out — far enough that the silhouette is on the horizon well before it arrives. Being caught is a watch you failed to keep, not something the game did to you.
  - **The warning is an instrument, not a banner:** a plate under the compass with the countdown and *"she's lying across it"* / *"bow into it"*, plus a red pip on the compass for its bearing. It never says *turn left* — reading the angle is the skill.
  - **`HullIntegrity.TakeShot`'s `amount` is a MULTIPLIER on `damagePerShot`, not a fraction.** Passing 0.30 there costs ~2.5% hull, not 30% — which is why the first run showed a mountain sea scratching the paint (hull 0.97–0.99 at every angle). Added `Batter(point, fraction)` for anything that isn't a cannonball.
  - **Roll is a damped spring**, so sampling it a second after impact reads it already settled. But following the peak showed the real problem: **`AddRecoilRoll` cannot lay a ship over.** Its spring peaks around 0.27s and the rotation `Slerp` (`angularResponse` 2.5) has a ~0.4s time constant, so it eats the peak before the transform ever sees it — and the target is clamped to ±26° regardless. Measured: a mountain sea managed **7°**, the same reason a full broadside only heels her ~5°.
  - **Added `ShipMotor.Knockdown(degrees, seconds)`**, applied *after* the roll clamp and decaying along a sine so she goes over hard and rights herself gradually. Allowed out to ±78°, so beam-on genuinely puts the rail under. `AddRecoilRoll` stays what it is — a nudge for gunfire, not a knockdown.

- 2026-08-19 — **Compass, speed, and the end of banner UI.**
  - Reported as: *"no ui as banner modes across the screen it breaks immersion."* `UITheme.Banner` was **deleted outright** so nothing can reach for it again. The crew-bailing banner was simply redundant (the pips, the water panel and five people visibly leaving the guns already say it); the swell warning became a small plate tucked under the compass.
  - **The bearing tape became a real compass**, carrying N/NE/E/SE/S/SW/W/NW. This is load-bearing now that the world is laid out by direction — "which way am I pointing" is the same question as "what am I sailing into".
  - **Speed added** to the bottom-left cluster, and an overloaded hold reads `26/24+` rather than a number past the line.
  - **Three passes to get the compass legible**, each caught by screenshotting the HUD rather than reasoning about it: graduations were running through the letters; then the full-height bow marker sat on whichever cardinal was dead ahead, and the home pip on whichever was abeam; and the slow-arc shading filled the tape as a grey slab. Fixed by construction rather than by tuning fractions — **ticks and pips live in the lower half only, the bow marker became a lubber line *below* the bar, and the slow arc became an underline.** Nothing can cross a letter now regardless of screen size.
  - **`ScreenCapture.CaptureScreenshot` is the only way to review this**: rendering a camera to a RenderTexture omits IMGUI entirely, and `capture_scene_object` shoots the scene view. `HudShot` in `Dev/Editor` does the warp, load and capture.
  - Caveat: the editor Game view was **landscape** while the target is portrait, and `UITheme.Unit` scales off the short edge — so some cramping in these shots is an aspect artifact, not a layout bug. Worth a portrait pass on device.
- 2026-08-19 — **Wind removed as physics; the sea itself became the only thing a heading argues with.**
  - Reported as: wind *"punishes the player for wanting to explore… basically impossible to go in different directions without getting hard stuck."*
  - **The polar was not the culprit** — its floor was already 0.55, so dead upwind was slow, never a wall. The real problem was **multiplicative stacking**: `polar × wind strength × cargo × hull damage` put an upwind leg in light air with a full hold at roughly a quarter of top speed, while the UI shouted `IN IRONS`, painted a red no-go band on the nav tape and turned the wind arrow red — **the game told the player they were stuck at the exact moment they were merely slow.** Fourth instance of the standing lesson that Kevin's feel complaints are rarely the system he names; this time the honest answer was still to remove it, because he'd hit it twice across sessions.
  - **Gone:** `SailPolar`, `NoGoDegrees`, `Luff01`, `InIrons`, `PolarEfficiency`, `WindAngleDeg`, `PointOfSailName`, `BestUpwindAngle`, the autopilot's beating/tacking, and `WindStrength` in the speed formula.
  - **Now:** `speed = maxSpeed × sailPower × (1 − headSeaPenalty × headSea × severity)`. In calm water severity is ~0, so **every heading is equally fast and exploring costs nothing**. Verified over 8 headings in a 0.58 sea: 1.00 resistance from the beam aft, 0.69 dead into it (23.0 → 14.6 m/s). Slow, never blocked.
  - **Raiders sail the same sea**, through the shared `ShipMotor.SeaResistanceAt`, so there is one formula rather than two that drift apart. They no longer tack.
  - **Wind survives as weather, not physics** — gusts, streaks, swell fronts and wave direction all stay. Instruments were repointed rather than deleted: the point-of-sail panel reads sea state and how much is on the bow, the nav tape shades where the seas run instead of a forbidden zone, and the yards shake when driving into heavy water rather than when pinching.
- 2026-08-19 — **Sea state is regional: distance is the difficulty curve, made physical.**
  - A calm shelf out to 260m (scale 0.35), ramping to 1150m and beyond (scale 1.50), multiplying the existing drifting `SeaState01`. Measured at mid-drift: **~1.1m wave range on the home shelf, 2.8m at 700m, 4.5m at the edge**; dead-into resistance 0.99 near home versus 0.55 out deep. Because the global drift still multiplies on top, the outer waters are **not always** mountainous — a calm window out there is a real opportunity, and a squall on the shelf is still possible.
  - **`WaveField.RegionScale` and `RegionScale()` in `Ocean.shader` are two implementations of one formula and must stay identical**, exactly like `ShoreAttenuation` before them — the ship floats on the C# one and the player looks at the GPU one. Verified by parking the hull at 150m and at 1150m and confirming it sits correctly in the surface at both, with the horizon visibly flat inshore and broken up in the deep.
- 2026-08-19 — **Sickness reworked: the meter only goes up, and mutiny is gone.**
  - **The meter is monotonic at sea.** 0 → 100, and nothing afloat brings it down. Puking used to shave 0.25 off it, which made the whole system a sawtooth: letting the crew heave literally bought off the mutiny, so the "clock" could be paid down with the thing that was supposed to be its symptom.
  - **Puking is now the tax, not the relief.** Past ~80% (offset per person by `ironStomach` plus a name-seeded jitter, so five people don't hit the rail on the same frame) they leave their post for the rail. Episodes lengthen 3.5s → 9s and close up 30s → 6s as the meter climbs, until at 100% they never come back — `State.Broken`, slumped over the rail, grey. **"A broken crew stops working" is a duty cycle, not a rule at 100%**, so there is no cliff anywhere on the curve.
  - **Mutiny deleted** (`MutinyController`, `Anger01`, `SailCap`, `VoyageManager.DitchCargo`). It was the *only* wire from sickness to the ship — cannons fired straight off player input and sails were set directly by the player — so removing it meant building the layer that should have been there.
  - **Crew are labour now** (`CrewRoster`). Every ship system asks it before acting: `Available` (at their post, not at the rail, not ashore) and `WorkRate01` (1 → 0.5 across the meter). Time lost to the rail and the slowness of queasy hands combine into one `Labour01` number.
    - **Sail is an order, not a setting.** `HelmInput` writes `ShipMotor.SailOrder`; the crew move `SailSetting` toward it at `0.55 × Labour01` per second. Canvas therefore *stays where it was last set* when nobody can work.
    - **Oars scale with `Labour01`** — the guaranteed way home stops being guaranteed exactly when the crew break, which is the point of having it.
    - **Guns are worked by named people.** One hand per gun in crew order (Bo→port fore, Mara→port aft, Pip→stbd fore, Tam→stbd aft; Ola is the spare/sail hand). `Cannon` reload is now a serviced countdown (`ReloadScale`, `Manned`) rather than an absolute `readyAt`, so a gunner at the rail leaves a half-rammed charge sitting there. `CannonBattery.PostGunCrews` stands each gunner at their own gun, **derived from the live gun transforms** rather than baked into the scene, so the deck can't drift out of sync with the battery. Their `railLocal` is their own gunport. *(2026-10-03: the named hand is now the gun's HOME hand; in a fight gunners walk to the engaged side's empty guns — see that entry.)*
  - **The captain always keeps the tiller.** Verified with every hand at 1.0: guns 0/2 both sides, oar power 0.00, sail refuses to move — and the ship still makes **14.5 m/s** and still steers, on `steerageWay` alone if it has to. **There is no state this system can reach that strands the player**, which was the one thing that had to be true before mutiny could be removed.
  - **Rates halved** (`baseRate` 0.005→0.002, `roughnessRate` 0.12→0.06, `rowingStrain` 2.1→1.7). Removing puke relief silently *tripled* the effective rate, because the old numbers were balanced against a meter that could be paid down. Measured targets: 0.12 roughness → first puke 5.2 min; 0.25 → 2.6 min; 0.53 (caught in the swell) → 47 s.
  - **Shore leave is a full reset to zero, at a rate** (`shoreRecoveryRate` 0.045/s, the old 0.2 floor removed) — so a real harvest settles a crew completely and a touch-and-go costs the ~22 s it takes. **This makes sickness a per-*leg* clock rather than a per-voyage one**: with islands 280–1150 m out, only the long outer runs can break anyone, which is "distance is the difficulty curve" doing the work it was always supposed to.
  - **`crewShock` 0.12→0.06 and `shotShock` 0.10→0.05.** Both were tuned against a recoverable meter; left alone, ~12 hits to wreck the hull would have finished the crew outright before the timber. Caught by a probe that showed sickness jumping +0.146 in 3s under fire.
  - **The probe trap bit again, fourth time.** The first trim measurement drove `motor.SailOrder` and reported 0.1 s trims — `HelmInput` reasserts `SailOrder` from its own `sailStep` every frame. Drive the owner's state, never the state it owns.
  - **Still open:** whether a fully-broken crew needs a real fail state or whether "slow and defenceless" is punishment enough — deliberately deferred until it's been played. `ShipMotor.AutopilotTarget` and its hard-won upwind tacking are now unused; kept as the hook for a future "set course home" order.
- 2026-08-18 — **Cannons (first pass) + crew of five.**
  - **Four guns, two a side**, one forward and one aft of the mast at x ±1.45, z +4.8 / −1.2, deck height 2.05. First attempt at the full beam (±2.0) buried the carriages — **the hull tumbles home above the waterline**, so guns have to sit inboard with only the muzzle over the rail.
  - **Aiming is the tiller.** You steer to bring a side to bear and fire that broadside (`Q` / `E`, or the two buttons bottom-left showing loaded guns and a reload bar). This deliberately keeps combat *inside* the sailing rather than competing for the player's thumb.
  - Firing gives recoil, muzzle smoke, and a **ballistic `CannonBall`** that arcs, hits the sea, throws a splash and **stamps the wake buffer** — the mark stays on the water afterwards, which is exactly what that buffer was built to make free.
  - **Crew of five** (Bo, Mara, Pip, Tam, Ola), stations laid out clear of the gun positions.
  - **The supplied `player_ship_cannon.fbx` is still an empty 4KB file** and was never re-exported, so the guns are built from primitives like the islands, props and cargo. `Cannon.Build()` is the only place to change when a real mesh arrives.
  - **Not built yet:** nothing to shoot at. Enemy ships / sea monsters, damage on hit, and fleeing-vs-fighting are all still open — see the cannons design discussion earlier in this log.
- 2026-08-19 — **Lock range tightened, and a serialization trap worth remembering.**
  - **Break range 260 → 200m, acquire 170 → 150m.** A break at 260m is nearly four times the guns' 67m reach, so the lock was holding — and paying its camera-swing cost — long after the fight was over. It read as having no range limit at all.
  - **A one-second grace** before releasing, so a single wave-driven metre over the line does not drop the lock, and the brackets **fade toward amber over the last quarter of the range** with the distance shown. Losing a lock should be something you watch coming, not something that happens to you.
  - **The trap:** changing a `[SerializeField]` default does **nothing** to a component already in a scene. Unity wrote 260 into `Sea.unity` when `CombatLock` was first added, and that value wins over the C# initializer forever after. The probe caught it only because it read the field back through reflection instead of trusting the source. **Any tuning change to a scene-authored component needs a `SerializedObject` pass** — and note this does not apply to `Cannon` or `EnemyShip`, which are `AddComponent`-ed at runtime and therefore always take the code defaults.
  - Verified: locks at 80m, holds just inside the limit, survives a momentary blip past it, releases when genuinely far.
- 2026-08-19 — **Camera, third pass: cruise widens, combat locks on.**
  - **The widen-only camera did not survive contact either.** Widening helps you see, but it does nothing about *losing* the enemy, which is the actual problem in a fight. Kept the zoom-out and moved it to where it belongs.
  - **Cruise framing.** Hold a heading above 50% of top speed for six seconds and the view eases back 10m, up 4.5m, and looks 5m *further* ahead. Eased in over ~3s on purpose: you should notice the sea got bigger, not the camera moving. Drops instantly when you slow, or the moment a lock is taken.
  - **Lock-on (space).** Locks the nearest enemy within 170m, breaks at 260m — hysteresis, or the lock drops every time they open the range. The camera then swings toward sitting opposite the target and backs off to fit both hulls, with the look point biased 34% toward the enemy so your own ship never leaves frame. Corner brackets on the target, so "locked" is something you see on the enemy rather than a word in a corner.
  - **The cost is real and was accepted knowingly.** Holding a target in view means the view must turn when they are off the bow, and in a drag-to-steer game turning the view re-maps the helm. That is why it is **opt-in and instantly releasable with the same key** — the player decides when the trade pays. Measured helm swing: 1° with the target ahead, 82° abeam, **106° dead astern**. The position swing is clamped at 78° but the look-point bias adds to it; bounding the total would push an astern target back off screen, which is the thing this exists to prevent. `lockMaxSwingDeg` and `lockBias` are the dials if it reads as too much.
  - **Verified at four bearings**: ship and target both on screen in every case (ship low-left, target upper-centre).
  - **Space is now contextual**: with an enemy in lock range it takes the lock, otherwise it runs the anchor command. `CombatLock.WantsSpace` is computed rather than stored, so it never depends on which component ran first that frame.
  - **Probe lesson, again:** the first run showed the target off screen in three of four cases and looked like a camera bug. It was not — `CombatLock` reasserts `chase.LockTarget = null` every frame when nothing is locked, silently overwriting what the probe set. Same trap as HelmInput and MutinyController. **Drive the component, never the state it owns.**
- 2026-08-19 — **Combat camera: widen, never rotate.**
  - *Note 2026-10-03: framing rule still holds; steering no longer depends on the camera (see "Sailing controls = DREDGE").*
  - **The lean-toward-the-broadside camera was a mistake, and a specific one.** SeaSick steers by dragging on screen, which makes the camera *the control frame*: the player's sense of "left" is defined by where it points. Every degree of combat yaw re-maps the thumb mid-fight, exactly when a precise heading is needed to keep a side bearing. It was not visual noise — it was rotating the controls while they were in use. Triggering it **per shot** compounded it: a rolling engagement became a continuous series of transitions.
  - **How ship games actually solve this.** The common patterns are (1) pull back and rise so both beams are visible, ship stays centred; (2) frame both combatants; (3) a *player-initiated* aim mode that gives up the steering reference only when the player chooses (World of Warships) — a bad fit here, since one thumb in portrait has no spare input and cannot afford to lose the helm; (4) don't move the camera at all, and make the shot readable instead (tracers, persistent splashes). The through-line: **heading stays stable on screen, because steering is the primary skill.** Aiming is served by information, not camera motion.
  - **Chosen: widen only.** While engaged, the camera goes 12m further astern, 5.5m higher, and looks 5m less far ahead — no yaw term anywhere, so the mapping between drag and rudder never changes. Tilt lands at ~24.8°, deliberately short of the 30° where the horizon leaves frame. Driven by **being engaged** (a target within 1.25× gun range), not by firing, so a fight is one transition rather than one per volley; a broadside merely refreshes the hold so the framing outlasts the shot's flight.
  - **Tracers** on every round carry the readability the camera used to: a thin smoke trail you follow down to the water. This is what makes "don't move the camera" survivable — the information moved instead of the view.
- 2026-08-19 — **Guns you can see, a hull that answers them, and shots that break something.**
  - **Raiders carry visible guns.** Four of them, two a side, built from the *same* `Cannon` component the player uses — mesh, recoil and muzzle smoke all come free, and only the ballistics differ, since a raider lays its guns by pointing the whole ship (`Cannon.RecoilOnly()` works them for the look while `EnemyShip` does the aiming). They fire from the actual muzzles now, so a broadside comes off the rail rather than out of the hull. **Scaled 1.7×** and given lighter iron: the player reads their own guns from the deck, but a raider is read at 46m and up, where a correctly-proportioned cannon is three dark pixels. Legibility at engagement range beats accuracy of proportion. Guns are built *after* the red tint is captured so they keep their own timber and iron instead of vanishing into a red hull.
  - **The hull answers the broadside.** `ShipMotor.AddRecoilRoll` is a **damped spring** (stiffness 34, damping 3.4 → ~1.1s period, underdamped), not a lerp back to level — because a boat does not return to upright, it rolls past and comes back once or twice, and that second little roll *is* the feel. ~14°/s of kick per gun, so a two-gun broadside peaks around 5° of heel. Firing to starboard heels her to port, which is where the reaction actually throws her. Added **after** the swell clamp so a broadside is felt even when the sea already has the hull hard over, with the total clamped to ±26°. Raiders get the same treatment, so you can see them roll when they fire at you.
  - **Hits break something.** `Impact.Burst` on every shot that bites: an additive flash that collapses inward, a hard scatter of sparks, lingering smoke, and a brief point light that *fades* rather than vanishing (a light that simply switches off pops). Short on purpose — long slow explosions read as fireworks; this should read as something breaking.
- 2026-08-19 — **Raiders shoot back: combat now spends the voyage clock.**
  - **Why this and not more content.** Combat was the only system that could not cost anything, so fighting was never a decision. Arming the raiders required one new thing — incoming fire — and lit up four systems that already existed: hull damage → **−40% top speed and added wallow** → rougher ride → `SmoothnessMeter` → **sickness rises faster**; and each impact → `CrewAgent.Jolt` → **sickness and anger spike** → mutiny risk. A fight now spends the voyage rather than running beside it, which is pillar #2 finally bearing on combat.
  - **`PlayerHull`** makes the player a normal `IHittable`, so being shot at uses the exact same path as shooting — one hit test, one registry, no special case. `HullIntegrity.TakeShot` costs ~8.5% hull a hit (**~12 hits to wreck**) and jolts every crew member. Deliberately slow: the timber is not what ends a voyage, the wallow and the terrified crew are, and those compound long before the hull runs out. **`Alive` is always true** — failure stays soft, exactly as §3 says: a wrecked ship is crippled, never destroyed, and the crew turning for home is the real fail state.
  - **Raiders circle instead of charging.** A bow carries no guns, so inside `engageRange` a raider stops steering at the player and starts orbiting them at ~46m — it has to give you its side to shoot you, which is the same bargain you are making. That one change is what turns a chase into an engagement.
  - **Partial lead (0.8) plus 3.4° spread** is the whole difficulty dial. Hold a straight course and they hit you; change course and their shot goes where you were going to be. The dodge costs nothing to implement because the player is already the one deciding the heading — and it rewards the sailing, not a dodge button.
  - **Now that the player is a target, every lookup has to say who is asking.** `HitTargets.Nearest`/`SweepFirst` take an `ignore`, shots carry their firer, and the traverse, aim assist and target HUD all pass themselves — otherwise the player's guns train on their own hull. Raider-on-raider fire is allowed and left in: it makes their positioning matter.
  - **Verified end to end**, ship parked in a guard zone for 30s: hull 1.000 → 0.915 (exactly one shot), `wasShot` true, crew sickness 0.21 → 1.59 across five crew. **Engagement was sparse** — one hit in 30s, because `Block` holds a standoff that is often outside the 60m gun range and only `Chase` closes. If fights feel too quiet, raise `chaseRange` (130) before touching anything else.
- 2026-08-19 — **Hit volumes are capsules, hulls are solid, and the camera leans on a broadside.**
  - **A ship is not a ball.** Targets were a single sphere, so a 21m raider wrapped in a 5.5m sphere let shots at the bow or stern pass clean through — it read as having no hit box at all. `IHittable` now also reports `HitAxis` (half the body length, in world space) and `HitTargets.SweepFirst` tests **segment-vs-capsule** when it is non-zero, falling back to the sphere when it is not. Raiders are a 4.2m-radius capsule ~16m long; the monster is 4.2m by ~9m. Closest-approach between the two segments rather than a swept-capsule quadratic: far cheaper, and near enough to the entry point for a ball crossing a hull.
  - **Hulls are solid to each other.** Nothing did ship-vs-ship collision, so the player sailed straight through raiders and raiders slowly converged into each other on a shared patrol circle. `HullIntegrity` now treats raiders as obstacles — **ramming costs hull and stops you dead**, at 0.55× a reef's bite because timber gives where rock does not — and `EnemyShip.KeepClear` shoves off the player and off other raiders. Separation (7.5m) is deliberately less than a ship's length so two vessels can still lie alongside for a broadside; the point is to stop them merging, not to fend them off the moment it gets interesting.
  - **The camera leans to watch the fall of shot.** A broadside pushes the chase camera's **look point** out along the firing side (34m) and lifts it slightly (5.5m), held for ~1.7s — about one time of flight — then drifts back. Crucially it moves the look point, never the anchor, and **never retargets onto a shell**: cutting to the shot and back is exactly what would make this unwatchable. The ship stays in frame throughout and the horizon never rolls. Eased in at 3.2/s and out at 1.1/s, so it arrives with the shot and forgets about it gently; firing again extends the hold rather than restarting the lean, so a rolling engagement leans once instead of pumping on every volley.
- 2026-08-19 — **Raider polish: solid shores, the player's hull in red, and space as the dock key.**
  - **Raiders were clipping through islands.** Steering could never fix this on its own: a raider makes 17 m/s and turns at 26°/s, so its turning circle is ~38m while an island can be 200m across — it physically cannot always turn in time. Three changes: the lookahead now **scales with speed** (a fixed 70m is fine at steerage way and far too short at full speed), patrol clearance 55 → 78m, and — the actual fix — **`KeepClear()` makes the shoreline solid**, shoving the hull back out to `RadiusToward + margin` and bleeding 45% of its way, exactly as `HullIntegrity` already does for the player. Avoidance is now the nicety and the shove is the guarantee.
  - **Raiders wear the player's ship, tinted red.** `BuildFromPlayer()` clones the live `PlayerShip`'s three visual children (`Hull`, `MastPivot`, `RudderPivot`) — crew are separate children and are deliberately left behind — strips every MonoBehaviour and collider off the copy, and tints via **`MaterialPropertyBlock` rather than material instances**, so raiders still batch with the player's hull. Cloned from the scene rather than a prefab so there is no second asset to keep in sync: a raider is always whatever the ship currently is. Built on the first `Update`, not in `Awake`, because they spawn from `ArchipelagoGenerator.Awake` and script order is not guaranteed. The primitive hull survives as a fallback.
  - **Space is the docking key.** It runs whatever the current state's primary button runs — land here → cast off → recall crew — and nothing when that button is absent or disabled. Labels now say `(space)`, since an undiscoverable shortcut is not a shortcut. Deliberately inert during `landingPending`, when the ship is still coming alongside and the button is a status line.
- 2026-08-19 — **Raiders: enemy ships that guard islands.**
  - **They sail, they don't drive.** `EnemyShip` takes its speed off the same polar the player does (`ShipMotor.SailPolar`, `NoGoDegrees`, `BestUpwindAngle`), so a raider cannot point straight upwind either — it beats, and it slows in irons exactly as you do. This matters more than any chase logic: it means a **wind advantage is a real advantage**, and running downwind is a genuine escape rather than an animation. Combat inherits the sailing pillar instead of sitting beside it.
  - **Pathfinding is steering, not a graph.** The sea is open water with a handful of round obstacles that already publish their own radii (`Island.RadiusToward`, `Reef.Radius`), so raiders use a lookahead plus a lateral shove along the tangent. Cheaper than a grid and more ship-like: a raider bears away from a shoal rather than following waypoints around it, and it never stops to think — a ship that halts reads as broken.
  - **Duties: Patrol → Block → Chase → Return.** Patrol steers to a point *further round* the orbit circle than the raider currently is, which gives a smooth sweep instead of the in-out weave you get from chasing the nearest point on the circle. **Block is the interesting one** — it puts the hull between the island and the intruder rather than charging, because that is what guarding actually means. Chase leads the player's velocity. Stand-down range (340m) is deliberately longer than alert range (260m) so a raider doesn't dither while the player circles at the edge.
  - **Only islands worth taking get guarded** — home and bare shelter rocks are skipped, since posting a guard on empty rock would just be a toll on safe harbour. [superseded 2026-09-22: no home island — see the innermost raider-free ring, `WorldSettings.raiderFreeRing`] Patrol direction alternates so two raiders on one island sweep opposite ways.
  - **They cost almost nothing to add** because `IHittable`/`HitTargets` was already the seam: the player's guns hit them, the aim assist and traverse already target them, and they take damage, flash, splinter and sink with no changes to the gunnery at all. `IHittable` gained `Health01`/`HitPoints`/`DamageTaken` so `TargetHUD` draws any target rather than only monsters, and raiders show their current duty above the health bar — knowing a guard has switched to coming for you is worth more than its hit points.
  - Minimap: raiders are red blips with a faint ring showing the water each one patrols; the patrol is the information, not the ship's momentary position.
  - **Unarmed on purpose.** They intercept and block but carry no guns yet, so there is still no fight — only a blockade. Arming them means the player needs damage coming back (hull, crew panic, sinking), which is the next increment and a bigger one than it looks.
- 2026-08-19 — **Why hitting was hard: the guns were riding the roll.**
  - **The cause, measured directly.** The guns are rigid children of the hull and point over the beam, so roll maps 1:1 into elevation. Under sail the barrel swung between **1.0° and 15.6°** (hull roll −6.6°…+7.9°), which lands the shot anywhere from **29.5m to 102.3m**. Against a target 8.4m wide that is not difficulty, it is a lottery — and nothing on screen tells the player where the hull is in its roll cycle. This measurement needs no hit-rate test and is the one solid finding of the session.
  - **Roll stabilisation** (`Cannon.rollStabilisation`, default 0.75): the barrel is laid against the horizon rather than the deck. Diegetically this is just what a gun crew does. Held below 1 on purpose so a heavy sea still costs accuracy as a *gradient*, matching how wind and sickness already work. Verified directly: elevation at the moment of firing goes from a 1.0–15.6° spread to sitting on the nominal 9°.
  - **Gun traverse** (`maxTraverseDeg` 18°, `trainWithinDeg` 60°): the crew train the guns onto the nearest beast, widening "the side bears" from a ~10° knife-edge into a zone. The ball still flies exactly where the barrel points — no projectile is bent. **Traverse must rotate about world up, not the gun's own up**: its own up is the mast, which leans with the roll, so swinging the gun sideways around a leaning axis also swings it in elevation. Measured at −10.4° before the fix, i.e. broadsides fired into the sea.
  - **Aim assist** (`aimAssistWindow` 8m, `aimAssistCap` 2.5m): the muzzle velocity is nudged once, at firing, toward the launch angle that passes through the target; the shot stays a clean parabola. The correction is capped in **metres at the target's range**, so a long shot bends no further than a short one. First version measured the miss where the ball reached the *water*, which is wrong twice over — the beast sits above the waterline and the shot passes through it on the way down — so a good shot read as a 20m miss and the assist never engaged at all.
  - **`velocityInheritance` still 0.** Unchanged and still undecided.
  - **Hit-rate A/B testing did not work and its results are discarded.** Identical baseline code scored 83%, 50% and 17% across runs. Sea state varies over a session (`WaveField`/`SwellDirector`), so separate play sessions are not comparable, and several runs landed in near-calm water where the barrel sat at a constant 9° and stabilisation had nothing to fix. **Any future gunnery A/B must pin sea state**, not just the ship. Harness lessons that cost real time: editing anything under `Assets/` mid-run triggers a domain reload that silently kills the running coroutine; an "anchored" ship still makes ~11 m/s and is 30m from where it was put within 3 seconds; and guns slew at 55°/s, so firing immediately after repositioning catches them mid-swing.
- 2026-08-19 — **Something to shoot at: static sea monsters, and the gunnery proved out.**
